<meta>This page begins the formal text of the work, following the bibliographic identification on the previous leaf. It contains the opening of Giovanni Boccaccio’s preface, dedicated to Queen Joanna I of Naples.</meta>

<note>A large, decorative initial letter **P** is illuminated in shades of green and pink. This "P" begins the word *Pridie* (Recently).</note> Recently, while I was separated a little from the common crowd and almost free from other cares, I wrote a small book in high praise of the female sex and for the comfort of my friends, rather than for the great benefit of the state. 

But while I was turning it over in my mind—to whom I should first send it so that it would not wither in my possession through idleness, and so that, supported by the favor of another, it might venture more safely into the public eye—I realized it should not be sent to a male prince. 

Instead, since it speaks about women, it ought to be destined for some distinguished woman. As I searched for one more worthy than all others, there came to mind that radiant Italian star <term>iubar</term> <note>original: "iubar." This term literally means a beam of light or radiance; Boccaccio uses it here to describe the Queen's brilliant reputation.</note> and the singular glory not only of women but also of kings: Joanna, the most serene Queen of Jerusalem and Sicily. <note>Joanna I of Naples (1325–1382) was a powerful and controversial monarch. Boccaccio’s dedication highlights her status as a "ruling woman" to justify his subject matter.</note> Considering both the brilliance of her illustrious lineage and ancestors, and the new praises she has sought for herself with a brave heart, I was seized by the desire to send it to her...

<summary>This page contains the opening of the dedication for Boccaccio's "On Famous Women," where the author explains his decision to dedicate the work to Queen Joanna I of Naples.</summary>
<keywords>Giovanni Boccaccio, Preface, Dedication, Queen Joanna I, Naples, Sicily, Jerusalem, Women’s History</keywords>

--- Page 4 ---
<meta>The previous page introduced a dedication to Queen Joanna I. However, this page concludes a dedication addressed instead to Andrea Acciaioli, Countess of Altavilla. Boccaccio revised this work several times, and different manuscript traditions reflect different dedicatees. We now transition from the end of this introductory letter into the formal start of the "On Famous Women" biographies.</meta>

...Indeed, supported by your favor, you will be flattered in your leisure; you will delight in feminine virtue and the pleasantness of these histories. Nor do I think this reading will stir your mind in vain, if you, emulating the deeds of these outstanding women, rouse your noble spirit to better things. And though you might occasionally find wanton passages mixed with the sacred—which the nature of storytelling sometimes requires—do not overlook the honesty with which you will be filled. Like a garden <term>viridarium</term> <note>original: "viridarium." A pleasure garden or grove, often used as a metaphor for a collection of literature.</note> of the mind, extend your ivory hands to the flowers, setting aside the sharp thorns. Gather the praiseworthy things, and set aside the obscenities. 

Whenever you read something worthy of a woman who has professed the Christian religion in a pagan woman <note>original: "gentali muliere." Boccaccio is encouraging his Christian reader to learn virtue even from non-Christian ("gentile" or pagan) historical figures.</note>, and you feel you do not possess it, let a blush of the mind excite you and rebuke yourself. You, who are anointed with the chrism of Christ, should surpass the ancients in honesty, modesty, and virtue. With your natural wit provoked to strength, do not merely allow yourself to be surpassed; if you surpass everyone in virtue, as you are already conspicuous for your youthful body and flowery beauty, you will become more outstanding than others—not only those of your own time, but also the ancients—in the integrity of your soul. 

Remember that beauty <term>formositas</term> <note>original: "formositas." Boccaccio uses this to refer specifically to physical beauty, which he argues must be secondary to moral character.</note> is not something to be decorated with pigments as many women do, but is to be adorned with honesty, holiness, and good works. Thus, while you give thanks to Him who gave you grace, you will not only be one of the shining minds in this mortal state, but when you put off the human form, you will be received into eternal love by the Giver of Graces. Furthermore, if you deem it worthy, most excellent of women, grant this book the boldness to go forth into the world. It will go, I believe, safe from the insults of the malicious under your protection, and it will carry your name as one splendid name among the other illustrious women. Since your presence cannot be offered everywhere, it will make your merits known to those present and preserve them for eternal posterity. Farewell.

-># The Book of Giovanni Boccaccio of Certaldo on Famous Women, Dedicated to Andrea Acciaioli of Florence, Countess of Altavilla, Begins Happily.<-

Some time ago, several of the ancients wrote books in compendium form about famous men. In our own age, that distinguished man and outstanding poet, Francis Petrarch <note>Petrarch (1304–1374) was Boccaccio's mentor and a central figure of the early Renaissance. His work "On Illustrious Men" served as the direct inspiration for this project.</note>, our teacher, is writing on the same subject at length and with a sharper style, and worthily so. For indeed, those who surpassed others in noble deeds and used every effort, blood, and spirit to seize the opportunity for glory, have certainly earned the right to have their names brought down to perpetual memory for posterity. 

Truly, I have marveled greatly that women have had so little influence among these men of letters that they have attained no special description in the grace of memory, even though it is clear from wider histories that some women have acted quite vigorously. And if men are to be extolled when they have performed great deeds with the strength granted to them, how much more so women, for whom everything from nature...

<summary>Boccaccio concludes his dedication to Andrea Acciaioli by urging her to use these stories for moral improvement and then begins his general preface, noting that while many have written of famous men, women have been unfairly neglected in history.</summary>
<keywords>Andrea Acciaioli, moral virtue, beauty vs. honesty, Petrarch, historiography, women in history</keywords>

--- Page 5 ---
<meta>This page concludes the preface of Giovanni Boccaccio’s *De Mulieribus Claris* (On Famous Women). Boccaccio is here defining his terminology—specifically why he includes women who were "famous" for bad reasons as well as good ones—before transitioning into the biographies themselves.</meta>

...to take the name of **fame** <term>claritas</term> <note>original: "claritatis." While "claritas" can mean "clarity" or "brightness," in this context Boccaccio uses it to mean "fame" or "renown."</note> so strictly that it always seems to result in virtue. On the contrary, with the readers' kind permission, I intend to bend it toward a broader sense; and I will understand as **famous** those women whom I find to be most well-known to the world by common report, regardless of the nature of their deed. 

For I remember often reading—among illustrious men like Leonidas, the Scipios, the Catos, and the Fabricii <note>These are archetypes of Roman and Greek virtue: Leonidas of Sparta, Scipio Africanus, Cato the Elder, and Gaius Fabricius.</note>—about the most faithless Gracchi, the treacherous Hannibal, the betrayer Jugurtha, the bloodstained Sulla and Marius (steeped in the blood of civil war), and Crassus, who was as rich as he was greedy, and others of that kind. 

Truly, since praising things worthy of memory and sometimes suppressing wicked things with rebukes will not only drive the noble toward glory but might also pull back the cowardly slightly from their ill-fated paths with reins, I have sought to restore that beauty which seems to have been taken away from this little work by the shameful acts of certain individuals. Therefore, I have decided to occasionally insert into these histories some **charming allurements to virtue**, and to add **stings** to provoke the flight from and detestation of crimes. And here f<insert>inishes the preface.</insert>

<summary>Boccaccio defines "fame" broadly to include both virtuous and notorious women, arguing that highlighting both serves a moral purpose by providing examples to follow or avoid.</summary>
<keywords>fame, renown, virtue, vice, moral education, Scipio, Hannibal, historiography</keywords>

--- Page 6 ---
<meta>This page concludes the preface of Giovanni Boccaccio’s *De Mulieribus Claris* (On Famous Women). Boccaccio is concluding his justification for the work, contrasting the worldly fame of the women he will profile with the eternal glory of Christian saints, explaining why he focuses on the former in this specific secular collection.</meta>

...compelled them by human endurance. Imitating both the commands and the footsteps of the sacred <term>preceptors</term> <note>original: "praeceptorum." Boccaccio refers to the holy teachers or Church Fathers who provided moral guidance.</note>, where they—either by some gift or instinct of nature, or rather by the desire for this **transient brilliance** <note>original: "moritanei fulgoris." This refers to worldly fame, which Boccaccio views as flickering and mortal compared to the steady light of the divine.</note>—endured at times the most grievous trials, whether by the impulse of Venus <note>Referring to the trials of love or lust.</note> or wandering Fortune, though not without a sharp strength of mind. 

Moreover, those true women, who shine even as the light of this world fails, live most brightly in **unconquered eternity**; this is due to their virginity, chastity, holiness, and virtue, and their invincible constancy in overcoming both the desires of the flesh and the tortures of tyrants. Because their merits demanded it, we know they have been described in individual volumes by men of old, renowned for their sacred writings and venerable majesty. 

However, since their merits <note>Referring to the famous women of antiquity who are the subject of this book.</note> have been demonstrated by no one else in a special volume of this kind, I shall describe them here as has already been stated.

<summary>Boccaccio concludes his preface by distinguishing between the worldly fame of the women in his book and the eternal glory of Christian saints, noting that while saints have their own sacred texts, he will provide the first dedicated volume for these famous historical figures.</summary>
<keywords>fame, virtue, eternity, Venus, Fortune, Christian saints, Church Fathers, moral strength</keywords>

--- Page 7 ---
<meta>This page transitions from Boccaccio’s general introduction into the specific biographies of famous women, beginning with the biblical figure of Eve (though she is not explicitly named in a heading) and moving into the historical-legendary figure of Semiramis.</meta>

Nevertheless, because women count beauty among their chief gifts, and have already attained much glory through the undiscriminating judgment of mortals because of it, it is not superfluous to include beauty among their distinctions—as if it were a **preeminent brightness** <note>original: "fulgor precipuus." Boccaccio often uses light metaphors to describe physical beauty and worldly fame.</note>—both here and in the accounts that follow. 

She <note>Referring to Eve, the first woman.</note> was made a citizen of Paradise by right of both her origin and her residence; she was clothed in a splendor unknown to us while she, together with her husband, eagerly enjoyed the delights of that place. The envious enemy of her happiness <note>The serpent or Satan.</note> instilled in her mind, through wicked persuasion, the idea that she could attain even greater glory by going against the single law imposed upon her by God. Because she gave credit to this enemy—with a **female levity** <note>original: "levitate feminea." This reflects the common medieval view that women were more easily swayed or less rational than men.</note> greater than was fitting for her or for us—and because she foolishly thought she would ascend to higher things, she first drew her flexible husband into her own opinion with a certain flattering suggestion. Then, acting against the law regarding the tree of the knowledge of good and evil, they tasted the fruit with rash daring. By this act, they thrust themselves and all their future descendants out from rest and eternity into anxious labors and a miserable death; they were cast out from a delightful homeland into thorns, clods of earth, and rocks. 

For when the flashing light in which they walked departed, and they were rebuked by their blessed Creator and stripped of all their finery, they were driven out as exiles from that place of delights into the fields of **Hebron** <note>A city in the Judean mountains; in medieval tradition, it was often cited as the place where Adam and Eve lived after the Fall.</note>. There, this remarkable woman—famous for these deeds—became the first (as some believe) to assist her husband as he turned the earth with hoes, while she took up the **distaff** <note>A tool used in spinning wool or flax. In art and literature, the distaff is a primary symbol of domestic womanhood.</note> to spin. She frequently experienced the pains of childbirth, and her soul was equally squeezed by the narrow anxieties of the deaths of her sons and grandsons. Suffering miserably from cold and heat, and other inconveniences, she finally reached old age and death, exhausted by her labors.

->**Concerning Semiramis, Queen of the Assyrians**<-

Semiramis was a distinguished and most ancient queen of the Assyrians. However, the vastness of time has obscured the parents from whom she drew her lineage, except for a fable that pleases the ancients, which claims she was the daughter of **Neptune** <note>The Roman god of the sea.</note>—whom they erroneously believed to be the son of Saturn and the god of the sea. Although this should not be believed, it is nonetheless evidence that she was born of noble parents. She was married to **Ninus** <note>The mythical founder of Nineveh and the Assyrian Empire.</note>, the illustrious king of the Assyrians, and bore him an only son, also named Ninus. 

Indeed, after Ninus had subdued all of Asia and finally the **Bactrians** <note>original: "Bacheris" (Bactris). An ancient Iranian people located in modern-day Afghanistan and Uzbekistan.</note>, he died from the strike of an arrow. Since she was still a young woman and her son was a mere boy, she judged it unsafe to commit the reins of such a grand Eastern empire to such a tender age. She possessed such a great spirit that what a fierce man [would do] with arms...

<summary>Boccaccio describes the Fall of Eve, emphasizing her "female levity" and the subsequent life of labor, before beginning the biography of the Assyrian Queen Semiramis.</summary>

<meta>The previous page concluded the introduction of Eve, the first woman, and her fall from Paradise. This page transitions into the biography of Semiramis, the legendary Queen of Assyria, focusing on her rise to power following the death of her husband, King Ninus.</meta>

...the nations he had subdued and restrained by force, this woman dared to take up these massive responsibilities through skill and wit. For, having devised a deception with a certain **womanly cunning** <note>original: "astu quodam muliebri." In the context of 14th-century literature, "cunning" often referred to a combination of intelligence and strategic deception, which Boccaccio frequently attributes to his female subjects.</note>, she first of all deceived the armies regarding the death of her great husband. It was not surprising, as Semiramis bore a very close resemblance to her son in her facial features. Because of this, her cheeks on both sides were not yet different from those of a boy due to her age; her voice was feminine, and in the stature of her body, she—being only slightly larger—differed very little from her offspring. 

Aided by these similarities, and lest she be exposed by some slip-up as time went on, she covered her head with a **tiara** <term>A high, cylindrical headdress or turban, often decorated with jewels, worn by ancient Near Eastern monarchs as a symbol of authority.</term> and concealed her arms and legs with long robes. Furthermore, since such clothing had been unheard of among the Assyrians until then, she ensured that the novelty of the outfit would not provoke suspicion among the local people by decreeing that the entire population should wear the same style of dress. 

In this way, now pretending to be the wife of the late king and now the son, a woman feigning to be a boy, she attained royal majesty with wonderful diligence and maintained military discipline. By concealing her true sex, she achieved great things and performed works that would have been outstanding even for the most robust men. And while n...

In this context, it refers to the rebellions and political chaos that followed the death of the King.</note> until she had forced that most powerful city—previously untouched by any siege—into surrender, bringing it back under her dominion with **hostile arms** <note>original: "infestis... armis."</note>. 

A massive statue, cast in bronze and erected in Babylon, stood for a long time as a testament to this **bold deed** <note>original: "animosi facinoris." The term suggests not just courage, but a spirited, almost aggressive determination.</note>. It depicted the woman with her hair flowing loose on one side and braided on the other <note>This refers to a famous legend: while Semiramis was having her hair styled, news reached her of a revolt in Babylon. She supposedly rushed to the battlefield immediately, refusing to finish braiding her hair until the city was reconquered.</note>. 

Furthermore, she founded many new cities and performed great deeds which antiquity has so swallowed up that almost nothing survives to our time except the praises already mentioned. Indeed, all these achievements would be considered wonderful, praiseworthy, and worthy of perpetual memory not only in a woman but in any **strenuous man** <term>In Latin, *vir strenuus* refers to a man of action, vigor, and military prowess—the Roman ideal of masculine virtue.</term>. However, one **obscene enticement** <note>original: "obscena... illecebra." This marks a thematic shift in the biography from her public triumphs to her private moral failings, specifically her legendary reputation for unbridled lust.</note> defiled them all. For when among...

## Concerning Ops, the wife of Saturn.

Ops—or Rhea, if we believe the ancients—shone with great renown amidst both prosperity and adversity. For she was the daughter of Uranus—a most powerful man among the still-primitive Greeks—and his wife Vesta. <note>In classical mythology, Vesta is often identified here as Gaia or Mother Earth.</note> As the sister and wife of King Saturn, she performed no other notable deed that has reached our knowledge, except that she distinguished herself through womanly cunning. She saved her sons—Jupiter, Neptune, and Pluto—from death by negotiating a pact with Titan <note>original: "Tutone," a variant of Titan</note>, her husband’s brother. 

Because of her reputation for justice, the ignorance of the people of that era allowed her to rise to the fame of a supreme deity. She did not only gain honor through her physical likeness; rather, through the great error of mortals, she was regarded as a distinguished goddess and the "Mother of the Gods." Temples, priests, and sacred rites were established for her by public decree. To such an extent did this enormous evil <note>The author likely refers to the "evil" of pagan idolatry and the deification of mortals.</note> grow strong...

<keywords>Ops, Saturn, Jupiter, Neptune, Pluto, Titan, Rhea, Mythology, Mother of the Gods, Vesta, Semiramis</keywords>

--- Page 11 ---
<meta>This page continues the genealogy of the children of Ops and Saturn, specifically focusing on the early life and deification of Juno (Hera), following the mention of her brothers Jupiter, Neptune, and Pluto on the previous page.</meta>

...born from the same birth of the gods <note>The author refers to Juno being born as the sibling of Jupiter to their mother, Ops.</note> and from her infancy she was sent across to Samos. There, she was diligently raised until she reached puberty, and finally, she was married to her brother, Jupiter. This was believed for many centuries, as evidenced by a statue in the temple at Samos. 

For the Samians, thinking it would bring no small glory to themselves and their descendants that Juno was raised and betrothed among them—since they considered her a queen and a goddess—built a huge temple, marvelous beyond all others in the world, and dedicated it to their deity. They caused her image to be sculpted from marble in the likeness of a virgin bride <note>original: "in habitu nubentis virginis"</note> and placed it within their temple. 

This woman, having finally married a great king, and as his empire grew day by day with fame carrying his name far and wide, attained no small amount of splendor herself. Indeed, after some time, through poetic fictions and the infamous generosity of the ancients <note>A common medieval and Renaissance trope suggests that "generous" ancient writers turned historical humans into gods through myth-making.</note>, she was made the Queen of Heaven.

This page shifts the focus to Ceres, discussing how two historical figures were combined into one goddess of agriculture, and defends the civilizing influence of her discoveries.</meta>

Providing the occasion for many fables to this matter. There was, moreover, another Ceres <note>The Roman goddess of agriculture, equivalent to the Greek Demeter.</note> at Eleusis, a city of the Attic region <note>Eleusis was the site of the Eleusinian Mysteries, the most famous religious rites of ancient Greece.</note>, who was famous among her own people for the same merits. They claim that Triptolemus <note>A mythical prince of Eleusis who was taught the arts of agriculture by Ceres and sent to spread the knowledge across the world.</note> was obedient to her. Because antiquity raised both of these women to divinity and honors equally, it seemed sufficient to record the talents of both under just one name. 

By Pollux! <note>original: "edepol"; an ancient Roman oath expressing emphasis.</note> I know not whether to praise or curse the inventions of these women. For who would condemn the gathering of those who once wandered in the forests? Who would condemn men being led out from the groves and into the city? Who would condemn those living in the manner of wild beasts being called forth to a better harvest? Who would condemn acorns <note>In ancient lore, humans were said to have survived on acorns before the "invention" of grain.</note> being changed into crops, by which limbs grow more vigorous and clear, and which provide nourishment more suited for human use? Who would condemn a world—once obstructed by decay, briars, and disordered thickets—being turned toward cultivation, beauty, and public utility? Who would condemn a rude age turned into a civilized one? Who would condemn minds stirred from idleness into contemplation? Who would condemn torpid strengths formerly hidden in caves being drawn into urban or rustic exercise, by which so many cities were enlarged...

--- Page 13 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the consequences of neglecting agriculture before transitioning to the life and inventions of the goddess Minerva, following the previous section on Ceres.</meta>

...the cultivated fields respond with wrath. Immediately, a scarcity of provisions enters, and fasts harsher than those of ancient times arise. A savage hunger, never known in the forests, stalks through the huts <note>original: "gorgusciolos"; likely referring to the small, cramped dwellings of the poor.</note> of the impoverished, and not without frequent danger to the wealthy themselves. From this comes a foul and wild emaciation, a deathly paleness, and a weakness that moves with a staggering gait; thus, multiple causes of disease and premature death arise. Having looked upon these things along with many others, I hardly know what to say—except that I know first of all that those ancient times, though they were rough and rustic, are to be preferred to these iron ages of ours and all others.

### About Minerva.

Minerva, who is also called Pallas, was a virgin of such conspicuous brilliance that foolish men thought she did not have a mortal origin. Some say she was first seen and recognized on earth at Lake Tritonis, not far from the Lesser Syrtis <note>A gulf on the northern coast of Africa, near modern-day Tunisia.</note>, during the time of King Ogyges. And because over the course of time they saw her doing many things never seen before, it was believed—not only among the unrefined Africans but even among the Greeks, who then surpassed all others in wisdom—that she was born from the head of Jove <note>Jupiter/Zeus</note> without a mother and had fallen from heaven. More faith was given to this ridiculous error the more hidden her true origin was. They wished her, before all others, to have flourished in perpetual virginity; and so that this might be believed with even fuller faith, they fashioned a story that Vulcan, the god of fire, struggled with her for a long time in a fever of carnal lust but was overcome. Moreover, they maintain that before her, the art of wool-working <term>lanificium: the entire process of cleaning, carding, spinning, and weaving wool</term> was entirely unknown to everyone.

For once she had shown in what order wool, cleansed of its impurities and softened by iron teeth <note>A reference to the process of "carding" wool to align the fibers.</note>, should be placed upon the distaff <term>distaff: a tool used in spinning to hold the unspun fibers</term> and finally drawn out into thread by the fingers, she then devised the craft of weaving. She taught them how the threads should be intertwined with one another, joined by the strike of the comb, and pressed firm into a fabric by the heel. In praise of this craftsmanship, that famous contest between her and Arachne of Colophon <note>In mythology, Arachne was a talented weaver who challenged Minerva and was eventually transformed into a spider.</note> is recounted. Furthermore, she discovered the use of oil, which was previously unheard of by mortals, and taught them to grind the berry-bearing fruits <note>Olives</note> in a mill and extract their essence.

Because this seemed to bring so much utility, victory was attributed to her over Neptune in the naming of Athens <note>According to myth, Minerva won the patronage of the city by gifting the olive tree, while Neptune offered a salt spring or a horse.</note>. They also wish it to be her work that she was the first to invent the four-horse chariot <term>quadriga: a chariot drawn by four horses abreast, often used in racing and triumphs</term> and to turn iron into weapons. She taught men to cover the body with armor, to arrange the battle line of combatants, and all the laws by which one goes into battle. They say, moreover, that she discovered numbers and arranged them into the order which we use to this day. Furthermore, from the bone of some...

<meta>This page continues the biography of the goddess Minerva (Pallas), specifically detailing her invention of musical instruments, the symbolic meaning of her iconography (her helmet, shield, and owl), and her eventual elevation to the Roman pantheon before beginning the entry for Venus.</meta>

...fields, or rather from marshy reeds, they believed she first produced flutes or pastoral pipes <note>original: "fistulas"; the pan-pipes or syrinx.</note> and threw them down to earth from heaven because the act of blowing them caused the throat to swell and the face to become deformed. Why say more? Because of the discovery of so many miracles, antiquity—the bestower of divinity—attributed the name of "Wisdom" to her. Drawn by this view, the Athenians were named after her; and because that city seemed suited for the studies through which everyone becomes prudent and wise, they took her under their protection and dedicated the art of learning to her. They constructed a huge temple consecrated to her divinity, and in that same place they depicted her with "curved eyes" <note>original: "oculis curuā"; likely a reference to a sidelong or subtle gaze, interpreted here as the far-reaching and non-obvious nature of a wise person's plans.</note> because it is rarely known toward what end the intent of a wise person tends.

She was shown wearing a helmet, wishing by this to signify that the counsels of the wise are covered and armed. She was dressed in a breastplate <note>original: "lorica"; a piece of body armor.</note> because the wise person is always armed against whatever blows of fortune may strike. She was fortified with a very long spear to show that the wisdom of a person strikes even at a distant target. Furthermore, she was protected by a crystalline aegis <note>The Aegis was the protective shield or buckler associated with Zeus and Athena.</note> with the head of the Gorgon <note>Medusa, whose gaze turned men to stone.</note> fixed upon it, suggesting by this that to the wise, all protective coverings are transparent and clear; they are always so fortified with serpentine cunning that the ignorant appear to be turned to stone <note>original: "larei"; likely a corruption or variant of "lapidei," meaning "stony" or "petrified."</note> by their very gaze. In her protection they placed an owl, affirming that just as in the light, so too in the darkness must the prudent be able to see. 

Finally, the fame of this woman and the reverence for her divinity spread so far and wide—and circumstances so favored her—that temples were constructed in her honor and sacred rites were celebrated almost throughout the entire world. She ascended so high that in the Capitol <note>The Temple of Jupiter Optimus Maximus on the Capitoline Hill in Rome.</note>, next to Jove <note>Jupiter</note> the Best and Greatest, a shrine was dedicated to her, and she held a place among the most powerful of the Roman gods along with Queen Juno. There are, however, some very serious men who assert that there was never just one Minerva who discovered all the many things that have been mentioned. I willingly agree with this, so that the number of famous women might be even greater.

### About Venus, Queen of the Cypriots.

It is the opinion of some that the Cyprian Venus was once a mortal woman. Regarding her parents, some authorities are uncertain. For some wish her to be the daughter of a certain Cyprus and Syria; others, indeed, the daughter of Cyprus and Dione, a Cypriot woman. Some, to extol the brilliance of her beauty, assert she was born of Jove and the aforementioned Dione. Truly, from whomever she may have been born, I have decided she should be described among famous women rather for her illustrious beauty than for any shameful invention. She shone with such facial beauty and grace of the whole body that the belief of those looking upon her was often deceived. For some...

<meta>This page transitions from the description of Minerva’s wisdom and iconography to the life of Venus. Following the common medieval and early modern practice of Euhemerism—the belief that gods were actually historical figures who were later deified—the author treats Venus as a mortal woman known for her beauty and her introduction of organized prostitution.</meta>

...they said she was the very star of heaven which we call **Venus**. Others said she was a celestial woman who fell to earth from the lap of **Jupiter**. Briefly, everyone, obscured by a blind darkness, although they knew she was born from a mortal woman, asserted she was an immortal goddess. And they affirmed with all their might that she was the mother of that ill-fated love which they called **Cupid**. Nor did she lack the arts of capturing the minds of foolish onlookers with various gestures. By these actions, it happened through their deserts that, since they were unable to resist many obscenities (not all of which I am about to write down immediately), she was held to be the daughter of Jupiter and one of the most venerable goddesses.

Nor was she appeased by incense alone at **Paphos**, the most ancient town of the Cypriots; for they thought that this dead and incestuous woman delighted in that fragrance, she who while living delighted in the filth and pleasures of brothels. But also among other nations and the Romans, who once built a temple to her under the title of *Venus Genitrix* <note>original: "veneris genitricis"; Venus the Mother, the ancestral goddess of the Roman people via Aeneas.</note> and *Verticordia* <note>original: "verticordiē"; "The Changer of Hearts," a title used when invoking Venus to turn women's hearts toward chastity and virtue.</note> and other honors. 

But because many things are said, it was believed she married two men. It is not entirely certain to whom she first married; therefore, as it pleases some, she first married **Vulcan**, king of the Lemnians and son of the Cretan Jupiter. After he was removed, she married **Adonis**, son of Cinyras and Myrrha, the king of the Cypriots. This seems more likely to me than if we were to say Adonis was her first husband. Whether it was done by the fault of her own temperament, or the infection of the region in which lust seems most powerful, or the malice of a corrupt mind, after Adonis was already dead, she fell into such a great itch of luxury <note>original: "luxurie pruritum"; a historical idiom for intense sexual desire or "lust."</note> that she seemed to have stained all the clarity of her beauty with frequent fornications, not merely obscured but entirely spotted.

Since it was known to the adjacent regions that she was caught with a soldier while married to Vulcan, her first husband; from which the story of her adultery with **Mars** <note>Mars is the god of war, here referred to as an "armiger" or soldier.</note> was believed to have taken its place. Finally, however, so that she might seem to have wiped away a little of the redness from her shameless face <note>A metaphor for losing the ability to feel shame or blush.</note> and gained a wider license for her lust, she thought up an unspeakable baseness: she first, as they say, invented public prostitution and established brothels <note>original: "fornices"; literally "arches," referring to the vaulted underground chambers in Rome where prostitutes often worked.</note> and compelled matrons to enter them. This was testified by the execrable custom of the Cypriots which has been witnessed through many past centuries. They long observed the practice of sending their virgins to the shores so that they might engage in intercourse with strangers, and thus in the future...

# X. Concerning Isis, Queen and Goddess of the Egyptians

**Isis**, whose name was previously **Io**, was not only a most famous queen of the Egyptians, but eventually became their most holy and venerable deity. However, among illustrious writers of history, there is much doubt as to what times she lived in or from what parents she was born. 

There are some who say she was the daughter of **Inachus**, the first king of the **Argives** <note>Ancient Greeks from the city of Argos.</note>, and the sister of **Phoroneus**. It is well established that these men lived during the time of **Jacob**, the son of **Isaac** <note>The author is using "Sacred Chronology" to date these myths to approximately 1800–1700 BCE.</note>. Others assert she was the daughter of **Prometheus** <note>The Titan famous for creating mankind and stealing fire.</note>, born while **Phorbas** was reigning at Argos, which occurred long after that first period. 

Some say she lived during the times of **Cecrops**, the King of the Athenians, and others that she flourished in the times of the Argives. Because such variations among celebrated men are not lacking in evidence, it is certain that this woman was outstanding among the women of her age...

<summary>This page concludes the critique of Venus's moral influence on Italy and introduces Isis, identifying her with the Greek Io and attempting to date her life using both Greek king-lists and Biblical patriarchs.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the biography of Isis, focusing on her contributions to civilization and her eventual deification. It then begins the biography of Europa, applying a "Euhemerist" interpretation—where myths are explained as historical events—by describing her abduction not by a literal god in animal form, but by a Cretan king on a ship.</meta>

...to sow seeds in cultivated fields, and finally to convert the harvested fruits in their season into food. Furthermore, she taught those who were wandering and almost wild to gather together, and having given them laws, to live in a civil manner. Also—what is far more remarkable in a woman—by applying her intellect to its full power, she showed the characters of the letters <note>Isis was often credited with the invention of the Egyptian alphabet or hieroglyphs.</note> by which language might be joined together. 

These things (to say nothing of the rest) seemed so wonderful to unaccustomed men that they easily believed Isis had not come from Greece, but had fallen from heaven. And because of this, they established divine honors for her while she was still breathing <note>Meaning while she was still alive.</note>. Indeed, her divinity—the devil deceiving the ignorant—grew into such great and famous veneration after her death that at Rome, now the mistress of the world, a massive temple was built for her, and a solemn annual rite was established according to the Egyptian custom. Nor is there any doubt that this error <note>The author, writing from a Christian perspective, views the worship of Isis as a "pagan error" inspired by demons.</note> penetrated even to the Western and barbarian nations. 

Furthermore, the husband of this so-famous woman was **Apis**, whom erroneous antiquity believed to be the son of **Jupiter** and **Niobe**, the daughter of **Phoroneus**. They say that after yielding the kingdom of **Achaia** to his brother **Aegialeus**, and having reigned at **Argos** for thirty-five years, he withdrew into Egypt. There, he ruled together with Isis, and was held as a god, named **Osiris** or **Serapis**. Let it be that there are those who say Isis had a certain theologian as a husband, and by her begot **Epaphus**, who later ruled the Egyptians and was esteemed as the son of Jupiter by her.

-># XI. Concerning Europa, Queen of the Cretans<-

Some believe **Europa** was the daughter of **Phoenix**, but far more say she was the child of **Agenor**, King of the Phoenicians. She was of such wonderful beauty that **Jupiter**, the King of Crete <note>In this historical interpretation, "Jupiter" is not a god, but a mortal king of Crete who shared the name of the deity.</note>, was seized with love for her. When this powerful man was preparing a plot for her abduction, he wanted it done through the seductive words of a certain person, so that the playful maiden might follow her father's herds from the mountains to the Phoenician shore. 

And then, having been snatched away immediately, she was placed upon a ship which had a white bull as its figurehead <note>original: "albus thaurus erat insigne." The author explains the myth of Jupiter turning into a bull as a misunderstanding of a ship's decorative emblem.</note> and carried away to Crete. I judge it least commendable for maidens to wander with too much freedom and to offer easy ears to the words of anyone. For I have often read it happened that, while acting in this way, shameful marks were sometimes impressed upon their honor, which even the glory of perpetual chastity could not later wipe away.

<summary>This page concludes the life of Isis, crediting her with teaching agriculture and literacy to the Egyptians, and begins the story of Europa, interpreting her abduction as a historical kidnapping by a Cretan king.</summary>
<keywords>Isis, Apis, Osiris, invention of writing, agriculture, deification, Europa, Agenor, Jupiter of Crete, Euhemerism, abduction of Europa</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the biography of Europa, transitioning from the historical circumstances of her abduction to her lasting legacy as the namesake of the European continent. It then begins the biography of Libya, daughter of Epaphus.</meta>

From these events, it is clearly evident how the fable took its origin—the one that tells how **Mercury** drove the herds with him to the shore and **Jupiter**, having turned into a bull, carried away the maiden **Europa** to **Crete** while she was at leisure <note>The author is applying a "Euhemerist" interpretation, suggesting the myth of the bull was a poetic veil for a historical abduction by a king or a ship.</note>. However, the ancients disagree on the timing of this abduction. For those who place it in a more ancient age say it happened while **Danaus** was reigning at **Argos**; others wish it to be during the reign of **Ogyges** <note>OCR reads "Ocāsio," likely a transcription error for Ogyges, a legendary primeval king.</note>; and finally, some place it under King **Pandion** while he was ruling the **Athenians**—which seems to coincide with the times of **Minos**, the son of **Europa**. Some simply hold that she was ravished by **Jupiter**, and that she afterwards married **Asterius**, the King of the **Cretans**, and bore him sons: **Minos**, **Rhadamanthus**, and **Sarpedon**. Most say these were the sons of **Jupiter**, with some asserting that **Asterius** and **Jupiter** were one and the same person.

While this dispute may be left to others, many prefer for **Europa** to be famous for her marriage to such a great god. Furthermore, some affirm that—whether because of her extraordinary nobility (for the **Phoenicians**, through many merits, were more famous than others for the standing of their ancestors), or perhaps out of veneration for her power and her husband, or for the sake of her sons who were kings, or even by **Europa's** own exceptional virtue—the third part of the world was named **Europe** forever after her name. I certainly judge her a woman distinguished by virtues, not only because of the name granted to a part of the world, but also because of a remarkable bronze statue dedicated to the name of **Europa** by the illustrious philosopher **Pythagoras** <note>original: "a pictagora illustri phō" (philosopho)</note>, though it is now lost.

His magnificent works are believed to have been consumed by the passage of years, but that they were once very great is sufficiently evidenced by the fact that she was held in such high regard among...

# X. LIBYA, DAUGHTER OF EPAPHUS

**Libya** was the daughter of **Epaphus**, King of the **Egyptians**, and **Cassiopeia**. It was from her, as some authors claim, that the region of **Libya** <note>In ancient geography, "Libya" often referred to the entire continent of Africa or the specific region west of the Nile.</note> received its name. 

As is recorded in the most ancient histories, she was a woman of extraordinary beauty and possessed—a quality rarely found in the women of that time—an exceedingly noble and independent spirit, which led her to place very little value on her father's authority. For this reason, when she had reached a marriageable age, she scorned both her father and her home in **Egypt** and sailed across the sea to **Africa** with a few companions. 

When she arrived in that territory which was then known as **Gaetulia** <note>A region in North Africa inhabited by the Gaetuli, located south of the Atlas Mountains.</note>, she settled there and commanded that the entire region be called **Libya** after her own name. Furthermore, as some traditions maintain, she consorted there with the god **Neptune** and gave birth to two sons, **Agenor** and **Belus**. Of these two, **Agenor** later migrated to **Phoenicia**, where he reigned and fathered **Europa**; **Belus**, however, returned to **Egypt** and secured the kingdom there <note>This genealogy links the royal houses of Egypt, Phoenicia, and Crete, suggesting a shared ancestry for the rulers of the Mediterranean world.</note>.

# XII. CONCERNING THISBE, THE BABYLONIAN MAIDEN

**Thisbe**, a Babylonian maiden, was made more celebrated among mortals by the outcome of her unhappy love than by any other deed. Is it not held even by our ancestors who her parents were? <note>original: "huus etiam non a maioribus nostris qui parentes fuerit habitum?" The author questions the lack of specific genealogical records for Thisbe compared to other noble women.</note> It is sufficiently believed, however, that she had a house adjoining that of **Pyramus**, a boy of her own age, within **Babylon**. Since they were neighbors by right, they were in constant association; and from this, while they were still children, a singular chance sparked a childish affection. 

As the years grew, and since both were exceedingly beautiful, this childish love increased into a massive fire. They would reveal this to one another at least by nods sometimes, as they were now approaching the age of puberty. Indeed, when **Thisbe** became a young woman, she began to be kept at home by her parents for her future wedding rites <note>original: "hymeneos," referring to Hymen, the Greek god of marriage ceremonies.</note>. Since they both bore this separation very poorly, and sought anxiously for some way they might at least speak sometimes, they discovered a tiny crack in a shared wall which had been noticed by no one until then. 

When they had met secretly at this crack quite often, and through the habit of speaking a little—with the wall acting as a barrier so they blushed less—they expanded their freedom to express their affections. Often they shared their sighs, tears, passions, desires, and all their sufferings. Sometimes they even begged one another for peace of mind, for embraces and kisses, and for devotion, faith, and perpetual love. 

Finally, as the fire of their love grew, they decided to form a plan for flight on the following night, so that as soon as each was able to deceive their household, they would leave their homes. If one escaped first, they would go into the grove nearest the city and wait for the slower one by the fountain near the tomb of **King Ninus** <note>original: "nini regis busto." Ninus was the legendary founder of Nineveh and husband of Semiramis.</note>. By chance, the ardor of **Thisbe** was greater; she deceived her family and, wrapped in a cloak in the dead of night, she left her father’s house alone. With the moon showing the way, she went trembling into the grove; and while she waited by the fountain, at every movement of a thing...

and bloodied cloak. Thinking Thisbe had been devoured by the beast, he filled
the place with great wailing, accusing his wretched self because

<meta>This page concludes the tragic story of Pyramus and Thisbe with a moralizing reflection on the nature of young love and the responsibilities of parents. It then begins a new chapter concerning Hypermnestra, the Queen of Argos, detailing the origins of the feud between her father Danaus and her uncle Aegyptus.</meta>

<note>A metal paperclip is attached to the left edge of the page. There is a partially visible marginal note on the right edge of the page.</note>

Who would not grant at least one small tear for these youths and their so unhappy end? He would be made of stone. These children loved one another; they did not deserve a bloody end for this misfortune. It is a crime of the spirit in a flourishing age, but it is not a horrific crime for those who have been released from life. Can going into marriage truly be called a crime? Perhaps it was the worst of Luck <note>original: "fors pessima," referring to personified Fortune or Chance.</note> that sinned, or perhaps the wretched parents sinned. For the impulses of the young must be reined in gradually, lest while we wish to oppose them with a sudden barrier, we drive those who have lost hope over a precipice. The passion of desire is of immoderate strength, and it is a common plague among adolescents. In them, by Hercules <note>original: "edepol," an archaic Latin oath dedicated to Pollux, common in Roman comedy and later humanistic writing.</note>, it must be tolerated with a patient mind, since the nature of things wills it so; while we are strong in our prime, we are naturally inclined toward it, lest the human race fall into ruin if union were deferred until old age.

## Of Hypermnestra, Queen of the Argives.

**Hypermnestra** <note>The OCR reads "P Permestra," likely a misreading of a decorative initial "H" or a scribe's stylistic choice.</note> was famous for her lineage and dignity; she was the daughter of Danaus, King of the Argives, and the wife of Lynceus. It is gathered from the histories of the ancients that there were once two brothers in Egypt, the sons of King Belus, who were preeminent in their remarkable power <note>original: "imperio." The OCR reads "impio" (wicked), but "imperio" fits the context of royal brothers better.</note>. One of these was named Danaus, and the other Aegyptus. The fortune of their offspring was not equal, even if their number was; for Danaus had fifty daughters, while Aegyptus had just as many sons. 

<margin>Danaus and Aegyptus</margin>

When Danaus had received an oracle stating that he would be killed by the hand of a nephew born of his brother, he was secretly tormented by fear, especially since he did not know, out of such a vast multitude, whose hands he ought to hold in suspicion. It happened that when the children of both were reaching maturity, Aegyptus requested that all of Danaus's daughters be joined in marriage to his own sons. Danaus, having devised a cruel crime in his mind, granted this of his own accord. When the daughters were betrothed to their cousins and the wedding rites were being prepared, he strictly warned them all that if they wished for their own safety, each one should kill her husband with a blade <note>original: "ferro," literally "iron," often used to mean a sword or dagger.</note> on the first night while he was soaked with wine and feasting and bound in heavy sleep. They all, having brought knives secretly into their chambers, killed the youths who were sluggish from their excessive drinking, by their father's command. But Hypermnestra alone abstained; for the maiden had already set her heart upon Lynceus <note>The OCR reads "linū seu linteū," likely a misreading of "Linceum," the name of her husband.</note> her husband, as is the custom...

<summary>The author laments the death of Pyramus and Thisbe, blaming their parents' strictness before beginning the story of Hypermnestra, who refused her father's command to murder her husband on their wedding night.</summary>
<keywords>Hypermnestra, Danaus, Aegyptus, Lynceus, Argives, Belus, marriage, murder, prophecy, youth</keywords>

--- Page 23 ---
<meta>This page continues the story of Hypermnestra. On the previous page, her father Danaus had ordered his fifty daughters to murder their husbands (the sons of Aegyptus) on their wedding night. This text describes Hypermnestra's decision to spare her husband, Lynceus, and the moral reflections of the author on the futility of worldly ambition.</meta>

[She was the only one] of the young women who, upon seeing her bridegroom, immediately loved him. Because of this, moved by great compassion for him, she refrained from the unspeakable slaughter, to her own great glory. She persuaded the youth to flee to a place where he would be safe. 

Truly, when the fierce father had applauded the other daughters in the morning for the crime they had performed, Hypermnestra alone was rebuked. Locked away in prison, she wept for some time over her pious work <note>original: "pium... opus." The author uses "pious" ironically or paradoxically; while she disobeyed her father, she was "pious" (dutiful) to the higher laws of marriage and humanity.</note>. 

Alas, wretched mortals! With what a greedy and fervent spirit we seize upon things that are destined to perish. Scorning to look upon our own eventual downfall, by what execrable ways do we climb to high places (if the opportunity is offered)! By what crimes do we preserve those heights we have reached! It is as if we might believe that fickle Fortune <note>original: "volubilem... fortunam." The author references the common medieval and Renaissance trope of the Wheel of Fortune, which is constantly turning and never stable.</note> can be made firm by filthy wealth. And what is ridiculous is by what crimes and wicked deeds we try—I will not say to lengthen, but to make eternal—this fleeting and fragile "little day" of our life <note>original: "dieculam," a diminutive of "dies" (day), used here to emphasize how pathetically short a human life is compared to eternity.</note>, even though we are certain to go toward death...

And while her sisters had descended into foul infamy, she herself, on account of her love...

<meta>This page transitions from the story of Hypermnestra to the tragic end of Niobe and her family, followed by a moralizing reflection on feminine pride. It then begins a new chapter on Hypsipyle, the Queen of Lemnos, known for saving her father during a massacre.</meta>

She <note>Niobe, the Queen of Thebes, who boasted of her many children to the goddess Latona.</note> watched as all her children, blooming in the beauty of youth, were consumed within a brief space of time by a lethal plague, down to the very last one. And Amphion, who from being a father of fourteen children was suddenly made childless <note>original: "orbem," meaning bereft of offspring</note>, driven by grief, pierced himself through with a sword by his own hand. The Thebans believed these things happened because the gods above were avenging countless injuries. 

Niobe, however, surviving so many funerals, a mournful widow, fell into such a great and stubborn silence that she seemed more like an immovable rock than a woman. For which reason it was later established by the poets that at Sipylus <note>A mountain in modern-day Turkey where a natural rock formation, the "Weeping Rock," resembles a female face and appears to weep when snow melts.</note>—where her children had been buried—she had been turned into a stone statue. 

It is first of all hard and hateful, I will not say to tolerate, but even to look upon proud men; but proud women are disdainful and unbearable. Since nature produced men with a fervent and elevated spirit from the start, but brought forth women with a gentle disposition and relaxed virtue, suited more for elegance than for commands <note>The author reflects a common medieval and Renaissance view that pride in women is a violation of their "natural" submissiveness.</note>. Wherefore it is less surprising if the wrath of God and His judgment incline against proud women whenever they happen to exceed the boundaries of their weakness, as the foolish Niobe did. 

She was mocked by the fallacy of Fortune and ignorant that to be the parent of a large offspring was not by the merit of the parent, but the work of nature bending the kindness of heaven toward her. It would have been sufficient for her, first of all, to have given thanks to God from her eyes <note>meaning with tears of gratitude or sincere vision</note> rather than seeking divine honors of any kind for herself, as if it had been by her own power that she had brought forth such a numerous and conspicuous offspring. Since she acted proudly rather than prudently, she brought it about that she wept for her misfortune while alive, and after many centuries her name would be loathsome to posterity.

### Concerning Hypsipyle, Queen of Lemnos

Hypsipyle was a woman distinguished as much for her piety toward her father as for her unhappy exile, the death of her nursling Archemorus <note>original: "arthenioꝝ." Hypsipyle later became a nurse to the infant prince Archemorus, whose death by a serpent led to the founding of the Nemean Games.</note>, and the timely recovery of her sons. For she was the daughter of Thoas, king of the Lemnians, reigning in that age when that madness entered the minds of the women of the island to withdraw their untamed necks entirely from the yoke of men <note>This refers to the "Lemnian Deeds," where the women of the island, neglected by their husbands, conspired to kill every male inhabitant.</note>. For, having weighed the command of the old king, and having taken Hypsipyle into their confidence, they came to a unanimous counsel against him: that on the following night they would rage with swords against any and all males. Nor did the deed fail the purpose. Indeed, soothing...

A gentler plan than that of the others occurred to the mind of Hypsipyle. For, judging that it would be inhuman to be defiled with her father’s blood, she revealed the crime of the other women to her parent <note>Hypsipyle’s father was Thoas, the King of Lemnos. The other Lemnian women had conspired to murder all the men on the island because they believed their husbands had been unfaithful.</note>. After sending him down into a ship so that he might flee to Chios and escape the public’s rage, she immediately constructed a massive funeral pyre and feigned that she was performing the final rites for her father. 

Because this was believed by everyone, she was placed upon her father’s throne in the king’s stead, and was substituted as queen over those wicked women. Indeed, the devotion <note>original: "pietas," a Roman concept encompassing duty, religious devotion, and familial love.</note> of children toward their parents is most holy. For what is more fitting, what is more just, what is more praiseworthy, than to repay with kindness and honor those by whose labor we received nourishment when we were weak? By their skill we were protected, and by their unceasing love we were guided into adulthood. We were instructed by them in morals and learning, and increased in honors and resources; through them we are made strong in talent and character. Since Hypsipyle bestowed all these cares upon her parent, she is not undeservedly added to the ranks of famous women. 

Therefore, during her reign—whether driven by the force of the winds or arriving by design—Jason, while returning from Colchis <note>The text reads "incolcon redeuntibus." While most mythological accounts place Jason's stop at Lemnos on the way to find the Golden Fleece, this version suggests it occurred on the return journey.</note> with the Argonauts, despite others trying in vain to prevent him...

# On Medea, Queen of the Colchians

**M**edea was a most cruel example of ancient treachery. She was the daughter of the famous Aeëtes, King of the Colchians, and his wife Perse. She was quite beautiful, and by far the most learned in magical arts. No matter who her teacher was, she possessed such an intimate knowledge of the power of herbs that no one knew them better. She clearly knew how to trouble the heavens with chanted spells, call the winds from their caves, stir up storms, stop rivers, concoct poisons, and prepare fires for any kind of conflagration—she could achieve all such things.

Nor was her mind—which was much worse—at odds with her magical arts. For when those arts failed her, she believed she should resort to the sword. At that time, she fell passionately in love with Jason, a young man from Thessaly renowned for his valor. Jason had been sent to Colchis by his uncle Pelias—who was plotting against the young man’s virtue under the pretext of a most glorious expedition—to steal the Golden Fleece. Captivated by Jason’s excellence, Medea acted to win his favor by first inciting a rebellion among the local inhabitants. This civil war provided Jason with the opportunity to achieve his goal. Who, indeed, would have thought that a single glance could lead to the destruction of a most wealthy king?

Therefore, once that crime was committed and she had earned the embrace of her beloved youth, she fled secretly with him, taking all her father’s wealth. Yet, not content with such a great crime, her fierce mind turned to even worse deeds. Thinking that Aeëtes would follow the fugitives, she ordered that her young brother, Absyrtus (or Aegialeus), whom she had taken as a companion on her flight, be slaughtered. She commanded that his small limbs be scattered here and there on the island of Tomis <note>An island or region on the Black Sea; the name was later associated with the Greek word "temno," meaning "to cut," in honor of this myth.</note>, through which the pursuer was likely to pass. She did this so that while the father wretchedly gathered the scattered pieces to give them tears and a tomb, she would gain time for her escape. Her calculation did not fail her; it happened just as she planned.

Finally, after many wanderings, she arrived in Thessaly with her Jason. She filled her father-in-law, Aeson, with such joy—both from his son’s return and the celebrated victory, plunder, and illustrious marriage—that he seemed to be restored to his youthful prime <note>According to myth, Medea used her magic to literally rejuvenate Aeson by draining his old blood and replacing it with a magical brew.</note>. To secure the kingdom for Jason, she used her art to sow discord <note>original: "zizaniam," a term for weeds or tares, used here to mean the seeds of conflict.</note> between the daughters of Pelias and Pelias himself, wretchedly arming them against their father <note>Medea tricked the daughters of Pelias into cutting up their father and boiling him, promising she would rejuvenate him as she did for Aeson, but she intentionally let him die.</note>. 

However, as the years passed, Medea became hateful to Jason. In her place, he took Creusa, the daughter of Creon, King of the Corinthians, as his wife.

<summary>This page details the life of Medea, focusing on her mastery of magic, her betrayal of her father for Jason, and the horrific murder of her brother to aid their escape. It concludes with her arrival in Thessaly and the beginning of her downfall as Jason abandons her for another woman.</summary>
<keywords>Medea, Jason, Golden Fleece, Aeëtes, magic, Absyrtus, Pelias, Thessaly, Aeson, betrayal, infanticide</keywords>

--- Page 28 ---
<meta>This page continues the narrative of Medea’s life following her betrayal of her father, moving from her initial flight with Jason to her vengeful acts in Corinth, her exile in Athens, and her eventual return to her homeland. The text concludes with a moralizing discourse on the dangers of the sense of sight, using Medea’s initial attraction to Jason as a cautionary example.</meta>

Impatient and raging, after she had devised many schemes against Jason, she broke out into such a fury that, by her magical skill, she consumed Creusa <note>Also known as Glauce, the daughter of King Creon of Corinth, for whom Jason intended to leave Medea.</note> and all of Creon’s palace with flying fire <note>original: "igne volatili," likely referring to a magical or combustible substance like "Greek fire."</note>. Then, while Jason watched, she slaughtered the sons she had borne him and fled to Athens. There, she was married to King Aegeus and bore him a son, Medus. After she tried in vain to kill Theseus with poison upon his return <note>Theseus was Aegeus's long-lost son; Medea saw him as a threat to her own son's inheritance.</note>, she took flight for a third time. 

When she had returned to Jason’s favor, and after they were both driven out of all Thessaly by Aegialeus, the son of Pelias, she returned home to Colchis. There, she restored her aged and exiled father to his kingdom. What she did at last, or under what sky or by what kind of death she ended her days, I remember neither having read nor heard.

But lest I omit it, full liberty should not be granted to every eye. For by looking with them, we recognize splendors, we lead them into our world, and we attract all desires. Through their action, greed is stirred; beauty is praised; and squalor and poverty are unworthily condemned. And since the eyes are unlearned judges and believe only in the surface of things, they very often prefer shameful fictions to sacred truths and anxieties. While they commend things that should be cast away, they infect minds with a most foul stain through a brief touch of flattery.

These eyes, unknowing, are captured by even dishonest beauty, by lewd gestures, and by youthful insolence as if by biting waves; they are dragged, seized, and held. And since they are the door of the heart <note>original: "pectoris ianua," a common metaphorical trope in medieval moral philosophy regarding the five senses.</note>, through them lust sends its messengers to the mind; through them desire blows its sighs and kindles all its fires; through them the heart sends out its groans and shows its seductive passions. 

If anyone were truly wise, they would either close them, lift them toward heaven, or cast them down to the earth; there is no safe path for them between the two. But if one must travel that path at all, they must be restrained by a sharp bridle lest they run wild. Nature provided them with doors—the eyelids—not only to be closed in sleep, but to resist harmful things. Indeed, if Medea had been able to close them or turn them elsewhere while she stared greedily at Jason, her father’s power, her brother’s life, and the unbroken honor of her virginity would have lasted longer—all of which perished through the shamelessness of the eyes.

### On Arachne, the woman of Colophon

Arachne was an Asian woman of common birth, the daughter of Idmon of Colophon, a dyer of wool <note>Colophon was an ancient city in Lydia, famous for its luxury and crafts; Idmon’s profession as a dyer explains Arachne's early exposure to the textile arts.</note>. Although she was of humble origin, she is to be praised for several merits. Certain ancient writers assert that she was the inventor of the use of linen. They also say she was the first to devise bird-catching and fishing nets to be cast upon the earth. And since her son, whose name was Closter <note>The name "Closter" comes from the Greek word *kloster* (κλωστήρ), meaning "spindle."</note>, discovered spindles suitable for wool-making, some believe she held the supremacy of the textile art in her time. It was as if she possessed such great genius in her fingers, her threads, her weaver’s slat <note>original: "spatula," a tool used to beat the threads together on a loom.</note>, and other tools fit for such a task, that she performed with them what a painter does with a brush—no small skill to be found in a woman.

Truly, while she was not only living among weavers <note>original: "ypheis," likely from the Greek "hyphantes" (weaving/weavers).</note> but heard her fame celebrated everywhere, she became so proud that she dared to enter into a contest against Pallas <note>Pallas Athena, the Greek goddess of wisdom and crafts, specifically weaving.</note>, the discoverer of this art. And when she could not bear being surpassed with an even mind, she ended her life by putting on a noose. From this, an opportunity was given to storytellers: for since the name for the spider <note>original: "aranea vermis," literally "the spider-worm."</note> corresponds with the name Arachne and her craft, and since the creature hangs from a thread just as she herself hung by a noose, they said that Arachne was turned into a spider by the mercy of the gods, so that she might spend her time in her former constant care of weaving.

Others, however, say that although she put on the noose to die, she did not die, as she was helped by her family; instead, she wasted away from this artfully placed grief.

Now, if there is anyone, I pray, who believes they excel others in anything, let them say if they think Arachne herself could turn the heavens and draw all honors to herself. Or rather, could she have made God himself, the author of all things, so kind toward her through her prayers and merits that He was compelled to pour all His graces into her from the fitting bosom of His bounty, ignoring everyone else? But why do I ask? For as she seems to have thought, it is indeed most foolish. Nature binds the heavens by an eternal law and provides talents suited for various things to all people. These talents, I believe, are made sluggish by leisure and sloth, yet through study and exercise they become bright and capable of the greatest things; and by that same driving nature, they are carried by a desire toward the knowledge of all things—even if they do not all possess the same skill or fortune. And if this is so, what prevents many from becoming equals in the same field? Why should anyone think themselves the only one of merit among such an innumerable multitude of mortals?

<summary>This page tells the story of Arachne, credited here with inventing linen and nets, whose pride led her to challenge the goddess Pallas and eventually hang herself, inspiring the myth of her transformation into a spider. The author concludes with a philosophical reflection on how talent is a gift of nature that requires hard work, warning against the arrogance of believing one is uniquely gifted.</summary>
<keywords>Arachne, Colophon, weaving, Pallas Athena, hubris, invention of linen, Idmon, Closter, mythology, artistic pride</keywords>

--- Page 30 ---
<meta>The previous page concluded the story of Arachne’s competition with the goddess Pallas Athena and her transformation into a spider, serving as a moral warning against intellectual pride and artistic hubris.</meta>

...to prevail over others in the race for glory is a sign of a foolish mind. I would wish, indeed, that Arachne were the only one among us who was a laughingstock in this matter, rather than the countless others who are ensnared in such madness. Those who hurl themselves into the abyss of foolish presumption make Arachne appear less ridiculous by comparison.

### On Orithyia and Antiope, Queens of the Amazons

Orithyia was the daughter of Marpesia. Along with Antiope—whom some believe to have been her sister—she was queen of the Amazons after Marpesia. Above all other things, she was notable and highly commended for her perpetual virginity. However, she was most powerful in wars alongside her partner in sovereignty, Antiope, so that she expanded the empire <note>original: "imperium"</note> of the Amazons with many honors. She raised the renown of her military discipline so high that Eurystheus, the King of Mycenae, believed her belt could only be obtained through war. For this reason, they say he commanded this task to Hercules as a great labor, so that he might bring the belt to the king.

It is surely an extraordinary glory for a woman that Hercules—who conquered all things—was set as an opponent against her because of the virtue of her splendid arms. When Hercules’ expedition had arrived and he had occupied the Amazon shore with his long ships <note>original: "longis navibus," referring to war galleys</note>, the Amazons were thrown into a tumult because of their small numbers and lack of preparation while Orithyia was absent; they thus provided him an easy victory. Menalippe and Hippolyte, the sisters of Antiope, were captured; once the queen's belt was surrendered, Menalippe was restored. 

But when Orithyia heard that Theseus, a companion of the expedition, had carried off Hippolyte, she dared to stir up war in Greece, having gathered reinforcements from all sides. However, having been deserted by her allies due to dissension and separated from her goal by the Athenians, she returned to her kingdom. I do not remember finding any record of what she did further.

### On the Erythraean or Eriphyle Sibyl

Erythraea, or Eriphyle, was one of the Sibyls and was very distinguished. Some think that these Sibyls were ten in number. They designate them by their proper names, but since they were all very powerful in prophecy, they give them all this surname. For *sios* in the celestial tongue <note>original: "celico sermone," likely referring to the Doric Greek dialect where "Theos" becomes "Sios"</note> sounds like "God" in Latin, and they said that *byle* means "mind." Therefore, "Sibyls" are so called as if they were divine in mind, or bearing God in their mind. They assert that this woman was the most celebrated of all these venerable figures, and that her origin was among the Babylonians some time before the Trojan War. Although some...

<meta>The previous page concluded the accounts of Orithyia and Antiope, queens of the Amazons, whose military prowess and virginity were held in high regard. ...they think she prophesied during the time of Romulus, the King of the Romans. Her name (as some say) was Eriphila <note>Also known as Herophile in classical Greek tradition</note> or Erythraea, so named because she stayed for a long time on the island of Erythraea, and many of her poems <note>original: "carmina," referring here to her oracular verses or songs</note> were found in that same place. 

Therefore, so great was the merit of her intellect, her speech, or her devotion in the sight of God that—by her vigilant study and not without a divine gift—she earned the right (if what we read was truly spoken by her) to describe things with such great clarity that her work seems more like a Gospel <note>original: "evangelium"; the author refers to the Christian belief that the Sibyls accurately predicted the life of Christ</note> than a prophecy. 

While the Greeks were still looking on, she described their labors and the destruction of Troy <note>original: "ylionis excidium," referring to the fall of Ilium or Troy</note> in verse so lucidly that nothing was known more clearly after the fact than before it happened. In the same way, she encompassed Rome itself and its various misfortunes in a few verses long before the city’s beginning, so that in our own age she seems to have written a brief summary <term>epitome|A condensed summary or abstract of a larger work or history</term> rather than to have predicted a future time. And (what is far greater in my judgment), she expressed the secrets of the divine mind, though only through figures...

--- Page 32 ---
<meta>The previous page concluded its account of the Erythraean Sibyl. This page begins mid-sentence, concluding a historical reinterpretation of the myth of Medusa, presenting her not as a monster, but as a wealthy and beautiful African queen whose "stony gaze" was actually a metaphor for her stunning appearance and immense riches.</meta>

...her body was raised up with a worthy height. But among her other great qualities, there was such a calm vigor in her eyes that those upon whom she looked kindly were rendered almost immobile and lost to themselves <note>original: "sui nescios"; the author suggests her beauty was so stunning it paralyzed onlookers like the mythical stone-turning gaze</note>. Furthermore, some assert that she was most skilled in agriculture, and that she obtained the surname "Gorgon" from that pursuit. By her work and with wonderful sagacity, she not only preserved her ancestral riches but increased them so immensely that those who were willing to believe it claimed she surpassed all western kings in her treasures. And thus, as much by her extraordinary beauty as by her wealth and wisdom, she attained the widest fame even among distant nations. 

But among others, news of her was brought by famous rumor to the Argives <note>The people of Argos in Greece</note>. Among them Perseus, the most flourishing of the Achaean youth, having heard such a report, fell into a desire both of seeing this most beautiful woman and of seizing her treasures. And so, having boarded a ship—which had the horse Pegasus as its figurehead <note>original: "insigne"; the author explains the "winged horse" of myth as a ship's decoration</note>—he was carried toward the west with wonderful speed. There, by his prudence, experience, and arms, he overcame the queen; and laden with gold and rich spoils, he returned to his own people. 

From these events, poetic fiction found a place for itself, through which we read that Medusa the Gorgon was accustomed to turn those whom she looked upon into stone; and that her hair was turned into snakes by the wrath of Minerva because she had violated Minerva's temple by lying with Neptune. The poets also say she gave birth to Pegasus, and that Perseus, sitting upon a winged horse, had flown into her kingdom and made use of the Aegis <term>Aegis|A mythological shield or breastplate associated with Athena (Minerva), often featuring the head of a Gorgon</term> of Pallas. 

Unhappy is the possession of gold! For if it is hidden, it provides no benefit to the owner; if it shines, a thousand snares are born from those who desire it. Even if the hands of violent men are held back, the anxious cares of the owner do not cease. For peace of mind is put to flight, sleep is withdrawn, fear enters, trust is diminished, suspicion is increased, and in short, every enjoyment of life is hindered for the wretched man. If indeed the wealth should perish by any chance, the greedy man, having become poor, is tortured by anxieties; the generous man praises the change, the envious man laughs, the destitute man is consoled, and the whole common crowd turns the suffering man's grief into a story.

### Concerning Iole, daughter of the King of the Aetolians.

**Iole** was the most beautiful daughter of Eurytus, the King of Aetolia, a maiden notable among all others of that region. There are those who assert she was loved by Hercules, the conqueror of the world. Eurytus had promised her marriage to him, but later, at the persuasion of his son, he denied Hercules when he asked for her. For which reason, being angered...

...and that he would deny her nothing that she might ask.
