She made him lay aside the skin of the Nemean lion—the badge of his bravery—and lay aside the poplar wreath, the quivers, and the arrows. 

Since these changes did not sufficiently satisfy her mind, she more boldly assailed his spirit with pre-planned charms <note>original: "telis"; literally "missiles" or "weapons," used here metaphorically for her feminine wiles</note>. First, she ordered his fingers to be adorned with rings, his rough head to be softened with perfumes, and his hairy locks to be parted with a comb. She ordered his shaggy beard to be anointed with nard <note>A costly, fragrant ointment used in the ancient world</note>, and for him to be decorated with girlish corals and even a Maeonian turban <note>original: "meonia... mitra"; a Lydian style of head-covering, indicating his transition into the soft, eastern customs of Queen Omphale's court</note>. 

Then, she ordered him to put on purple robes and soft clothes. This young woman, armed with such deceits, considered it a far greater glory to have effeminated so robust a man through her wanton charms than to have killed him with a sword or weapons. Furthermore, when she judged that her indignation <note>or "scornful pride"</note> was not yet sufficiently satisfied...

<meta>This page continues the moral reflection on the downfall of Hercules. ...[the danger] was greater than those things which he himself had often subdued. This situation ought to instill no small amount of fear in those concerned for their own salvation, and even shake the very body, since it is clear how strong and powerful an enemy looms over us. Therefore, we must be vigilant, and our hearts must be armed by us with the greatest possible strength. For this enemy does not weigh upon the unwilling. 

Resistance must therefore be made at the very beginning <note>original: "principiis"; a reference to the classical maxim *Principiis obsta*—resist the first advances of an illness or passion before it takes root</note>; the eyes must be bridled lest they gaze upon vanities; the ears must be stopped up in the manner of the asp <note>A reference to the "deaf adder" from the Psalms, a common medieval metaphor for a person who closes their ears to the "charms" of sin or worldly temptation</note>; and lust must be suppressed by unceasing labors. For it offers itself as something charming to the unwary and appears peaceful at first sight; and if it is welcomed with joyful hope, it delights at its first entry. 

It persuades; it adorns the composed manners of the body with urban wit <term>facetias urbicas: sophisticated, city-bred humor and social graces associated with courtly life</term>, dances, songs and poems, games, banquets, and similar things. But after it has occupied the whole man through his own approval, and once his liberty has been subdued and chains and various bonds have been cast upon his mind, it rouses anxieties <note>original: "suspicia"; likely a variant of *suspiria* (sighs) or *suspicionem* (suspicions), indicating the restless state of a lover</note> beyond what was hoped for. It forces the intellect into deceptive arts, making no distinction between virtues and vices, so long as it achieves what it desires, counting as an enemy whatever stands in its way. From this point, with burning flames...

...it heaps up anxieties and desires; it brings almost intolerable pains, curable by no remedy except tears, complaints, and sometimes death. Flatteries turn into threats; violence is prepared; frustrated affection is condemned. Nor is it lacking that this craftsman of evils <note>The "craftsman" refers to Cupid or the personified spirit of Lust</note> sometimes instills such madness that he drives the wretched into snares and onto swords. O how sweet, how pleasant is this love!—which, though we ought to shudder at and flee from it, we extol as a god; him we worship, him we entreat as suppliants, and we fashion a sacrifice from sighs and tears. Defilements, adulteries, and incests are what we offer, and we cast on the crowns of our obscenities <note>original: "obsceitatu nŕay [nostrarum] coronas imittimus"</note>.

->### On Deianira, the wife of Hercules.<-

**Deianira** was the daughter of Oeneus, king of the Aetolians <note>original: "Etholozuz"; the Aetolians lived in a mountainous region of Greece</note> (as some assert), and the sister of Meleager. She was a maiden of such remarkable beauty that a contest arose between Achelous <note>original: "Atheolum"; the river god Achelous, who wrestled Hercules for the right to marry Deianira</note> and Hercules to win her in marriage. When she had been yielded to Hercules as the victor, she was lusted after by the centaur Nessus. As Hercules was transporting her from Calydon to his homeland, he encountered the eager Nessus at the river Evenus in Calydon. Since he was a horse-man <term>eques: literally "horseman," but here describing the physiology of a centaur, who is half-man and half-horse</term>, he offered his service to Hercules to carry Deianira across. 

Hercules granted this, intending to swim across after his wife. But Nessus, as if his wish had been granted, once he had crossed the river, seized his beloved and took flight. Since Hercules could not overtake him on foot, he struck the fleeing centaur with an arrow tainted with Lernean poison <note>original: "sagitta lernea infecta tabe"; Hercules had dipped his arrows in the poisonous blood of the Hydra, a monster he killed in the Lernean swamp</note>. Feeling this and knowing himself to be dying, Nessus immediately gave Deianira a garment stained with his own blood, claiming that by keeping it thus bloodied, she could restrain Hercules from any other love and keep him for herself. 

The credulous Deianira, taking it as a most significant gift and keeping it hidden for a long time, eventually sent it cautiously via the servant Lycas to Hercules, who was then in love with Omphale or Iole. But when he had dissolved the blood, tainted with poison, through his sweat, and it had been absorbed <note>original: "potis qz bibisset"; literally 'having drunk it in,' referring to the skin or the body absorbing the toxic mixture</note>, he turned to madness and went forward of his own accord to be consumed by fire. Thus Deianira, while hoping to win back such a great husband, lost him, and she even paid the price for Nessus's revenge.

<summary>This page concludes a moral warning against the destructive nature of lust and recounts the myth of Deianira, whose attempt to regain Hercules' love with a poisoned robe led to his agonizing death.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the catalog of famous women from antiquity. ## On Jocasta, Queen of Thebes.

**Jocasta**, queen of Thebes, was more famous for her misfortune than for her merits or her kingdom. Since she traced her noble origin back to the very first founders of Thebes <note>The founders of Thebes refer to Cadmus and the "Spartoi," the warriors who sprang from the dragon's teeth he sowed.</note>, she was married as a maiden to **Laius**, the king of the Thebans. When she had conceived a son by him, because of a grim response received from an oracle <note>The oracle of Apollo at Delphi had warned Laius that any son he fathered would kill him.</note>, she sorrowfully handed the newborn over to be cast out to wild beasts. When she believed he had been immediately devoured, he was instead raised by the king of Corinth as a son. Having already grown to maturity, and after Laius had been killed by him in Phocis <note>Oedipus killed Laius at a "triple crossroads" in Phocis, not knowing the man was his father.</note>, she—now a widow—took the unknown man as her husband. By him she bore sons, **Eteocles** and **Polynices**, and as many daughters, **Ismene** and **Antigone**. And when she already seemed happy in both her kingdom and her offspring, she learned by a response of the gods that he whom she considered her lawful husband was in fact her son.

Though she herself bore this most bitterly, he bore it even more so; to such an extent that, out of shame for the crime committed, and while desiring a cruel death, he plucked out his eyes and cast them into the darkness <note>original: "⁊ ignū"; likely a contraction of "inane," referring to the void or darkness of blindness.</note>. Taking this discord as a pretext, the sons went to war, having broken their treaties. Although with great sadness she often saw them descend into combat against each other, she eventually learned that they had killed one another with mutual wounds in a duel. Unable to endure this grief, the wretched mother and grandmother—though she still saw her brother **Creon** and her bereaved son and husband captive, and her daughters Ismene and Antigone entangled in falling fortune—she at last drove out her soul, struggling and weary of evils, with a blade. Thus, she ended her anxieties along with her life. There are some, however, who believe she could not bear her guilty errors for so long, and that as soon as she saw Oedipus plucking out his eyes, she immediately turned her violence upon herself.

## On Amalthea, or Eryphile, the Sibyl.

The maiden **Amalthea** (whom some call **Eryphile**, daughter of Glaucus) is believed to have drawn her origin from Cumae, an ancient town of Chalcidian Campania <note>The "Chalcidian Campania" refers to the region in Italy settled by Greeks from Euboea; Cumae was the most famous of these colonies and the home of the Sibyl.</note>. Since she was one of the **Sibyls** <term>Sibyls: Ancient female prophets who were believed to speak the will of the gods while in a state of divine ecstasy.</term>, some believe she flourished at the time of the destruction of Troy and lived to such an advanced age that she survived until the time of **Tarquinius** <note>Tarquinius Priscus (the Elder), the fifth king of Rome.</note>, the king of the Romans. It was her way of life that she never allowed herself to be defiled by the touch of a man. And although the tales of the poets testify that she was loved by Phoebus <note>Apollo</note>, and by his gift received both many years and...

<meta>This page continues the biography of the Cumaean Sibyl, following the account of her prophetic powers. It details her interaction with the Roman kings and the famous story of the Sibylline Books, concluding with a moral exhortation on the value of study over idleness.</meta>

...attained divinity. I, for my part, believe that by the merit of her virginity she received the light of prophecy from the true sun (who enlightens every person coming into this world <note>original: "qui illuminat omnē hominē venientem in hunc mundū" — This is a reference to the Gospel of John 1:9, here used by the author to suggest that the Sibyl received her foresight from a divine source recognized by Christian theology.</note>). Through this light, she foretold many things and wrote of the future. 

Moreover, they say that on the shore of Baiae, next to Lake Avernus <note>Lake Avernus was considered by the ancients to be the entrance to the underworld due to its volcanic vapors.</note>, there was a famous oracle belonging to her. Indeed, I myself have seen it and heard of it, and it preserves her name to this day. Although it may be worn away by great age and the structure is half-collapsed, it still preserves its ancient majesty and offers a sense of wonder to those who gaze upon its greatness. 

There are some, furthermore, who say that she provided guidance to the fugitive **Aeneas** to the underworld <note>Aeneas’s journey to consult his father in the land of the dead is the subject of Book VI of Virgil’s *Aeneid*.</note>; this I do not believe, but I will speak of this elsewhere. Those who wish to claim she lived through many centuries assert that she came to Rome and brought nine books to **Tarquinius Priscus** <note>Tarquinius Priscus was the fifth King of Rome; however, other versions of this legend attribute the story to the seventh king, Tarquinius Superbus.</note>. When Tarquin refused the requested price for them, she burned three of the books before his eyes. On the following day, she demanded the same price for the remaining six (the same price she had previously asked for the nine), asserting that if it were not given, she would immediately burn three more. On the day after that, the remaining books were purchased by Tarquin at the requested price. When these were preserved, it was discovered by later generations that they contained all the future deeds of the Romans. For this reason, the Romans thereafter guarded them with the greatest care, and whenever a crisis arose, they would return to them as to an oracle to consult about the future.

For me, it is hard to believe that she was the same person as **Deiphobe** <note>Deiphobe is the specific name Virgil gives to the Sibyl of Cumae.</note>. Nevertheless, we read that she ended her days among the Sicilians, and for a long time her tomb was pointed out by the inhabitants there. 

Therefore, through study and divine grace, we are made illustrious—gifts which are denied to no one who makes themselves worthy. But if we look at those who are sluggish with laziness, we see clearly that with their time wasted from the very womb, even when they die at a great age, they are carried to the grave as if they had never lived. Finally, if women who are ever-watchful in intellect and godliness possess such power, what must be thought of wretched men? For men have a readier aptitude for all things; if they would only cast off their sloth, they would surely rise to divinity itself. Let those weep and waste away, therefore, from whom so great a gift has been taken by inertia; let them admit that among living men they are but stones—which will happen when, though speechless, they are forced to confess their own shame.

# Concerning Nicostrata, or Carmenta, daughter of King Pallas

**Nicostrata** (who was later named **Carmenta** among the Italians) was the daughter of **Pallas**, King of the Arcadians <note>Arcadia was a mountainous region in Greece, often idealized in literature as a pastoral paradise.</note>. According to some, she married **Pallas** of Arcadia; according to others, she was his daughter-in-law. She was distinguished not only by the splendor of her royal status, but even more so because she was most learned in Greek literature. Her mind was so versatile that through watchful study she reached the point of prophecy and became a most famous seer. Because she would often reveal the future in song <note>original: "carmine"—The author is explaining the etymology of her name: *Carmen* (song/poem/incantation) becoming *Carmenta*.</note> to those who asked and often of her own accord, the Latins named her **Carmenta**, almost entirely setting aside her original name, **Nicostrata**, as if it had been abolished.

She was the mother of **Evander**, King of the Arcadians, whom the fables of the ancients claim was conceived by **Mercury**—either because he was an eloquent and fluent man, or because he was cunning <note>In mythology, Mercury (Hermes) was the god of both eloquence and thieves/tricksters; the author provides a rationalist explanation for the myth of divine parentage.</note>. Evander (as some say) had accidentally killed his true father, or (as others prefer) was driven from his ancestral kingdom due to a rebellion of his citizens arising from another cause. At the urging of his mother **Carmenta**, who promised great things through her prophecy if he sought the dwelling she pointed out, she became his companion in travel. Boarding ships with a portion of their people, they sailed with a favorable wind from the Peloponnese to the mouth of the **Tiber**. 

Led by his mother, he settled on the **Palatine Hill** (which he named after his father **Pallas** or his son **Pallas**, where later great **Rome** was founded) with his followers and his mother, and there he built the town of **Pallanteum**. Truly, when **Carmenta** took charge of the indigenous people, who were almost like wild men—even though they had recently learned to sow crops through the gift of Latin refugees—and who had no use of letters, or only a modicum, and were accustomed to the Greek style; foreseeing from afar with her divine mind how great the fame of those people and that region would be in the future, she thought it unworthy that their great deeds should be shown to future ages with the help of foreign letters. 

She applied herself with all the strength of her intellect to provide the people with their own letters, entirely different from those of other nations. God was not absent from this undertaking. For by her grace, it came to pass that she taught the combinations of new characters she had invented according to the Italian idiom. She was content to have forged only sixteen figures, just as long before **Cadmus**, the founder of **Thebes**, had invented for the Greeks <note>Ancient tradition held that the original alphabet consisted of only 16 letters, with others added later by figures like Palamedes or Simonides.</note>. These we call Latin to this very day. And we hold her gift...

<summary>This page recounts the legend of Nicostrata (Carmenta), a learned Greek woman who migrated to Italy with her son Evander. She is credited with founding the settlement on the Palatine Hill and inventing the sixteen letters of the original Latin alphabet.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the biography of Nicostrata (Carmenta), moving from her arrival in Italy and her prophetic gifts to her lasting legacy in Roman geography and the honors bestowed upon her by the early inhabitants of the city.</meta>

And fittingly, certain wise men have added that nothing among the ancients was more enriched <note>original: "auctiora"—suggesting her contributions significantly expanded or improved the existing culture.</note>. Though the Latins greatly admired her prophecy in letters <note>This refers to her legendary role in adapting the Greek alphabet into the Latin one, effectively giving the Latins the gift of literacy.</note>, this invention seemed so marvelous that the unrefined people truly believed **Carmenta** was not a human woman, but rather a goddess. 

For this reason, since they had celebrated her with divine honors while she was alive, they built a shrine in her name after her death at the foot of the **Capitoline Hill** (where she had lived her life); and for her perpetual memory, they called the adjacent places the **Carmentalia** <note>The Carmentalia was also the name of a Roman festival held in her honor in January, reflecting her status as a patroness of childbirth and prophecy.</note> after her name. Even when Rome had grown great, it did not allow this to be abolished; on the contrary, for many centuries they called the city gate (which the citizens had built there as necessity required) the **Carmental Gate** <note>The *Porta Carmentalis* was a double gate in the Servian Wall of Rome, located near the shrine of Carmenta.</note> after the name of **Carmenta**.

In times past, **Italy** flourished with many gifts above the other regions of the world, and shone almost with a celestial light; nor was such a splendid brightness sought only under its own sky. For from **Alba** <note>original: "alpa"—likely referring to Alba Longa, the ancient mother-city of Rome, noted for its royal lineage.</note> came opulence, and royal finery, and the nobility of blood; and if much were to be added <note>The text ends mid-sentence, suggesting further details on the origins of Italian nobility follow on the next page.</note>...

<meta>This page continues the praise of Italy's ancient contributions, specifically focusing on the invention of the Latin alphabet by Carmenta (Nicostrata). <note>A paperclip is attached to the left edge of the page.</note>

Through these <note>original: "hijs"—referring to the letters of the alphabet discussed on the previous page.</note> we transmit our own thoughts and receive those of others with faith. Through these, we join friendships across great distances and preserve them through mutual correspondence. These letters describe God to us as far as is possible; they designate the heavens, the lands, the seas, and all living creatures. Nor is there anything you might seek that is possible to know which you cannot perceive through these, if you are vigilant. In short, whatever cannot be embraced or held by the mind’s breadth due to its vastness, the work of these letters commits to the most faithful safekeeping <note>Writing is presented here as a "faithful custody" that overcomes the limitations of human memory.</note>. 

And although some of these benefits may fall to others, it nevertheless takes nothing away from what is commendable in our own. Moreover, of such excellent gifts, some we have lost, some we have given away, and some we still hold in name only rather than in practice <note>original: "solo nomine potius quam effectu"—this suggests a lament that the power of Latin learning has diminished into mere titles or reputation.</note>. 

But whatever has become of the rest, whether by our own fault or by fortune, neither German rapacity, nor Gallic fury, nor English cunning, nor Spanish ferocity <note>The author lists national stereotypes of the era—German greed, French anger, English trickery, and Spanish cruelty—to emphasize that Latin culture survives all external threats.</note>, nor the unrefined barbarity or assault of any other nation, can steal away this so grand, so remarkable, so excellent glory of the Latin name... <note>The text cuts off mid-word at "surrip," likely for "surripere," meaning to snatch or steal away.</note>

<meta>This page continues the author's argument from the previous page. Having just mentioned various national vices (German greed, Gallic fury, etc.) that threaten the Latin name, the text now pivots to a classical mythological example—the story of Cephalus and Procris—to illustrate the corrupting power of gold and the dangers of jealousy.</meta>

...tried to draw him into her own opinion with prayers <note>The text likely refers to the goddess Aurora, who in myth kidnapped the hunter Cephalus and tried to turn him against his wife, Procris.</note>. From which, it is said, she spoke: "O Cephalus, you will regret having loved Procris so fervently; you will soon discover—if there is anyone to tempt her—that she has preferred gold to your love." 

Hearing this, the young man, eager to test her, departed, feigning a long journey. Then, having turned back toward his homeland in disguise, he tested his wife's constancy through a mediator with gifts. No matter how great the things promised at first, they were unable to move her. Yet, as he persevered and increased the jewelry, he finally bent her wavering mind; the desired embrace and night were promised to him, provided the pledged gold were given. 

Then Cephalus appeared, overwhelmed with grief, because through this trick he had caught the frivolous nature of Procris's love. She, covered in blushes and driven by the consciousness of her crime, immediately fled into the woods and gave herself over to solitude. However, the young man, unable to bear his love, went further and granted her pardon, and with prayers called back the one who had spurned him into his favor <note>original: "reuocauit in graciam"—literally "recalled into grace," meaning he restored her to his good graces.</note>. 

But what does it matter? The power of indulgence is nothing against the stings of conscience. Procris was driven by various impulses of the soul; and divided by jealousy—lest perhaps her husband might do to her through the charms of love what she herself had traded for gold—she began to follow the hunter secretly through cliffs, steep mountain ridges, and the secrets of the valleys. 

While she was doing this, it happened that while Procris was hiding among the grassy valleys and marshy reeds, she moved; believed by her husband to be a wild beast, she was pierced by an arrow and perished. I know not what I should say: whether nothing on earth is more powerful than gold, or whether it is more foolish to seek that which you do not wish to lose <note>The author is critiquing Cephalus for testing his wife's fidelity—seeking a truth that only brought him misery.</note>. While the woman, looking upon both, brought upon herself an indelible mark of shame and found the death she was least seeking. 

But so that I may end this discussion of the immoderate desire for gold (to which we foolish people are almost all drawn), I ask those seized by such stubborn zeal to say what benefit they feel they gain from it—what honor, what praise, or what glory they achieve. In my judgment, this is a ridiculous sickness of the mind, taking its origin from the faint-heartedness of the sufferer, since we see it nowhere else but among those who consider themselves of such lowly virtue that they easily concede that anyone at all should be preferred over them.

### Concerning Argia, the wife of Polynices, and daughter of King Adrastus.

<margin>Argia was a Greek woman, drawing her noble lineage from the ancient kings of the Argives; she was the daughter of King Adrastus.</margin> Just as she offered a joyful sight of herself to her contemporaries through her remarkable beauty, so too did she leave for those who followed an enduring, most honest, and famous testimony of conjugal love. Because of this, even down to our own day, her bright name has reached us with exceptional brilliance.

Therefore, she was married to Polynices <note>original: "Pollicinio"</note>, the son of Oedipus, King of the Thebans. He was an exile, and although she had already borne him a son named Thersander, she noticed that he was being tormented by "biting cares" <note>This refers to the deep anxiety and resentment Polynices felt while living in exile.</note> due to the deceit of his brother. Driven headlong into anxiety for him, she not only pleaded with her father—already an old man—with tears and prayers, but she even roused him to take up arms against Eteocles <note>original: "ethyoclem"</note>, who was occupying the Theban kingdom in violation of the laws of their agreement with his brother <note>The brothers Eteocles and Polynices had agreed to alternate the kingship of Thebes every year. Eteocles served the first year but refused to step down, prompting Argia to help her husband reclaim his right by force.</note>.

<summary>The text introduces Argia, daughter of King Adrastus, as a historical paragon of marital loyalty who supported her husband Polynices in his quest to reclaim the throne of Thebes.</summary>
<keywords>Argia, Polynices, Adrastus, Eteocles, Thebes, marital devotion, Seven Against Thebes, Greek mythology</keywords>

--- Page 43 ---
<meta>This page continues the story of Argia, the wife of Polynices. Following the bloody defeat of the Seven Against Thebes, Argia defies the edicts of the new King Creon to provide her husband with a proper burial. The text then transitions to the life of Manto, the daughter of the famous blind seer Tiresias, highlighting her skill in the occult arts.</meta>

She could not hide her grief from her husband <note>The text refers to the deep devotion she showed to Polynices even after his death on the battlefield.</note>. The filth of his bloodied face could not keep his wife's kisses away. No voices, no tears, and not even the great power of Creon's command could turn her aside. For after she had often sought the spark of life from his mouth with her kisses, washed his foul-smelling limbs with her tears, and frequently called his lifeless soul back into her arms with her voice, she finally gave him over to the now-fragrant funeral flames <note>original: "flaminis," likely a scribal error for "flammis"</note>—lest she omit any duty of piety. Once he was consumed by the fire, she shared his burial. 

Nor did she tremble with fear of the sword or chains of the severe king for her deed. Many women have often wept over the illnesses, imprisonment, poverty, and various misfortunes of their husbands, yet they did so while hope for better fortune remained and while fear of a harsher fate was held in check by love. Although this is seen as praiseworthy, it cannot be called the ultimate test of affection, as Argia's service can. She sought out enemy fields while she could still have wept safely in her own fatherland; she handled a foul corpse when she could have anointed others; she granted the honors of royal funeral fires to him when, considering the circumstances, a secret burial would have been enough; she cried out with womanly wails where she could have passed by in silence. She had nothing to hope for from her slain, exiled husband, yet she had everything to fear from the enemy. Thus true love, thus perfect loyalty, thus the sanctity of marriage and unblemished chastity were able to persuade her. By this merit, Argia comes to us worthy of praise, to be cherished and extolled with a splendid proclamation.

## Concerning Manto, daughter of Tiresias.

Manto was the daughter of Tiresias, the greatest of the Theban prophets, in the time of King Oedipus and his sons. She was a woman of distinction. Beneath her father’s guidance, she possessed a mind so quick and capable that she learned the art of pyromancy <term>Pyromancy: The practice of divining the future by observing the behavior of fire and smoke.</term>—which some say was invented by the ancient Chaldeans or, as others would have it, by Nimrod—to an extraordinary degree. 

In her time, no one understood better the movements, colors, and crackling sounds of flames through which (by some devilish work, I know not what) the demonstrations of the future are said to be revealed. Furthermore, she recognized with a sharp, intuitive gaze the portents in the fibers of livestock, the livers of bulls, and the entrails of any animal. It was very often believed that through her arts she compelled unclean spirits and the ghosts of the underworld <note>original: "inferorum manes"</note> to speak and give answers to those who questioned her. Certainly, when the Argive kings who had besieged Thebes had fallen in war and Creon had seized the city, she—as it pleases some to say—prepared a new...

# Of Polyxena, daughter of King Priam.

**P**olyxena was the virgin daughter of King Priam, King of the Trojans, and Hecuba. She was a young girl of such flourishing beauty that she was able to ignite the flames of desire in the stern breast of Achilles, son of Peleus. Through the trickery of her mother, Hecuba, she led him alone by night to the temple of Apollo, only to bring about his death. Oh, how much less than her due was her fate! After the Trojan forces had fallen and been cast down, she was led by Neoptolemus <note>Also known as Pyrrhus, the son of Achilles</note> as a sacrificial victim <note>original: "piaculum," an offering meant to appease the spirits of the dead</note> by the hand of her father's killer to his tomb. There (if any faith can be placed in the writings of the ancients), seeing that the fierce youth had drawn his sword while all the others standing around were weeping...

->### About Hecuba, Queen of the Trojans<-

Hecuba was the most illustrious queen of the Trojans; she was both an extraordinary flash of fleeting splendor and the most certain testament <note>original: "documentum," meaning a lesson or evidentiary proof of a concept</note> to human misery. ->### About Clytemnestra, Queen of Mycenae<-

**Clytemnestra** was the daughter of Tyndareus, King of Oebalia <note>original: "Oebalie," an ancient name for the region of Laconia or Sparta</note>, by Leda; she was the sister of Castor and Pollux, and of Helen. As a maiden, she married Agamemnon, King of the Mycenaeans. Though she was illustrious enough by birth and by her marriage, she became more famous through a wicked daring.

For while her husband Agamemnon was commanding the Greek forces at Troy—after she had already borne him many children—she fell into lust for a lazy and idle young man named Aegisthus <note>original: "egisti," the son of Thyestes</note>, the son of Thyestes and Pelopia (who, because of a priesthood, abstained from arms). And—as some believe—she came into his embrace and bed through the persuasions of the old man Nauplius, who was the father of the late Palamedes <note>Nauplius sought revenge against the Greek leaders because they had unjustly executed his son Palamedes during the Trojan War</note>.

From this crime it followed that—whether out of fear of Agamemnon’s return because of the deed she had committed, or by the persuasion of her lover and a desire for the kingdom, or from a weave of indignation regarding Cassandra (whom Agamemnon was bringing back to Mycenae)—the spirited woman, with an armed mind and through deceits, rose up against her husband with a reckless daring. She received him into the palace with feigned joy on her face as he returned victorious from Ilium <note>Troy</note> and weary from the storms of the sea. And as some would have it, she ordered him to be struck down by the adulterer emerging from an ambush while he was dining and perhaps already soaked with wine.

Others, however, say that while he was reclining, the adulterous wife, acting peaceful, persuaded him—who was wrapped in the garments of victory as if it were to be a Greek festival—to put on his ancestral robes, which she herself had prepared beforehand for this purpose. She boldly offered him a garment that lacked an opening for the head. And when the man had already thrust his arms into the sleeves and was searching, wrapped up, for where he might put out his head, being half-bound, he was surrendered to the adulterous assassin at the latter's prompting. And thus he was struck while no one saw him.

After this deed, she seized the entire kingdom and ruled with the adulterer Aegisthus for seven years. Indeed, in the meantime, Orestes—Agamemnon’s son by her—had grown up. His friends had secretly saved him from his mother’s fury, and when the time was right for avenging his father's murder, he killed her along with the adulterer. I do not know which is more a wonder: the crime or the audacity. First, that the illustrious man had not feared such a heavy evil; second... <unclear>how much</unclear>

It was fitting for a treacherous woman, and I hold it all the more abominable; however, I have something to praise in the honorable: namely, that virtue which, though it endured for a long time, did not allow itself to be held back by piety toward an incestuous mother. Instead, the son rushed in as a spirited avenger for the undeserved death of his father. He acted against an ill-deserving mother because of what his less-deserving father had suffered at the hands of an adulterous priest <note>Aegisthus, who in some traditions held a priestly office, was Clytemnestra’s lover and Agamemnon’s murderer</note> under the command of an incestuous woman. Thus, he turned the crime back upon its authors, so that the paternal blood which had been shed by their command and help might be atoned for by the shedding of their own blood.

->### About Helen, Wife of King Menelaus<-

**Helen** was a woman most famous throughout the whole world, both for her well-known wantonness <margin>b</margin> (as it seemed to many) and for the long war that followed from it. She was the daughter of Tyndareus, King of Oebalia <note>An ancient name for the region of Laconia or Sparta</note>, and of Leda, a most beautiful woman; she was also the wife of Menelaus, King of the Lacedemonians <note>The Spartans</note>. Her beauty was so celebrated (as all the ancient Greeks and the Latins after them say) that she is easily preferred above all others. Indeed, she exhausted—to say nothing of others—the man of divine genius, Homer, before he could sufficiently and appropriately describe her in verse according to the rules of his craft.

Moreover, many excellent painters and sculptors all took up the same labor so that they might leave to posterity at least a likeness of such extraordinary beauty. Among these, Zeuxis of Heraclea—the most famous painter of that age and superior to all others—was hired by the people of Croton <note>A Greek city in Southern Italy</note> for a vast sum of money. He devoted all his talent and the powers of his art to depicting her with his brush <note>original: "pinniculo," literally a small feather or brush</note>.

And since, besides Homer's poem and her great fame everywhere, he had no other model by which he could mentally conceive of her face and the rest of her physical stature, he devised a plan. He believed he could first perceive that divine image of Helen through other beautiful forms, show the design to those who requested it, and leave it to posterity once it was shown. First, he chose the most beautiful boys, and then their sisters; from these, he selected five who were particularly remarkable for their beauty. By gathering a single form from the combined beauty of them all, and exerting all the refined strength of his famous genius, it is scarcely believed that he was able to fully perfect through art what he desired. Nor do I wonder that...

For no painting or statue, whether by brush or pigment, could ever capture the joy within her eyes, the peaceful charm of her whole expression, her heavenly smile, or the varied and graceful movements of her face that shifted according to her words and actions—for such things are the work of nature alone. ...nor did she value [her reputation] highly, provided she obtained something she desired. She was noted for preserving her modesty <note>original: "pudiciam." Here, the author suggests she maintained an outward appearance of virtue while acting otherwise.</note> unharmed only in name. In this way, by her allurements and ornate speech, she not only drew many of those who approached her into her enticements, but even drove others into robbery and piracy. She incited several others, having cast aside all honor, to conduct business and trade through deceit. Many more, out of their singular affection for her, she lifted up into pride. Thus, those in whom human reason was seen to be stripped away by the works of this ill-fated woman were rightly believed to have been turned by her into the beasts of their own crimes. From these things, we can sufficiently understand—by observing the morals of men and women—that there are "Circes" everywhere, and far more men who, by their own decadence and crime, have been turned into beasts. Ulysses, however, being forewarned by the counsel of Mercury <note>The god Mercury represents reason or eloquence that protects the mind from base desires.</note>, clearly represents a prudent man whom flatterers are unable to ensnare. Furthermore, by his own teachings, he often freed those already ensnared from their bonds. The rest of the story clearly pertains to history, established by the fact that Ulysses remained with Circe for some time. Moreover, it is said that this same woman was the wife of Picus <note>original: "pici." Picus was an early Italian king often associated with the woodpecker.</note>, the son of Saturn and King of the Latins, and that she taught him the science of augury <note>The art of interpreting omens from the flight and song of birds.</note>. Out of jealousy, because he loved the nymph Pomona, she was said to have transformed him into the bird of his own name. For he kept a tame woodpecker <note>original: "picus."</note> as a domestic bird, from whose song and movements he took omens regarding the future; and because he led his life according to the actions of the woodpecker, he was said to have been turned into one. As for when, by what kind of death, or where this Circe died, I have no certain record.

From her very day of birth, this maiden’s fortune was severe. For a short time after the mother's funeral, Metabus was driven from his kingdom by a sudden rebellion of the citizens. Alone and on foot, the wretched man <note>This refers to Metabus, who was fleeing into exile after being overthrown by his subjects.</note> fled, and carrying his companion Camilla in his arms, he reached the Amasenus river <note>original: "Emasenum fluuium." Since he was hindered by the burden of the infant girl and could not swim across, he arrived at a timely plan by the grace of God <note>original: "porrigente deo." While the author uses the singular "God," the classical source refers to divine inspiration from the gods or fate.</note>, who did not wish for this maiden—destined for future fame—to be consumed by an ignoble fate. 

Therefore, having wrapped her in the bark of a cork tree <note>original: "suberis cortice." This is a famous detail from Virgil’s Aeneid, where the cork bark acts as a primitive float or protective casing.</note>, he bound her to a spear which he happened to be carrying. He then vowed her to the goddess Diana, promising she would serve the goddess if she were kept safe. With his arm swung with all his strength, he hurled the spear with his daughter attached to the opposite bank, which he immediately followed by swimming across. 

When he found her unharmed by the gift of the deity, joyful in his misery, he sought the hiding places of the woods. There, not without great labor, he raised the little girl on the milk of wild beasts. When she had reached a stronger age, she began to cover her body with the skins of wild animals and to brandish weapons with her arms; she learned to swing slings <note>original: "fidas," likely a transcription for "fundas" (slings).</note>, to bend the bow, and to carry quivers. She would pursue stags and wild goats in the chase and, surpassing them, she came to look down upon all traditional feminine labors. Above all else, she preserved her virginity inviolate.

Far be it from me to believe such a thing <note>original: "quod absit vt credam"</note> about a woman so celebrated for her chastity in the writings of so many authors; many others, asserting the contrary, maintain that Penelope remained nothing but the most chaste of women. Indeed, her virtue is all the more brilliant and commendable for being so rare; she persevered with unshaken constancy despite being driven by so great a struggle.

# Concerning Lavinia, Queen of the Laurentians

Lavinia, queen of the Laurentians <note>The Laurentians were an ancient Italian people; their capital, Laurentum, was a city in Latium, south of the Tiber.</note>, tracing her lineage back to Saturn of Crete <note>In Roman myth, Saturn (Cronus) fled to Italy after his defeat by Jupiter and brought a "Golden Age" to the region.</note>, was the only daughter of King Latinus and his beloved wife <note>Amata, whose name literally means "beloved."</note>. She eventually became the wife of Aeneas, the most valiant leader of the Trojans. She is famous more as the cause of the war between Aeneas and Turnus the Rutulian <note>Turnus was the leader of the Rutuli and Lavinia's original suitor; his war against Aeneas forms the second half of Virgil’s *Aeneid*.</note> than for any other...

He is the namesake of the Julian clan of Rome.</note> was believed to be less than kind toward his stepmother, and since he had founded for himself the city of Alba Longa, he voluntarily withdrew and left his father’s kingdom to Lavinia. 

Lavinia, bearing the ancient nobility of her heart with honor and living modestly, held the kingdom with the greatest diligence. She preserved it until such a time as she could resign it, entirely undiminished, to her son when he reached the age of puberty. Some, however, claim that she was recalled from the woods and married a certain Melampus, and that Silvius was raised by Ascanius with brotherly kindness.

# Concerning Dido, or Elissa, the Tyrian Queen of the Carthaginians

Dido (whose name was previously Elissa) founded Carthage and was its queen. It is my pleasure to enter into the true praises of this woman, widening the margins of my speech a little <note>The author uses a metaphor of "expanding the fringes" of a garment to suggest he will speak at greater length and with more ornament.</note>, if perhaps by these few small letters of mine I might be able, at least in part, to wipe away the stain unworthily cast upon the beauty of her widowhood <note>The "stain" refers to the famous story in Virgil’s *Aeneid* where Dido commits suicide due to her passion for Aeneas. Many historical writers, including Boccaccio, argued that the real Dido was a model of chaste widowhood who died to remain faithful to her first husband, Acerbas.</note>. 

And so that I may take up her glory more deeply for a moment: the Phoenicians (as is well enough known) were a people most famous for their industry, hailing from the very borders of Egypt...

--- Page 61 ---
<meta>This page continues the biography of Dido, shifting from the author's introductory defense of her character to the historical narrative of her flight from Tyre after the murder of her husband, Sychaeus, by her brother Pygmalion.</meta>

Elissa <note>The OCR reads "Elpissa," a common scribal or transcription error for Elissa, Dido's original Phoenician name.</note> took his death so hard that she could scarcely restrain herself from ending her own life. Indeed, after she had spent a great deal of time in tears, repeatedly calling out in vain to her most beloved husband <note>Dido’s husband was Sychaeus, a wealthy priest of Hercules in Tyre.</note> and casting bitter and dire curses upon her brother, she deliberated on taking flight—whether she was warned in her sleep (as some believe) or by her own mind's counsel—lest she herself might also be brought to her death by her brother’s greed. 

Then, setting aside feminine softness and strengthening her spirit with a manly vigor—from which she later earned the name **Dido**, which in the Phoenician tongue means the same as **Virago** <note>original: "virago." In a medieval context, a "virago" was a woman who possessed "manly" virtues like courage and strength; it was a term of high praise for a female leader.</note> in Latin—she first won over to her cause several of the leading men of the city whom she knew hated Pygmalion for various reasons. She then seized her brother’s fleet, which had been prepared either for transport or some other purpose, and ordered it to be quickly filled with her companions from the shipyards. 

Under the cover of night, she took all the treasures she knew were her husband’s, along with whatever she could steal from her brother, and had them secretly placed upon the ships. Then, by a clever trick, she loaded many bundles filled with sand, pretending they were the treasures, and did this while everyone was watching. When they reached the deep sea, she ordered the bundles to be thrown into the water while the ignorant onlookers marveled. With many tears, she swore that the death she had long desired had finally come to her through the bitter sinking of her treasures. 

She then claimed to pity her companions, whom she said she did not doubt would be butchered with dire punishments by that most greedy and fierce king along with her, if they were to return to Pygmalion. Indeed, she asserted that if they were willing to take flight with her, she would not be lacking in providing for them and their needs. Hearing this, the wretched sailors—though they left their native soil and ancestral homes with heavy hearts—easily came to an agreement to go into exile, terrified by the fear of a cruel death.

With the prows turned and with her as their leader, they came to **Cyprus**. There, she seized several maidens who were performing offerings to Venus on the shore according to their custom, intended to provide comfort for the young men and for the procreation of children. She also took as a companion for her pilgrimage the priest of Jupiter, along with his entire family, who had been forewarned of her coming and who prophesied that great things would follow this flight. 

And now, with **Crete** left behind and **Sicily** on the right, she turned her course toward **Africa** <note>original: "affrū," referring to the Roman province of Africa (modern Tunisia).</note> and, skimming the coast of the Massylii <note>A Berber confederacy in eastern Numidia.</note>, she entered a bay. It is well enough known where she eventually decided to give a little rest to those weary from rowing, having judged it a safe harbor for the ships. There, as the neighbors arrived out of a desire to see the foreigners...

<meta>This page continues the story of Dido’s arrival in North Africa and the founding of Carthage. It describes her famous "ox-hide" trick to secure land and the beginning of the conflict with the local King Iarbas (here called the King of the Musitani).</meta>

and while others were bringing provisions and merchandise, as is the custom, conversations and friendships began to be formed. And since it appeared pleasing to the inhabitants that they should remain there and make use of the resources that had traveled with them from Tyre, she persuaded the envoy <note>The local representative or leader of the indigenous inhabitants.</note> that a settlement should be granted immediately. Even though she heard her brother was threatening war, she was terrified by no fear; she took care not to seem to inflict any injury, and so that no one would suspect she was about to do anything great, she purchased from the inhabitants of that land a site for her seat on the shore, no larger than what could be covered by the hide of an ox.

O, the cunning of the woman! By her command, the ox hide was cut into tiny strips; when these were joined together, she encompassed a much larger space than the sellers could have imagined. Under the omen of a horse's head <note>According to legend, the Phoenicians dug up a horse's head on the site, which was interpreted as a sign that the city would be powerful in war and wealthy.</note>, she founded a warlike city which she named **Carthage**, and its citadel she named **Byrsa**, after the hide of the ox. <note>original: "birsam." The author explains the name through the Greek word *byrsa*, meaning "tanned hide" or "skin," referencing the clever land deal.</note>

And when she had revealed the treasures she had hidden through her deception and encouraged her companions with the great hope of escape, walls, temples, a forum, and both public and private buildings immediately arose. She herself gave the people laws and a standard of living. When the city had suddenly become excellent, the celebrated fame of her beauty, her unseen and unheard-of virtue, and her chastity was carried throughout all Africa.

Therefore, since the African people are most prone to passion, it happened that the King of the Musitani <note>This refers to Iarbas, the king of the local tribes (often identified as the Maxitani or Gaetulians).</note> began to lust for her. Through certain leading men of the city, he demanded her in marriage, threatening war and the destruction of the rising city if she were not given to him. When these men came to the widowed queen and saw her tears and her inflexible commitment to chastity, they feared greatly for themselves, lest they be swallowed up in war if the suitor's desire were frustrated. Not daring to explain to Dido what was being asked when she questioned them, they thought to deceive the queen with words and lead her toward the desired result by their own plan.

They told her the King desired that, through their instruction, his wild barbarism might be refined into more civilized ways, and for this reason, under threat of war, he demanded teachers from among them. However, they claimed they were uncertain who among them would be willing to take up such a heavy burden—to leave their fatherland and go to live with such a savage king. The queen did not perceive the trick; on the contrary, she cried out to them: "What laziness is this, O excellent citizens? What baseness? Are you ignorant that we are born first for our country? Nor can he rightly be called a citizen who..."

I think some woman will say—for our women are most perceptive when it comes to finding excuses <note>original: "cum perspicacissime ad excusationes nostre sint femine." The author is taking a sharp, didactic tone common in late medieval moral treatises.</note>—"It had to be done this way: she was destitute; her parents and brothers had gone to their deaths; suitors were pressing her with flatteries. I was unable to resist. I am made of flesh, not iron." 

O, how ridiculous! I ask: in whose support did she trust? Was her only brother not an enemy while she was an exile? Were there not many suitors for Dido? Furthermore, Dido herself was not made of stone or wood, any more than women are today. No, indeed; and so, being strong at least in her spirit—since she did not believe she could escape these allurements by her own physical strength—she escaped by dying, taking the only path she could. 

But for us, who say we are so forsaken, is not Christ our refuge? Truly, the pious Redeemer is always present for those who hope in Him. --- Page 65 ---
<meta>This page continues the author’s moralizing comparison between the Carthaginian queen Dido and Christian widows. Having previously established that Dido chose death over breaking her vow of chastity to her late husband, the author now addresses the excuses Christian women use to justify remarriage, ultimately returning to the narrative of Dido’s funeral.</meta>

If she had commanded her own unbridled desires, all the things mentioned before would have been negated by a single refusal. Dido was able to die so that she would not live as an unchaste woman. In order to live honestly, she could not simply refuse the marriage <note>Dido was being pressured to marry the African King Iarbas; she chose suicide to remain faithful to her deceased husband, Sychaeus.</note>.

At the judgment, one woman, craftier than the others, will step forward and say: "I was young. Youth burns (as you know); I could not contain myself." Quoting the Teacher of the Gentiles <note>original: "doctoris gencium." This is a standard medieval title for Saint Paul.</note>, she says: "It is better to marry than to burn" <note>A reference to 1 Corinthians 7:9. The author argues that women use this biblical permission as an excuse for lack of self-control.</note>, following his counsel. Oh, how well said! As if I were commanding chastity only of the elderly; or as if Dido was not a young woman when she strengthened her spirit toward chastity. 

O, what a wicked deed! That holy counsel was not given by Paul so that it might be so often and so foully twisted in defense of a sin. We can restore exhausted strength gradually with food, yet do we claim we cannot diminish superfluous desires through abstinence? A pagan woman could rule over her passion for the sake of empty glory and impose laws upon herself; yet a Christian woman, in order to obtain eternal glory, cannot rule over her own? 

Woe is me! While we think we are deceiving God with such excuses, we withdraw ourselves from eternal honor for the sake of a fleeting one, and we hurl ourselves into the abyss of eternal damnation. Let them blush, therefore, looking upon the lifeless corpse of Dido; and while they contemplate the cause of her death, let them cast down their faces in sorrow, because they, as followers of Christ, are surpassed in modesty by a "member of the devil" <note>original: "membro dyaboli." In the medieval worldview, virtuous pagans were still considered part of the devil's body because they were unbaptized, making their superior virtue even more shameful for Christians.</note>.

Let them not think that because they wipe away tears and have put on mourning clothes for the deceased, they have fulfilled all their duties. Love must be preserved until the very end, if they wish to fulfill the office of widowhood. Nor should they think it right to move on to subsequent vows (as many often do) simply to satisfy their lust under the feigned name of marriage, rather than obeying the sacred union so that they might be free from the stain of unchastity. For what else is it to demand the embraces of so many men and to seek so many encounters, than to follow in the footsteps of Valeria Messalina <note>Valeria Messalina (c. 17–48 AD) was a Roman empress notorious in historical literature for her alleged promiscuity and many lovers.</note> and enter brothels and vaults? 

But enough of this for now. I confess, however, that I have excessively overstepped the bounds of the work I began. But who is so much a master of himself that he is not sometimes carried away by impulse beyond his original purpose? I beg those who read this to forgive me, and let us return to where we diverted. 

The citizens, therefore, with public tears and mourning, carried out Dido’s lifeless body. They performed a magnificent funeral, honoring her not only with human tributes but even with divine ones.

**On Pamphile, Daughter of Platea.**

I find that Pamphile <note>original: "Amphilem" [likely a transcription error for Pamphilem]. Pamphile of Cos is credited by ancient sources like Pliny the Elder with being the first to weave silk.</note> was once a Greek woman, and although antiquity has taken away the knowledge of which country she was from, it has kindly left her father's name. For she is found to have been the daughter of a certain Platea. And even if she cannot be decorated with the grandest titles <note>This implies she was of common birth or did not hold political power.</note>, since she added something of good to the commonwealth, she ought not to be cheated of her portion of praise by silence. For nothing new—however easy it may seem after it is done—is discovered by anyone that is not evidence of a keen intellect and, according to the quality of the thing, should sometimes be counted as glory.

Authors (to whom some credit is due) hold that she was the first to collect the downy <term>silk-worm</term> <note>original: "bombicem." The text refers to the cocoons of the *Bombyx mori* or a similar species found in the wild.</note> cocoons from the little trees, and to have cleaned them of waste with a <term>comb</term> <note>original: "pectine." A tool used to align fibers before spinning.</note>, and having cleaned them, to have placed them on the <term>distaff</term> <note>original: "colo." A tool used in spinning to hold the unspun fibers.</note>, and even to have drawn thread from them, and then to have taught how to weave. And thus she introduced the use of silk, known even to this day. The method she devised easily shows how much strength of mind Pamphile must have possessed in conducting other affairs.

<meta>This page transitions from the moralizing conclusion of Rhea Ilia's life to the biographies of two distinct women of antiquity: Gaya Cyrilla, a model of domestic Roman virtue, and Sappho, the Greek lyric poet who broke the gendered expectations of her era.</meta>

# XLIV. Concerning Gaya Cyrilla
->The Wife of King Tarquinius<-

**Gaya Cyrilla**, the wife of the Roman king **Tarquinius Priscus**, was a woman of ancient and singular virtue. <note>Tarquinius Priscus was the legendary fifth king of Rome, reigning in the 6th century BC. Gaya Cyrilla, also known as Tanaquil, became the archetype of the virtuous Roman matron.</note> She was so celebrated for her modesty and her mastery of the womanly art of wool-working that she became a permanent symbol in the Roman marriage ceremony. Even many centuries after her death, it remained the custom for a new bride, upon first entering her husband’s home, to be asked her name and to respond: "Where you are **Gaius**, I am **Gaya**," <note>original: "Ubi tu Gaius, ego Gaya." This legal formula invoked the name of Cyrilla to signify that the bride would be the mistress of the household just as the groom was the master.</note> honoring the memory of this industrious queen who was never found idle.

# XLV. Concerning Sappho, the Girl of Lesbos and Poet
->The Poetess of the Lyre<-

**Sappho**, a girl from the island of **Lesbos**, possessed a spirit of such nobility and fire that she was not content with the common tasks of women. Through her own genius and persistent study, she dared to ascend the steep and laurel-shaded peaks of **Mount Parnassus** <note>original: "parnasi." A mountain in central Greece sacred to the god Apollo and the Muses; it represents the highest pinnacle of poetic and intellectual achievement.</note>. 

In an age when women were expected to remain silent, she joined the company of the **Muses** <note>The nine sister goddesses who presided over song, poetry, and the arts.</note>. Having taken up the plectrum <term>plectrum: a small pick or tool used to strike the strings of a musical instrument.</term>, she struck <note>original: "pulsa."</note> the strings of the lyre in the manner of <note>original: "vice."</note> the great masters of old. Through her dedication to these sacred <note>original: "sacris."</note> arts, she composed verses of such sweetness and technical brilliance that she was called the "Tenth Muse." Although she was a woman, her name was inscribed among the greatest poets <note>original: "vates." A term implying both a poet and a divinely inspired prophet.</note>, proving that the heights of intellect and fame are accessible to anyone through labor and the favor of the gods.

<summary>This page profiles two exemplary women: Gaya Cyrilla, the Roman ideal of domestic industry and marital harmony, and Sappho, the Greek poet who achieved immortal fame through her mastery of the lyre and the arts of Parnassus.</summary>
<keywords>Gaya Cyrilla, Sappho, Tarquinius Priscus, Parnassus, Muses, Roman marriage, lyric poetry, wool-working, virtue, Lesbos</keywords>

...and found it suited to her. Groaning against the stubborn coldness of her beloved, <note>original: "in eius obstinatam duritiem." This refers to Phaon, a ferryman of Lesbos with whom Sappho was legendarily infatuated, but who did not return her love.</note> they say she sang mournful verses. I would have thought these were elegies—given that such a poetic form is dedicated to mourning—had I not read that she spurned the traditional forms of song and invented a new genre. This style moves with "feet" <note>In poetry, "feet" refers to the rhythmic units or meter of a line.</note> different from all others and is still called **Sapphic** after her name. But why should we blame the **Muses**? <note>original: "pyerides." Another name for the Muses, the goddesses of inspiration.</note> For they, when **Amphion** touched his lyre, were able to move the very stones of **Thebes**, <note>original: "ogygia saxa." In Greek mythology, Amphion played his lyre so beautifully that the stones moved of their own accord to form the city walls of Thebes (Ogygia).</note> yet they were unwilling to soften the heart of a young man while Sappho sang.

# XLVI. Concerning Lucretia, the Wife of Collatinus.

**Lucretia**, the preeminent leader of Roman modesty and a most sacred honor to her ancient lineage, was the daughter of **Spurius Lucretius Tricipitinus**, a man of great distinction among the Romans. Tarquinius Collatinus was one of the first two consuls of the Roman Republic in 509 BC.</note> It remains uncertain whether she was considered more beautiful among the Roman matrons for the loveliness of her face or for the integrity of her character. Since she, because of her faithfulness...

<meta>This page continues the story of Lucretia, beginning with the arrival of Sextus Tarquinius at her home and concluding with her death and the resulting liberation of Rome.</meta>

...she was seen to be. **Collatinus**, however, kindly received the other young men into his home, where they were treated with honor. **Sextus**, the son of **Tarquin the Proud**, <note>original: "superbi filius." Sextus Tarquinius was the son of the seventh and final king of Rome, Tarquinius Superbus.</note> cast unchaste eyes upon the dignity and beauty of this virtuous woman. Inflamed by an unholy fire, he secretly resolved to overcome her loveliness by force (if it were not otherwise granted to him). Not many days later, driven by his madness, he secretly left the military camp and came at night to **Collatia**, where he was courteously received and honored by **Lucretia** because he was a kinsman of her husband.

After he perceived the whole house was silent and judging everyone to be asleep, he followed his plan and entered Lucretia’s bedroom with a sword. He revealed who he was and threatened her with death if she made a sound or did not yield to his desire. When he saw she was resisting his lust and was unafraid of death, he turned to a wicked trick, saying that he would kill one of his slaves and place him beside her, and then tell everyone he had killed her because she had been caught in the act of adultery with a servant.

Hearing this, the woman trembled, terrified by such a foul disgrace. Fearing that if she were killed under those terms, there would be no one to clear her innocence, she permitted her body to the adulterer, though her soul loathed him. When he had satisfied his enticing lust and departed, victorious in his own judgment, Lucretia—heartsick at such a monstrous <note>original: "celesti." While this literally translates to "heavenly," in this context it likely implies an act of extraordinary or "monstrous" proportions, or may be an error for "scelesti" (wicked).</note> crime—at daybreak ordered her father **Tricipitinus**, her husband, and **Brutus**, a kinsman of Collatinus who until then was considered witless <note>original: "amantem," likely a transcription error for "amentem" (mindless/witless). Lucius Junius Brutus famously feigned stupidity to avoid being executed by the King.</note>, and other relatives to be summoned immediately. 

When they arrived, she related with tears and in order what Sextus had done to her in the dead of night. And as her relatives miserably tried to console her while she wept, she drew out a dagger she had hidden beneath her clothes and said: "Even if I absolve myself of the sin, I do not free myself from the punishment; nor shall any unchaste woman live hereafter by the example of Lucretia." Having said these words, she plunged the blade into her innocent breast and, falling upon the wound, she collapsed dying before the eyes of her husband and father; soon after, her soul poured out with her blood. 

Truly her beauty was unfortunate, yet her never-sufficiently-praised modesty is to be extolled with worthy proclamations all the more brightly as the shame inflicted by force was more sharply expiated. For from this act, not only was her honor restored—which the foulness of the young man’s incestuous crime had shaken—but Roman liberty followed. <note>The death of Lucretia sparked a revolution that overthrew the Roman monarchy and led to the establishment of the Roman Republic.</note>

->## About Tomyris, Queen of the Scythians ##<-

**Tomyris** <note>original: "Thamiris." Tomyris was the legendary queen of the Massagetae, a Scythian people of Central Asia, famous for defeating Cyrus the Great.</note> was an illustrious queen of the **Scythians**. Although the Scythians live upon barren soil and in a freezing region of the sky, connected to the **Riphean** and **Hyperborean** mountains <note>In ancient geography, these were legendary mountains in the far north, believed to be the source of cold winds.</note> and are therefore known mostly only to themselves, her origins remain obscure. From which parents **Tomyris** was born or to whom she was joined in marriage is unknown. For this reason, she is notable and recognized for the distinction of her nobility primarily because she commanded wild and untamed peoples.

When **Cyrus** <note>original: "Cyrio." Cyrus the Great, founder of the Achaemenid Persian Empire.</note> already held the kingdoms of Asia, he perhaps desired to be more widely known, and thus he fell into a longing for the Scythian kingdom—more to exalt his own glory than to increase his empire. He had heard, of course, that the Scythians were poor and forest-dwelling people, but that they had remained unconquered by the greatest kings until his own time. Drawn by this greed, he led an army against Queen **Tomyris**.

When **Tomyris** learned of his arrival (even though he was formidable to all of Asia and nearly the whole world due to the scale of his deeds), she did not, as a terrified woman might, seek out hiding places or request terms of peace through a herald. On the contrary, having gathered her forces and become a leader of war, she allowed him to cross the **Araxes** <note>A major river in the Caucasus/Central Asian region.</note> with his entire army and enter her borders, even though she could have resisted him with naval works. The wise woman judged that **Cyrus’s** madness could be defeated far better within her own borders than outside them.

And I believe, after he had penetrated into the interior of the kingdom, she granted her only son, a young man, a third part of her forces and ordered him to go out to meet **Cyrus** in battle. But **Cyrus**, considering both the nature of the locations and the habits of the people he had heard about, sensed the young man coming with his army and decided to conquer him more by trickery than by arms. He feigned flight, leaving behind a camp filled with wine (which the Scythians did not yet know), as well as feasts and other delicacies. When the young man entered the empty camp as if he were the victor and the enemy had been put to flight, he and the Scythians—joyful as if invited to a feast rather than a war—began to gorge themselves on ample food and unknown drinks. Consequently, military discipline dissolved and sleep followed. Overwhelming them while they were buried in sleep, **Cyrus** put him and the others to death, and as if certain...

<summary>This page introduces Tomyris, Queen of the Scythians, and details the beginning of her conflict with Cyrus the Great, who used a deceptive feast of wine to ambush her son's army.</summary>
<keywords>Tomyris, Scythians, Cyrus the Great, Persian Empire, Araxes River, military strategy, trickery, Spargapises</keywords>

--- Page 73 ---
<meta>This page continues the narrative of Tomyris, the Scythian queen, focusing on her decisive counter-attack against Cyrus the Great and the subsequent introduction of Leaena, a Greek courtesan noted for her fortitude.</meta>

...proceeded further in his victory. However, when **Tomyris** <note>original: "Thamiris."</note> heard of the slaughter of her people, even though she was greatly moved as a widow by the death of her only son, she did not give herself over to tears in the usual feminine manner. Instead, once she had calmed her grief with anger and a desire for revenge, she turned to her remaining forces. She reckoned that the enemy—shrewd as he was—could be captured by the same trick by which she had heard her son was taken: by leaving behind a camp filled with drinking cups.

Being familiar with the terrain, she feigned a retreat and led her greedy pursuer a short distance into the barren and frost-chilled mountains. Having drawn them into the rugged mountain passes where they lacked all resources, she turned upon them and destroyed nearly the entire army. Not even **Cyrus** himself escaped; he died a bloody death to satisfy the widow’s rage. 

With a hardened heart, **Tomyris** ordered that Cyrus's body be sought out among the corpses. When it was found, she commanded that his head be cut off and plunged into a wineskin <note>original: "utrem." A leather container often used for wine or water.</note> filled with the blood of his own people. As if providing a fitting tomb for the arrogant king, she said: "Satiate yourself with the blood for which you thirsted." <note>original: "saciare sanguine quem sitti." According to legend, Tomyris decapitated Cyrus to mock his bloodlust and imperial greed.</note> But we have no other records concerning **Tomyris** beyond this deed, which is all the more famous because the empire of **Cyrus** had been so great.

## Concerning Leaena the Courtesan ##

I believe **Leaena** <note>original: "Leenam." Leaena was a famous Athenian hetaira (courtesan) associated with the tyrannicides Harmodius and Aristogeiton.</note> was a Greek woman. Although she was less than chaste, I would nevertheless like to describe her among famous women, by the leave of honorable matrons and illustrious queens. For (as was said previously), I promised to include "famous" women based on any notable deed, not only those who were chaste. Furthermore, we are so indebted to virtue that we should not only elevate those whom we see established in a position of distinction, but we must also strive to bring into the light any virtue that deserves it, even if it is buried under a shameful covering. 

For virtue is precious everywhere, and it is no more defiled by the contagion of crimes than a sunbeam is polluted when it touches the mud below. Therefore, if we ever see virtue instilled in a heart devoted to a detestable profession, we ought to loathe the profession in such a way that the praise due to the virtue is not diminished; indeed, virtue is all the more wonderful and worthy in such a person, the more distant it was thought to be from them.

<summary>Tomyris avenges her son by trapping and killing Cyrus the Great, famously mocking his severed head. The author then introduces Leaena, arguing that virtue is noteworthy even when found in a woman of ill-repute.</summary>
<keywords>Tomyris, Cyrus the Great, Scythians, revenge, Leaena, virtue vs. reputation, Greek history</keywords>

--- Page 74 ---
<meta>This page continues the biographies of notable women, moving from the warrior-queen Tomyris to the Athenian courtesan Leaena, whose fortitude is presented as a rebuke to the "slothful" noble classes.</meta>

<note>A metal clip is attached to the left edge of the page.</note>

For this reason, the memory of courtesans should not always be despised; rather, when they have made themselves worthy of remembrance through some merit of virtue, they should be extolled with broader praise for readers. For in such women, a proven virtue acts to strike a blush of shame into wanton kingdoms, since the sloth of those realms provides an excuse for their own slippery luxuries <note>The author argues that the moral strength of a lowly woman shames the corruption of "great" kingdoms.</note>. Furthermore, so it may appear that great spirits are not always joined only to illustrious titles, and that virtue disclaims no one who is willing to follow her, **Leaena** <note>original: "leena." Her name means "Lioness" in Greek, which the author plays upon later in the text.</note> must be added to such a celebrated assembly of women, so that even in that role where she acted so vigorously, she may be praised as one who served well.

Therefore, **Leaena**, though devoted to a shameful trade of prostitution through a detestable service, caused her origins and homeland to be unknown. Nevertheless, while **Amyntas** was reigning among the Macedonians <note>The author identifies the era by the Macedonian king Amyntas I, though the event took place in Athens.</note>, and when **Harmodius** and **Aristogeiton** <note>original: "Armormis ꝛ Ariston." These were the famous "tyrannicides" of Athens who plotted to overthrow the sons of Peisistratus.</note>—excellent young men—had killed the cruel tyrant **Hipparchus** (whether to wipe out the shame of a homeland subjected to foul tyranny or driven by some other cause), she was captured among others as one conscious of their deeds, because she was an associate of theirs.

And when she was forced by dire tortures to betray her fellow-conspirators, this woman of "slippery" reputation considered within herself how holy and venerable the name of friendship is. With pious consideration, and wishing not to inflict any violence upon that bond just to spare herself, she first commanded her own mind with wondrous constancy for a long time so that she would not say what was being sought. Finally, as the tortures intensified and her physical strength failed, this manly woman <note>original: "virilis femina." A standard historical praise for a woman who shows courage typically associated with men.</note>—fearing that if her bodily strength were weakened, her resolve might also be enervated—exceeded her purpose with even greater strength. She acted so that the power of speech would be taken away from her just as her physical strength was, and with a sharp bite, she cut off her own tongue and spat it out.

By this single but most illustrious act, she took away from the torturers all hope of learning from her what they required. Who could say that **Leaena** inhabited the brothels except by some crime of Fortune? Certainly, he did not know her who once said that women only keep silent about what they do not know! Alas, sometimes the wanton wealth of a household and the excessive indulgence of parents lead young women onto slippery paths. Their wayward ease, unless restrained by austere bridles and held by the most vigilant care of mortals, sometimes slips even without being pushed; and once fallen, the grace of their former honor and dignity is trampled by despair.

<summary>This page describes the heroism of the Athenian courtesan Leaena, who bit off her own tongue to avoid betraying the conspirators Harmodius and Aristogeiton under torture.</summary>

...finally cannot be recalled by any force. # Concerning Athaliah, Queen of Jerusalem

**Athaliah’s** savage mind made her a "stepmother" <note>The Latin "noverca" (stepmother) carries the historical connotation of being cruel, plotting, or hostile to the legitimate heirs of a household.</note> to the Syrians and Egyptians; however fortunate she might have been for the line of David, let it be said that her house was everywhere defiled by the blood of her own kin and by manifold slaughter. She added no less ill-omened fame to her name than the brilliance found in the crowns of kings. 

She was, therefore, the daughter of **Ahab**, King of the Israelites, and of **Jezebel**, that most wicked woman of a Queen. She was married to **Joram** <note>Jehoram of Judah.</note>, the son of **Jehoshaphat**, King of Jerusalem. Eventually, after Jehoshaphat and **Ahaziah** <note>original: "Ozia." This refers to Ahaziah of Judah, the eldest son.</note>—to whom the succession of the kingdom had fallen upon the death of his father—were removed from the midst, her husband **Joram** was crowned King of Jerusalem beyond all expectation. He also wished this wife of his to be Queen. To this splendor—her father **Ahab** having died—her brother **Joram** <note>Joram of Israel, Athaliah's brother, who succeeded their father Ahab.</note>, having succeeded to his father's throne, added no small light. 

Indeed, in the course of time, after being shaken by many prior misfortunes, her husband died, and she saw her son **Ahaziah** <note>original: "Ottoziam."</note> sitting on his father's throne, so that the woman shone on all sides with royal honors. Truly...
