<meta>This page continues the narrative of Athaliah’s bloody rise to power in Jerusalem. The previous page ended with the death of King Ahaziah, which prompted Athaliah to attempt to exterminate the entire Davidic royal line to secure her own rule.</meta>

...had carried the small boy <note>Joash, the infant son of the late King Ahaziah.</note>, whom she had snatched away, into the house of the high priest Jehoiada, her husband, so that he might be kept safe and nourished. And so, through the blood of so many who were impiously slain, this bold woman dared to ascend the royal throne as if into a possession made vacant by her own handiwork, and she began to manage all the affairs of the realm. 

Why should we marvel at **Atreus**, **Dionysius**, or **Jugurtha**? <note>original: "atreum... dyonisiū... iugurtā." The author compares Athaliah to famous male tyrants: Atreus of Greek myth who killed his nephews; Dionysius, the harsh tyrant of Syracuse; and Jugurtha, the King of Numidia who murdered his rivals to take the throne.</note> We hear of these men of sharp intellect who, drawn by a lust for the kingdom, impiously reached the summit of power through the wounding of their own kin. Yet here we behold a woman who, in order to arrive at that same height, stabbed all the royal offspring and did not even spare her own descendants. Therefore, **Athaliah** shone with the royal diadem, though she was indeed more notable for being splattered with purple blood than for any royal insignia <note>The author plays on the word "purple," which was both the color of royalty and the color of fresh blood.</note>. 

Truly, just as she had cruelly raged with the sword against the innocent souls of the line of David, she was equally capable of venting her fury against her own embittered relatives and strangers alike. She could easily have looked upon her brother **Joram** <note>Jehoram of Israel, Athaliah's brother.</note>, the King of Israel, as he lay in the field of **Naboth**, his wounds and blood being licked by dogs. <note>original: "agro nabaoth." Naboth was a vineyard owner executed by Athaliah's parents, Ahab and Jezebel, so they could seize his land. The author references the biblical prophecy that the house of Ahab would perish in that same field.</note> In the same way, she could have seen her mother **Jezebel**, adorned in royal...

--- Page 77 ---
<meta>This page continues the moralizing reflection on the rise of Athaliah. After describing her bloody ascent, the author pivots to a general meditation on the nature of tyranny and the psychological and social cost of seizing power through unholy means.</meta>

...we busy ourselves and entangle our very selves in greater crimes <note>original: "flagicijs." This refers to shameful acts or outrages that trap the soul.</note> while we least expect it, swallowed by a storm when it no longer does any good for us, miserable as we are, to weep for the things we have committed. For the lust for a kingdom beyond what is lawful is a terrible thing; it is a savage occupation into which one rarely enters by chance. It is necessary to ascend either through fraud or through violence. If you are driven by fraud—through deceits, snares, perjuries, betrayals, and the like—it will be managed through constant calculation. If it weighs upon you by violence—through boasts, riots, rumors, cruelty, and madness—then you must have prepared your strength for the greater path you are about to travel. All these things are considered the work of wicked men; unless you become a slave to these deeds, you cannot be the lord of a kingdom.

What is it, at last, to go to the throne? You must harden your ears against complaints. You must turn your eyes away from tears, crimes, and slaughters, and harden your heart into stone. Let cruelty be armed; let piety be shut out. Let reason be neglected; let injustice be cultivated. Let power be taken away from sacred laws; let it serve lust; let malice be summoned; let simplicity be mocked. Greed, luxury, and gluttony <note>original: "ingluuies." Literally "the maw," referring to insatiable excess or devouring hunger.</note> will devour the provinces of the famous kingdom; let it spare neither divine nor human things. Let sacred and profane <note>original: "aphana," a variation of the Latin "profana."</note> things be mixed, and let life, weighed down by the highest wickedness, wade into blood. Let pious men be struck down; let the impious be exalted. Let virgins be ravished and boys be dragged into abuse. Let the virtue of the mind <note>original: "ingenij... virtus." This refers to the moral excellence or the natural talent of a person.</note> be condemned, and let vice be forgiven; and everywhere, let discord celebrate its triumphs without peace. 

O, what a spectacular accession for a king! But why go to the throne through blood and shameful deeds? Would that, however it was sought, one could live thereafter without guilt. But immediately, as suspicion presses, the leading men are driven into exile. The rich are reduced to poverty; old friends are banished. Brothers, sons, grandsons, and parents are imprisoned and killed as if they were rivals. No faith, no holiness, and no justice is preserved. One keeps watch anxiously; one sleeps only with difficulty. Nor is food tasted without fear. Once the faithful have been driven out, the whole of life is surrendered to the wicked. O, what a beautiful, desirable, and praiseworthy possession, sought with such cares! It was indeed easier to have entered a poor man's little hut, which is full of peace, strong in security, and empty of anxiety. These lofty heights, which are sought with blood, are held with such great fear. And we, who have sent away our faithful friends because we suspected them, find ourselves caught in the snares of our own crimes...

<summary>A scathing meditation on the nature of tyranny, describing the moral and psychological degradation required to seize and hold a throne through violence and fraud. It contrasts the miserable, paranoid life of a usurper with the peaceful security of a poor man's cottage.</summary>

...we commit ourselves. Often, by the help of those through whom we made our entry into power, we meet such exits, or even more detestable ones. In a single hour, along with our own destruction, that which had been wickedly gathered over many unlucky days is snatched away—a truth Athaliah realized only when it was too late.

### Concerning Cloelia, a Roman Maiden

**Cloelia** was a distinguished Roman maiden. Whether her ancestors left no record of her origins for her descendants, or the memory was abolished by the passage of time, is uncertain. However, I believe she was born of noble stock, as the nobility <note>original: "generositas," referring to both high birth and a noble character.</note> of her spirit bears witness; for she was given as a hostage for peace, among other noble Roman women, to Porsenna, king of the Etruscans, during the war involving Tarquin the Proud. To explain her praiseworthy boldness in greater detail, one must take note of the context: when King Tarquin the Proud was expelled because of the monstrous crime committed by his son Sextus against Lucretia, and when his attempts to return by trickery failed, the situation erupted into open war. 

Porsenna, the King of Clusium, had joined this war at the request of Tarquin. However, when the Etruscans were held back from the city by the integrity of Horatius Cocles <note>original: "Horatii Coclitis," the legendary hero who famously defended the Sublician Bridge alone against the invading army.</note> as he defended the Sublician Bridge, and when Porsenna was shaken by the boldness of Mucius Scaevola <note>original: "Mucii Scaevolae," who proved Roman resolve by thrusting his right hand into a fire after failing to assassinate the king.</note>, he entered into a peace treaty with the Romans. To ensure this peace was kept, hostages were taken as pledges, and it so happened that Cloelia was sent along with many other maidens. 

When it perhaps seemed to her a great loss to the Republic that so many maidens were held by a foreign king, she armed her maidenly heart with manly daring. Deceiving the guards at night, she led many of the girls to the riverbank and mounted a horse—which she had perhaps never ridden before—that was grazing by the Tiber. Unafraid of the river's depth or the rushing water, she led all the maidens safely to the opposite side and restored them to their families. 

When Porsenna discovered this in the morning, he was deeply troubled. In a full session of the Senate, it was decided that the leader of the escapees should be returned to the king at his request, with the condition that she would later be returned safely to her own people by the king. The king, however, marvelling at the maiden’s virtue and delighted by her daring, not only allowed her to return to her people but gave her the power to choose which of the remaining hostages she wished to take with her. From all of them, she chose only the young boys <note>original: "impuberes," those who had not yet reached adulthood.</note>, a decision which was seen as commendable to her maidenly honor and was most welcome to the city. This was because she had specifically chosen to liberate those of an age most vulnerable to injury <note>original: "iniuriae," implying both physical harm and the risk of sexual violation or mistreatment.</note>. For this reason, she was decorated by the grateful citizens with a type of honor never seen before: she was granted an equestrian statue <note>A statue of a person on horseback, a high honor usually reserved for male military heroes.</note>, which was placed at the top of the Sacred Way <note>The "Via Sacra," the main street of ancient Rome, leading to the Forum.</note> and remained there undisturbed for a long time.

### Concerning Hippo, a Greek Woman

**Hippo** was a Greek woman, as is sufficiently understood from the records of the ancients. However, I do not believe she excelled in only a single noble deed <note>original: "unico tantum optimo valuisse opere," suggesting that her ultimate sacrifice was the result of a lifelong habit of virtue.</note>, for we ascend to the higher steps of character by degrees, as no one becomes truly great suddenly. But since the malice of antiquity has stripped away her lineage, her homeland, and her other deeds, it is my intention to bring forward what has reached us, lest it perish or her well-deserved glory be withdrawn from her.

We have learned that this Hippo was captured by chance by enemy sailors. Because she was possessed of great beauty and realized that the pirates intended to violate her and her modesty, she considered the glory of chastity so precious that she believed it could not be preserved except through death. Without waiting for violence to be done to her, she threw herself headlong into the waves; by these waters her life was taken, but her modesty was saved.

Who would not praise such a resolute <note>original: "severum," implying a strict or uncompromising moral choice.</note> decision by a woman? For the sake of a few years by which her life might perhaps have been extended, she redeemed her chastity and sought everlasting glory for herself through an untimely death. This work of virtue the stormy sea could not hide, nor could the deserted shore carry it away; instead, it is preserved in the light of day with its proper honor through the eternal monuments of literature. 

After her body had been tossed about for some time by the waves, as if in sport, it was driven by them onto the Erythraean shore <note>original: "eritreum litus," likely referring to the coast of Erythrae in Ionia, a Greek city-state.</note> and buried by the inhabitants of the coast in the manner of a shipwrecked person. Eventually, when the report spread from the enemies as to who she was and the cause of her death, the site of her grave on the shore was adorned by the Erythraeans with the highest veneration—a massive and long-lasting tomb served as a testimony to her preserved honor. Thus we may know that the light of virtue can be obscured by no shadows of adverse fortune.

### Concerning Megulia Dotata

I believe that **Megulia** (whom the ancient Romans nicknamed "Dotata," or "the Endowed") was a noble Roman woman. She was considered illustrious in that unrefined and—if I may put it this way—sacred age, before the best citizens had yet broken away from the nurturing arms of poverty to rush into the splendors of Asia and the luxury of great wealth, neglecting their former ways. She obtained this surname of "the Endowed," as I judge, because of the magnificence of her ancestors...

--- Page 80 ---
<meta>This page concludes the account of Megulia Dotata, contrasting ancient Roman frugality with the perceived decadence of the author's own time, and begins the biography of Veturia (mother of Coriolanus).</meta>

...[she was noted for] **extravagance** <note>original: "prodigalitate," here referring specifically to the size of her dowry rather than wasteful behavior.</note> rather than for any merit of her own deeds. For the fact that five hundred thousand pieces of **copper** <term>aes</term> <note>The *aes* was the standard copper or bronze coin of early Rome; in that era of austerity, such a sum was considered a shocking fortune.</note> were given as a dowry to her husband seemed so monstrous in that age that the nickname **"The Endowed"** <note>original: "Dotata."</note> was bestowed upon her. This name persisted for a long time, so much so that if anything exceeding the usual custom of the citizens was added to a maiden's dowry, she was immediately called a "Megulia Dotata" herself. 

O blessed simplicity! O praiseworthy poverty! You caused that which is the common **luxury** <note>original: "lasciuie," referring to the indulgence and excess of the 14th century.</note> of today to be judged as monstrous and rightly ridiculous. For we have now so exceeded the proper measure of things in every respect that you will hardly find a cobbler, a wood-splitter, a smith, a mercenary, a camp-follower, or even a farm steward who is willing to take a wife into his home for such a meager pittance. 

Nor is it any wonder, for even common women of the lowest rank have claimed for themselves the golden crowns of queens, as well as brooches, bracelets, and other regalia, which they wear—I will not say shamelessly, but certainly with great pride. Alas! I do not know whether to say our spirits have become so puffed up because we trust too much in one another, or rather—as I believe is most true—that by our own failing, the ambitions and insatiable desires of mortals have been so greatly exalted.

<meta>This page continues the narrative of Roman history, transitioning from the discussion of Megulia’s dowry to the story of Veturia, the mother of the exiled general Coriolanus, who is leading an army against his own city.</meta>

<note>A paperclip is attached to the right edge of the page. On the left, some text from the previous page is visible.</note>

A little while before, he had withdrawn to the enemies of the Romans. By them, he was received both kindly and honorably, for everywhere **virtue** <note>original: "virtus," which in this context implies both moral character and military courage.</note> is held in high esteem. Through both his own trickery and that of Attius Tullius the Volscian, he led those people back into a war against the Romans. Having been made the leader of the war by them, he led the army to the **Cluilian Trenches** <note>The *Fossae Cluiliae* were ancient earthworks located about five miles from Rome, marking the traditional border of Roman territory at that time.</note> at the fourth milestone from Rome. He reduced Roman affairs to such a state that messengers were sent to him, the exile, by the Senate to beg for peace on fair terms. Marcius dismissed them back to their homeland with a **harsh response** <note>original: "atroci," meaning fierce or cruel; he refused to negotiate unless the Romans restored Volscian lands.</note>, on account of which they were sent a second time, but were not received at all. For a third time, the priests went as suppliants, veiled with **religious fillets** <term>infulis</term> <note>The *infula* was a woolen headband worn by priests and those seeking sanctuary or peace, signaling their sacred status.</note> and carrying their insignias, but they returned in vain. 

And now, despair had invaded the minds of the Romans from every side. Then the matrons, coming in great crowds and with mournful complaints to **Veturia** <note>In some traditions, Veturia is the mother and Volumnia is the wife; other sources swap these names.</note>, the mother of Coriolanus, and **Volumnia**, his wife, prevailed upon the woman—now of great age—to go into the enemy camp with his wife. It was their hope that she might appease her son with prayers and tears, since it seemed the Republic could not be protected by the arms of men. Nor was a huge crowd of following women lacking from their company. 

When Coriolanus learned of her arrival, although he was swollen with pride, he was nevertheless dismayed by his mother’s coming. Rising from his seat, he left his tent and went to meet her to receive her. Indeed, Veturia—holding the wife of Coriolanus on one side and his children on the other—did not even look at her son before she set aside her **motherly devotion** <note>original: "pietate," the Roman virtue of duty toward family and gods.</note> and kindled herself into anger. Where she had left the city as a suppliant, upon entering the enemy camp, she became a rebuker. 

Stirring up the strength in her worn-out body, she said: "Stop your step, you hostile youth! I would wish to know, before I accept your embrace, whether you come to receive a mother or a captive enemy. I think it is an enemy. Oh, wretched me! Has that length of life so desired by mortals led me to this: that I should see you condemned to exile and thereafter an enemy of the Republic? Recognize, I beg you, where you stand as an armed enemy upon your throne. Recognize the homeland you have in your sight. Recognize indeed—and if you do not know it—this is the land in which you were conceived, in which you were born, and in which you were raised with great labor. With what spirit, then, with what mind, and with what impulse were you able to bring hostile arms against her? Did not the honor due to a parent, the love of a sweet wife, the devotion of your children, and reverence for your native fatherland meet you as you entered?"

<summary>This page recounts the legendary confrontation between the Roman exile Coriolanus and his mother, Veturia, who pleads with him to abandon his siege of Rome.</summary>

--- Page 82 ---
<meta>This page continues the speech of Veturia to her son Coriolanus (Marcius), as she attempts to dissuade him from attacking his home city of Rome. It picks up mid-sentence during her rebuke of his stubbornness.</meta>

...did not move your fierce heart, nor could they overwhelm your anger, however justly it was taken up. Did it not come to your memory when you first looked upon those walls: "There is my home and my ancestral **household gods** <term>penates</term>; there are my wife and children; there, through her own misfortune and my doing, is my unhappy mother"? The fathers <note>The "patres" or Senators of Rome.</note> came; the priests came; yet they could not move your stony heart to do as a request what you ought to have done of your own accord.

I perceive clearly enough, wretched as I am, that my own fertility has been adverse to my country and to myself. Where I thought I had given birth to a son and a citizen, I see now that I have given birth to an enemy—one most hostile and inflexible. Indeed, it would have been better had I never conceived; through my barrenness, Rome might have stood without being besieged, and I, a poor old woman, might have died in a free fatherland.

But I can no longer suffer anything that is more miserable for me or more shameful for you; nor, though I am most wretched, shall I be so for long. Look to these children of yours: if you persist, either an untimely death or long servitude awaits them. 

Tears followed these words, and then came the prayers of his wife and children, the mutual embraces, and the cries of the weeping and praying matrons. By these words, groans, and prayers, it was brought about that what the majesty of the ambassadors and the reverence of the priests could not achieve, was accomplished by the veneration of a mother: the anger of the fiercest leader was broken, and his purpose was turned back. 

With his forces scattered and dismissed, the camp of the enemy was moved back from the city. From this it followed—lest ingratitude detract from the glory of the women—that by a decree of the Senate, in that place where Veturia had softened her son's anger, a temple was built for the perpetual memory of this event, and an altar to **Women's Fortune** <note>original: "fortuna muliebri." The Temple of Fortuna Muliebris was located at the fourth milestone of the Via Latina.</note> was constructed of hewn stone. 

Which indeed, although it is most ancient, has endured almost undiminished into our own age. The Senate also ordained that when women passed by—to whom, until that age, no honor or only the very least was offered by men—men should stand up and give way on the road, a practice which is preserved in our fatherland by ancient rite even to this day. It was also granted that they were permitted to use the notable ear-ornaments of Eastern women, along with purple clothing and golden brooches and bracelets. Nor are there lacking those who assert that by the same decree of the Senate it was added that women could now receive an inheritance from anyone, which was not permitted before.

<summary>Veturia’s emotional appeal successfully convinces Coriolanus to abandon his siege of Rome, leading the Senate to grant Roman women unprecedented honors and legal rights.</summary>
<keywords>Veturia, Coriolanus, Rome, Fortuna Muliebris, women's rights, Senate decree, Roman history, maternal influence</keywords>

--- Page 83 ---
<meta>This page concludes the story of Veturia—the mother of the Roman general Coriolanus—following her successful plea to stop her son's invasion of Rome. It then transitions to the biography of Thamyris, a Greek painter.</meta>

Whether the merit of this woman <note>Veturia</note> should be more hateful to men or more pleasing to women is a matter of opinion, though I consider the matter most certain. For through the drive for ornaments, the resources of men are exhausted, and women walk about distinguished by royal finery. Men are impoverished; with the inheritance of their ancestors taken away, women are enriched. Both noble and common women follow this path and are honored. Many disadvantages for the former and advantages for the latter have followed from this. I would pursue Veturia with curses for the pride she bestowed upon women, had Roman liberty <note>original: "Romana libertas"</note> not stood upon its own feet. But I cannot praise that generosity of the Senate—a harmful custom persisted through so many centuries. They <note>the women</note> would have been content with a smaller gift; a temple enriched for **Female Fortune** <term>Fortuna Muliebris; a cult dedicated to the luck of women, specifically established after Veturia and Volumnia saved Rome</term> seemed quite sufficient for the status of women.

But what is the world of women <note>original: "muliebris mundus," referring here to their jewelry and dress</note> if not "womanish" men? Truly, what time has been adverse to men—though it has consumed many useful things—it has not been able to consume nor diminish the rights of women, who keep them with tenacious perseverance. Therefore, let women applaud Veturia; let them honor her name and her merit whenever they are adorned with precious stones, purple, and golden buckles, and when men rise for them as they walk by, even as the wealth of dying men is counted out for them.

# About Thamyris, daughter of Micon.

Thamyris was a woman who was an outstanding painter in her age. Although antiquity may have first taken away her name, it has not yet been able to take away her excellent artistry. Some hold that she was the daughter of the painter Micon in the ninetieth **Olympiad** <note>Approximately 420–417 BCE</note>. Truly, when we read that there were two Micons, both painters, and that both flourished at Athens at the same time, authors do not distinguish between them except with these few words: that she was the daughter of the Micon to whom the surname "the Younger" was added. 

Certainly, whoever's daughter she was, she possessed such wonderful genius that, having despised womanly duties, she imitated her father's art. Thus, while Archelaus was reigning among the Macedonians, she attained such singular glory in painting that at Ephesus, where **Diana** <note>The Greek goddess Artemis</note> was worshipped with special honor, they preserved for a long time a likeness of the same Diana, painted by her hand upon a certain panel, as a celebrated work. Since the skill of her artistry had endured for a very long time...

She gave such pleasing testimony of this skill that it is seen as memorable even to this day; it is indeed most praiseworthy if we look upon the works and baskets of others.

# About Queen Artemisia.

Artemisia, Queen of Caria, was a woman of vast spirit and a most holy and rare love, as well as an eternal example of chaste widowhood for posterity. Although it has not come down to our day from which parents she sprang or from which fatherland she came, it is sufficient for the praise of her nobility to know that she was the wife of Mausolus, the most powerful king of Caria. She loved him so much in life that, surviving him, she could not forget the dead. Of this matter, remarkable monuments have long stood. 

For (if faith is to be placed in famous writers), when first her most ancient husband ended his days, she carried out his lifeless corpse with honors. Nor did she allow his ashes—carefully collected after the funeral pyres—to be preserved and hidden in a golden urn, judging every other vessel to be unsuitable for so beloved a spouse except for that breast in which, after his death, the flames of old love burned far more than usual. Therefore, so that what was earthly might rest where the perpetual memory of his past life remained, she mixed the collected ashes little by little into her cups and drank them all until they were gone. Her remaining life was dedicated to perpetual tears, and thus, consumed by grief <note>original: "lūore," likely *lugore* or mourning</note>, believing she would go to her husband, she happily met her death.

Yet as a widow, she performed great deeds. It was an ancient custom for distinguished men to have remarkable tombs erected; Artemisia, setting aside all greed, devised a work that appeared most wondrous and costly, as was fitting for her love. Content neither with one nor with a common craftsman, she ordered Scopas, Bryaxis, Timotheus, and Leochares to be summoned (whom Greece proclaimed the foremost observers <note>In this context, the greatest artists and sculptors</note> of the whole world in that age). According to their judgment, she had a magnificent **Mausoleum** <term>Mausoleum; a grand tomb named after King Mausolus, which became the root for the modern word</term> designed for Mausolus and constructed with prepared marbles. Through that wondrous work—even if no other memory remained—she would make the name of her beloved husband eternal. This building indeed excelled almost all buildings of the world in both art and expense, and was long remembered as one of the **Seven Wonders of the World**.

<summary>This page concludes the biography of the painter Thamyris and details the extreme devotion of Queen Artemisia of Caria, who consumed her husband's ashes and commissioned the Mausoleum at Halicarnassus.</summary>
<keywords>Artemisia II of Caria, Mausolus, Mausoleum at Halicarnassus, Seven Wonders of the World, widowhood, Scopas, Bryaxis, Timotheus, Leochares, ancient art</keywords>

--- Page 85 ---
<meta>This page continues the biography of Queen Artemisia of Caria, specifically detailing the construction of the Mausoleum at Halicarnassus, one of the Seven Wonders of the Ancient World, which she commissioned for her late husband.</meta>

it will not be absurd to have made a singular mention of these things. For the fame of the craftsmen will flourish, and the magnificence of the renowned woman will become more illustrious through them. 

Therefore, the architects at Halicarnassus, the chief city of Caria, by the queen’s command, established the tomb in a square form; those sides which face the south and the north <note>original: "archos," referring to the Arctic or northern constellation</note> they extended sixty-three feet in length. The remaining sides were shorter, and they raised the structure to a height of one hundred and forty feet. They also desired that the entire monument be surrounded by thirty-six marble columns. 

Furthermore, they say that Scopas <note>A famous Greek sculptor and architect of the 4th century BCE</note> sculpted that part which faces the east <note>The OCR reads "eorum," but in the context of the classical sources Boccaccio cited, this is "Eurum," the East Wind</note>. Bryaxis carved the side which turns toward the north, while Leochares had undertaken the carving of the side turned toward the west. The fourth side was left to Timotheus. 

In sculpting statues and narrative scenes <note>original: "hystoriis," referring to relief carvings of mythological or historical events</note> and other elements pertaining to the work, these men expressed the powers of their genius with such great skill—each desiring to surpass the others in mastery—that onlookers sometimes believed they had drawn living faces out of the marble. This was seen to be so not only then, but for many centuries afterward; for the glory of the artist's hand [remained] there... <note>The text ends mid-sentence; the full thought in the original Latin suggests the artists competed for glory until the end of their lives.</note>

...taken to Mount Algidus <note>A mountain in the Alban Hills near Rome, frequently the site of battles with the Aequi people</note> against the Aequi <note>original: "equos," likely a transcription error for "Aequos," a neighboring tribe frequently at war with early Rome</note>, while Virginius was serving as a soldier in that same campaign. When the girl, still in her tender youth, frustrated his flatteries and could not be bent by any great gifts, nor by prayers or threats—for her heart was imbued with chastity—Appius was seized by such an insane madness that, since he wished to steer her wavering mind but judged it unsafe to openly use force <note>original: "vim publice inferre"</note>, he turned his ingenuity toward deception. 

He acted according to a prearranged plot so that Marcus Claudius—a freedman of his and a man of supreme audacity—should seize the girl as she was passing by the Forum <note>The central public square of Rome, used for law and commerce</note> (at the first opportunity) and claim her as his runaway slave <note>original: "mancipium fugitivum"</note>, leading her to his own home. And if anything should perhaps block the attempt, he was to immediately drag the matter into a legal case before Appius himself. 

When, a few days later, the freedman seized her with reckless daring as she was passing by and claimed her as his own, the girl cried out and resisted the vile man with all her strength. With the help of the matrons with whom she was walking, a crowd of people suddenly gathered. Among them arrived Icilius <note>Virginia's fiancé</note> and Numitor <note>Virginia's uncle</note>; after arguments from both sides, the matter reached the point where the girl was led into the praetorium <note>the magistrate's court</note> before her lover-judge. It was then obtained from the burning Appius that the judgment be delayed until the following day. 

In this matter, the fraud of Marcus Claudius failed; although orders had been sent to the camp that Virginius should not be permitted by the commanders to come to Rome if summoned, the father, having been called, arrived promptly. And with his daughter, and with his other friends, and dressed in a mourning cloak <note>original: "pallio sordidatus," referring to the soiled or dark clothing worn by those in mourning or facing legal peril to elicit public sympathy</note>, he came into the court where, on the contrary, Marcus Claudius was seeking the slave-girl. For after Virginius was heard, the lustful presiding judge adjudicated Virginia to be a runaway slave. 

When Marcus wished to seize her, and Virginius had spoken many things in vain against Appius, Virginius finally—with teeth gnashing in rage—obtained permission that it might at least be lawful for him to speak for a little while with her and her nurse, so that perhaps, once the truth of some old mistake were discovered, he might relinquish his grief with less hesitation. And when he had withdrawn with them near the shops of Cloacina <note>The "Tabernae" or shops near the shrine of Venus Cloacina in the Roman Forum</note>, yet still in sight of the court, he snatched up a butcher’s knife and said: "In the only way I am able, my beloved daughter, I claim your liberty!" <note>original: "libertatem," abbreviated in text</note>

However, the reckless client Marcus Claudius lamented his crime—though not in the way he deserved, for he fled and was protected—through exile. The goods of both the patron and himself were confiscated to the public treasury. 

Nothing is more ruinous than an unjust judge. Whenever such a person follows the command of a wicked mind, it is inevitable that the whole order of law is perverted. The power of the law is dissolved, the work of virtue is weakened, the reins are loosened for wickedness, and in short, the entire public good is dragged into ruin. If the wicked undertaking of Appius does not appear sufficiently elsewhere, the things that followed from it bring it into the light. For while this powerful man failed to put a bridle on his evil lust, he nearly succeeded—through a freedman steeped in fraud—in turning a free woman into a slave, a maiden into an adulteress, and a betrothed woman into a mistress. 

By his detestable decree, it came to pass that a father was armed against his daughter, and familial devotion <note>original: "pietas"</note> was turned into cruelty. And so that this incestuous man would not rejoice in his fraud-won desire, an innocent girl was killed, cries rose up in the city, rioting broke out in the military camps, and a separation of the commoners from the fathers <note>original: "patribus," referring to the Patricians or Senators</note> arose. And almost all Roman affairs were placed in danger. 

O famous governor and distinguished lawmaker! That which he should have punished in others with dire punishment, he himself did not fear to commit. Alas for me, how often we mortals are endangered by such a plague, and how often we, the undeserving, are dragged to our destruction. We are burdened by a shameful yoke, despoiled, and killed by pressing wickedness. What evil is this? Those who are supposedly perfect do not fear to turn that which was invented to restrain lusts into a license for crimes, terrified by no fear. And while it behooves [a philosopher/magistrate] to [keep his] eyes...

...[so great a man] was born, nor would one like him be born in the future. This added no small amount of luster to Olympias, if indeed it is a glory for mothers to have given birth to such outstanding sons. But this radiance could not entirely escape without being eventually frustrated by certain marks <note>The author uses "marks" or "notis" to mean stains on her reputation or moral character.</note> cast upon it. Almost nothing more shameful could have happened to a queen, and what was even more disgraceful was the suspicion that Alexander had been conceived through that adultery.

This suspicion so deeply moved Philip <note>Philip II of Macedon (r. 359–336 BCE).</note> that he not only openly declared at one time that Alexander was not his own son, but he also marked Olympias with the ignominy of divorce. He then took Cleopatra <note>Historically known as Cleopatra Eurydice, the last wife of Philip II.</note>, the daughter of Alexander of Epirus, as his wife. Olympias could not hide how much she suffered from this slight. For she, who until that day—except for that one stain of suspected adultery—was famous only for her royal splendors, now made herself notable for various enormities.

It was indeed believed that the young Pausanias—a man born of splendid and honorable blood—was stirred up and driven by her to the murder of Philip, her own husband, and that Alexander was even aware of the plot. For on the morning following the day Pausanias had been fixed to the cross <note>original: "in cruce pendentis." A few days later, by her command, his corpse was taken down and honorably burned over the remaining royal remains of Philip, according to the Macedonian rite, and then buried with a solemn procession. Furthermore, she ordered that the sword with which Pausanias had killed Philip be placed in the Temple of Apollo under the name Myrtale <note>original: "miscalis," likely a corruption of "Myrtale," which was Olympias's name before she took the name Olympias.</note>.

Eventually, her son Alexander increased in fame through his greatest victories. However, after he died by poison in Babylon, and after her brother Alexander was slain among the Lucanians, and when King Arrhidaeus <note>Philip III Arrhidaeus, Alexander the Great's half-brother.</note> of Macedon and his wife Eurydice prohibited her from entering Macedonia from Epirus, she used the favor of the veteran Macedonians to have them put to death. She then obtained the kingdom of Macedonia for herself alone as widow and queen. But when she began to rage everywhere like a wild beast in the blood of both noble and common Macedonians, she was besieged by Cassander <note>Cassander was one of the "Diadochi" or successors who fought for control of Alexander's empire.</note> in the city of Pydna. She was so constrained there that she, along with the townsfolk, suffered a lack of all things...

### Concerning Claudia the Vestal Virgin

I would believe Claudia the Vestal Virgin <note>Claudia Pulchra, a Vestal Virgin who famously intervened in her father's (Appius Claudius Pulcher) triumph in 143 BCE.</note> to be worthily born from the noble blood of the Romans when I consider her remarkable devotion <note>term: pietas - a Roman virtue denoting duty and religious-like devotion to one's family, gods, and country.</note> to her father. 

Indeed, her father was celebrating a triumph <note>term: triumph - a civil ceremony and religious rite of ancient Rome, held to publicly celebrate and sanctify the success of a military commander.</note> in a spectacular procession on his own authority, despite a decree of the Senate. While a great crowd of Roman commoners watched, one of the Tribunes of the Plebs, breaking out into a private grudge against him as if he were undeserving, thrust himself into the middle of the scene. With the insolent boldness typical of a Tribune, he laid violent hands upon the man in the triumph and attempted to drag him down from his chariot.

When the virgin Claudia saw this among the spectators, she was immediately moved by urgent piety. Grieving and forgetting the modesty of her sex and her sacred headbands <note>term: vittae - woolen headbands worn by Vestal Virgins as a symbol of their purity and status.</note> with which she was covered, she could not endure it. Instead, she suddenly broke through the middle of the crowds with force. By her bold appearance, she compelled the throng to give way, and with unwearied strength, she inserted herself between the arrogance of the Tribune and the glory of her father. By whatever daring it was achieved, once the Tribune was removed, her father’s ascent to the Capitol <note>The Capitoline Hill, the destination of the triumphal procession where sacrifices were made to Jupiter.</note> proceeded freely.

O sweet love! O unbroken devotion! What else shall we believe gave strength to the weak body of a virgin, or caused her to forget her religious duties, except for him? To see him oppressed by injury whom she remembered as the nurturer of her infancy, the one who soothed her with pious endearments, the one who offered vows for her safety, the remover of all harms, and the instructor of her advancing years. But so that enough may be said of this, I ask: who, while men were raising an uproar because of this, [would blame] the holy woman in...

Indeed, I would not undeservedly doubt who brought home the more remarkable triumph: whether the father led it to the Capitol, or the daughter brought it back into the temple.

### Concerning Verginia, the wife of Lucius Volumnius

<margin>V</margin> Verginia was indeed a famous matron among the Romans. She was the daughter of a certain Aulus, a patrician, yet she was distinct from the Verginia mentioned earlier <note>This refers to the Verginia whose death at the hands of her own father sparked the overthrow of the Decemvirs.</note>. In addition to the marks of her nobility, she was considered superior to others in her era by the merits of her chastity. To recognize her whole life and pay her the deserved renown, it will suffice to recount a single but most praiseworthy deed. 

As is well known, there was once in the city of Rome, in the Cattle Market <note>term: foro boario — the site of the primary meat and fish market in ancient Rome, located near the Tiber.</note> near the round temple of Hercules, a famous shrine to Patrician Chastity <note>term: patricie pudicitie — a cult dedicated to the personification of modesty, initially reserved for women of the noble patrician class.</note> which had long ago been most holily dedicated by noble women. During the fourth consulship of Quintus Fabius and Quintus Publilius Decius Mus <note>The year 295 BCE.</note>, when by order of the Senate supplications were being made in all the temples to ward off omens, and the patrician women were performing the sacred rites there with the utmost purity according to ancient custom, it happened that Verginia approached with the others to perform the sacrifice. 

However, she was haughtily removed from the place by the ruling patrician matrons on the grounds that she had married Lucius Volumnius, a plebeian man <note>term: plebeio — a member of the general citizenry of Rome, as opposed to the privileged patrician class.</note>, even though he had been consul the previous year. A brief womanly altercation arose at the sacred building, which finally flared up into a greater fire of spirits through womanly indignation. But when Verginia had declared that she was both chaste and a patrician, and should by no means be barred from the temple of Patrician Chastity simply because she had married a plebeian man as a virgin, and after she had extolled the deeds of her husband with wonderful praises, she left the patrician women and returned home in indignation. There, she added a remarkable work to her words. For since she had a large house on the Long Street <note>term: vico longo — a street in ancient Rome that ran between the Quirinal and Viminal hills.</note> where she then lived with her husband, she decided that a portion of it was sufficient for a modest chapel; she enclosed it from the rest of the house, and there [set up] an altar...

<summary>This page concludes the praise of Claudia's filial devotion and begins the story of Verginia, a patrician woman who, after being excluded from a shrine because of her marriage to a plebeian, established her own shrine to Plebeian Chastity.</summary>
<keywords>Claudia, Verginia, Lucius Volumnius, Patrician Chastity, Pudicitia, Forum Boarium, Vicus Longus, Roman Matrons</keywords>

--- Page 95 ---
<meta>This page continues the narrative of Verginia, daughter of Aulus. Having been excluded from the Temple of Patrician Chastity due to her marriage to a plebeian, she has just established a competing shrine for plebeian women. The text concludes her story and begins the account of Flora.</meta>

...established it, and complained that she had been subjected to a suspected injury by the patricians. "I therefore pray and exhort you," she said, "that just as you see the men of this city maintain a continuous contest for virtue, so you also may take up a contest among ourselves for the sole honor of matronly modesty. Give every effort so that this altar, which I dedicate as the Altar of Plebeian Chastity <note>original: "plebeie pudicicie"</note> in your presence, may be worshipped more holily <note>original: "sanctius" — the OCR "sauccius" is likely a transcription error for "sanctius".</note> and by more chaste women than that other one. Let it appear by your actions that heavenly souls are not only infused into the breasts of patrician women." 

O worthy and most holy words of a matron! O indignation to be praised, and an invention to be raised to the stars with joyful applause! This conspiracy by a woman <note>original: "virginea" — used here likely to denote her character or status as a dedicated wife rather than a literal virgin.</note> was not aimed at seizing the wealth of men or wanton ornaments; rather, she first took a stand against the wanton and petulant eyes and desires of young men. She proceeded with the best institution and holiest strength to earn the glory of chastity. So much so that it was then begun, and for a long time thereafter practiced, that no woman—unless she was of proven modesty and had been married to only one man <note>term: univira — a woman who remained faithful to her first husband even after his death; such women were held in the highest social regard in Rome.</note>—was granted the right to sacrifice in that temple. Thus, with the lustful hope of lewd onlookers broken, the sanctity of this place equaled the patrician altar. Nor would I doubt that many women, out of a desire for glory and to avoid the ignominy of being barred from the sacrifice, applied themselves to the cause and study of preserving their chastity.

## Concerning Flora the prostitute, goddess of flowers and wife of Zephyrus.

Antiquity seems to testify that Flora was a Roman woman. To the degree that her shameful trade lacked honor, her fame and favoring fortune matched it. She was (as all assert) a most wealthy woman, but accounts differ concerning the source of her riches. For some say that she consumed the entire flower of her youth and bodily beauty in public prostitution <note>original: "meretricio publico"</note> among the brothels, wicked pimps, and young men. By stripping the substance of various foolish men with wanton flatteries—as is the custom of such women—and gnawing and plucking away at them from every side, she came into such vast wealth. 

Others, however, thinking it more honorable, relate a charming and ridiculous story about her. They assert that a temple guardian <note>term: aedituum — a sacristan or person in charge of maintaining a temple.</note> of Hercules at Rome, being idle, began a game of dice <note>original: "tesseris"</note> playing one hand against the other. Since he had assigned the right hand to Hercules and the left to himself, they say he made a wager that if...

...should Hercules lose, he would provide a feast and a female companion for himself from the temple’s collection <note>original: "de stipe templi" — the collection of small coins offered by worshippers.</note>. If, however, Hercules emerged as the victor, the guard said he would do the very same for the god using his own money. Indeed, since Hercules—who was well-accustomed to overcoming even monsters—triumphed, the guard confirmed that he had prepared a banquet and a noble courtesan named Flora <note>Flora is often identified in Roman myth as a goddess of flowers, but here the author follows a tradition that rationalizes her as a wealthy historical woman.</note> for him. 

They say that while she was sleeping in the temple, she had a vision that she had lain with Hercules, and that she was told by him that she would receive the payment for her intimacy from the first man she encountered upon leaving the temple at dawn. When she encountered a very wealthy man named Tarrutius <note>The OCR "fanicio" is a likely corruption of "Tarrutio," a wealthy Roman who, according to legend, married Flora or Acca Larentia.</note> as she was leaving the temple, she was loved by him and taken to his home. After she had lived with him for a long time, she was left as his heir upon his death, and in this way, she became enriched. 

Indeed, there are those who say that this woman was not Flora, but Acca Larentia <note>Acca Larentia was the legendary foster-mother of Romulus and Remus; some traditions claimed she was a prostitute before becoming the twins' nurse.</note>, who either had nursed or did nurse Romulus and Remus. In truth, I do not concern myself with this discrepancy, provided it remains established that Flora was both a prostitute and a woman of great wealth. This woman, however (to move toward the point I desire to make), as the end of her mortal life approached, since she had no son and possessed a desire to perpetuate her name—as I believe—through feminine cunning for the future...

--- Page 97 ---
<meta>This page continues the biography of Flora, moving from her historical identification as a wealthy Roman woman to the mythological origins claimed by ancient writers. It bridges the gap between the "historical" courtesan and the goddess of flowers.</meta>

Long ago, they say there was a native nymph of marvelous beauty named Chloris <note>original: "clozaz" — a common transcription error for "Chloris," the Greek goddess of flowers.</note>, who was most ardently loved by the wind Zephyrus <note>The Greek god of the west wind, associated with the coming of spring.</note> (whom in Latin we call Favonius). At last, she was taken as his wife. To her, by that same wind—whom the ancients in their foolishness numbered among the gods—divinity was granted as a dowry gift <note>original: "dotalitio... munere"</note> or for the sake of the marriage (as is customary).

With this office, she was the first to adorn the trees, hills, and meadows with flowers and preside over them; for this reason, from "Chloris" she was also called "Flora." And because fruit follows from flowers, the people of antiquity granted sacred rites and games to this goddess, so that by appeasing her divinity through these games, she might grant them a plentiful harvest and bring the fruits to maturity with great abundance.

Led astray by this deceit <note>The author, likely Boccaccio or a similar medieval moralist, is critiquing the pagan transformation of a "sinful" woman into a goddess.</note>, they believe that she—who while alive had frequented the brothels <note>original: "fornices" — the vaulted underground chambers in Rome where prostitutes worked, from which we get the word "fornication."</note> and prostituted herself for anyone even for the smallest coin—was carried off to heaven by Zephyrus on his wings and now sits with Queen Juno and the other goddesses.

<summary>This page critiques the mythological origins of Flora, arguing that the goddess of flowers was actually a common prostitute whose history was scrubbed and replaced with a divine romance with the wind Zephyrus.</summary>
<keywords>Flora, Chloris, Zephyrus, Favonius, mythology, Roman religion, prostitution, etymology, spring, flowers</keywords>

--- Page 98 ---
<meta>This page continues a collection of biographies of famous women. The previous page concluded the story of Flora, and this page begins mid-narrative with the story of "Roman Charity"—a daughter who saved her mother from starvation in prison.</meta>

With great cleverness, she obtained permission from the guard to enter the prison on the condition that she carry nothing inside. Then, she helped her mother, who was now starving, with her milk (with which she was then overflowing from a recent birth). Finally, after several consecutive days, the guard began to wonder how the condemned woman could have stayed alive for so long without food. Watching in secret to see what the daughter was doing with her mother, he noticed that she was drawing out her breasts and offering them to her mother’s mouth to be sucked. 

Marvelling at such devotion and the daughter's unusual method of nourishing her mother, he brought the discovery to the triumvir <note>original: "triūviro" — a member of a board of three Roman officials responsible for judicial matters and prisons.</note>, the triumvir to the praetor <note>original: "pretori" — a high-ranking magistrate or judge in ancient Rome.</note>, and the praetor announced it to the public council. By common consent, it was decided that the mother's deserved punishment should be pardoned as a gift to the daughter's devotion. 

If antiquity bestowed the oak crown <note>original: "querneam coronam" — The Civic Crown, one of the highest military honors in Rome, given to a soldier who saved a fellow citizen's life.</note> upon a man who saved a citizen by force in battle, with what praise shall we decorate a daughter who saved her mother in prison? Truly, you will find no garland among the leaves worthy enough for such a pious deed. 

This devotion was not only holy but admirable; it is not just to be equaled, but rather preferred to the duty of nature. By nature, we are taught to guide infants through their weak age with milk; by this act, parents are rescued from death. Wondrous, therefore, are the powers of devotion. For it penetrates not only feminine hearts (which are easily drawn to compassion and tears), but sometimes even those hardened in unbridled and adamantine obstinacy. Seated first in the surrounding heart-strings with flexible humanity, it softens everything hard. As the best seeker of opportunities, it acts so that people mix their tears with the wretched, and at least undergo sicknesses and dangers out of longing; and sometimes, if remedies are lacking, it may even undergo death in another's place. These effects are so great that we should wonder less if we, as pious children, do anything for our parents, since in doing so we seem rather to be returning a favor and paying back what we received elsewhere through a debt of restitution.

--- Page 99 ---
<meta>The previous page concluded the biography of the Assyrian Queen Semiramis, focusing on her military prowess and her legendary lust. This page begins the account of Marcia, a Roman woman celebrated for a very different set of virtues: her lifelong chastity and her remarkable talent as a painter and sculptor.</meta>

For I find that she was not bound by a priestly robe, nor dedicated to the mark of Diana <note>The Roman goddess of the hunt and a symbol of virginity; the author notes Marcia chose her lifestyle freely rather than through a religious vow.</note>, nor involved in any other profession (by which many women are either coerced or restrained). Rather, by the integrity of her mind alone, she overcame the **sting of the flesh** <note>original: "carnis aculeo." A common medieval and Renaissance metaphor for physical desire or sexual temptation.</note>—to which even the most excellent men have sometimes succumbed—and preserved her body untainted by the **contagion of man** <note>original: "contagione hominis." In the context of the author's time, "contagion" here refers to the moral and physical "pollution" of sexual intercourse.</note> even unto death.

Truly, if Marcia is to be highly praised for such commendable constancy, she is no less to be commended for her **manly intellect** <term>original: "ingenij virilis." In Boccaccio's era, intellectual excellence was often gendered as "manly," even when found in a woman.</term> and the skill of her hands. Whether she learned under a master or nature showed her the way remains uncertain to us. What seems most certain is that, having spurned traditional womanly duties so as not to waste away in idleness, she gave herself entirely to the study of painting and sculpture. 

In time, she painted with a brush and carved from ivory images so skillfully and with such polish that she surpassed Sopolis and Dionysius <note>Two famous painters of antiquity mentioned by Pliny the Elder.</note>, the most celebrated artists of her age. The most well-known proof of this was that the panels painted by her were more valuable than all others. And—what is far more wonderful—they assert that she did not only paint exquisitely (as has occasionally happened with others), but that she possessed such **swift hands for painting** that no one ever had their equal.

Furthermore, there were many lasting signs of her art, but among them was her own **likeness** <note>original: "effigies." This refers to a self-portrait.</note>, which she rendered on a panel using a mirror to guide her. She captured the lines, the soft colors, and the expression of her face so perfectly that no contemporary who saw it had any doubt as to who it was. 

Among her other traits (to come now to her unique character), they assert it was her particular custom, whether painting with a brush or carving with a **chisel** <term>original: "celte." A small chisel used for fine detail in sculpture or ivory work.</term>, to very frequently create images of women, while rarely or never depicting men. I believe her **modest strength** <note>original: "pudicus robur." This suggests her commitment to modesty was a source of personal power.</note> was the cause of this habit. For since the ancients mostly depicted naked or semi-naked figures, it seemed appropriate to her either to create "imperfect" men <note>Meaning men who were clothed or whose anatomy was hidden, which might be seen as poor artistic technique at the time.</note> or, if she made them "perfectly" (physically complete), she would seem to have forgotten her virginal modesty. To avoid falling into either error, she judged it easier to abstain from both.

<summary>This page introduces Marcia, an ancient Roman artist praised for her self-imposed perpetual virginity and her extraordinary skill in painting and ivory carving, which allowed her to surpass famous male contemporaries. It highlights her famous self-portrait and her refusal to depict the male nude to preserve her modesty.</summary>
<keywords>Marcia, Roman Art, Painting, Sculpture, Chastity, Modesty, Self-Portrait, Ivory Carving, Boccaccio, Virtuous Women</keywords>

# Concerning Sulpicia, Wife of Fulvius

Sulpicia was once a most venerable woman, no less celebrated by the testimony of Roman matrons for her strictly observed chastity than Lucretia, who achieved her fame by ending her life with a blade <note>Lucretia is the legendary Roman heroine whose suicide after being raped by the son of the king led to the founding of the Roman Republic.</note>. She was the daughter of Servius Paterculus and the wife of Fulvius Flaccus; both were noble men. 

When the Senate, by the order of the Ten Men <note>The *decemviri sacris faciundis*, a college of priests responsible for interpreting sacred texts.</note> and according to ancient custom, had determined from the Sibylline Books <note>The Latin text reads *sublimis libris*, likely a variant or transcription error for the *Libri Sibyllini*, the prophetic books of Rome.</note> that a statue of Venus Verticordia <note>Latin: *Venus Verticordia*, the "Turner of Hearts," a title for the goddess tasked with turning the minds of women from lust toward virtue.</note> should be consecrated in the city so that virgins and other women might not only abstain from lust but also more easily be turned toward praiseworthy modesty <term>pudicitia: a complex Roman virtue combining modesty, sexual honor, and social propriety</term>. 

Following the mandate of the Ten Men (which stipulated that the most chaste of the Roman matrons should dedicate the statue), a judgment was held among the vast multitude of women that then abounded in Rome to find the most pure. First, through their proceedings, one hundred women were chosen from the whole assembly who were considered more renowned for their chastity. Sulpicia was one of those chosen. Then, by order of the Senate and the judgment of those same women, ten even more illustrious women were selected from the hundred, and Sulpicia was numbered among them. Finally, when one was sought from the ten, Sulpicia was chosen by the supreme consensus of everyone. 

While it was a beautiful honor for her to have dedicated the statue of Venus Verticordia at that time, her fame was far more beautiful because, in the estimation of such a vast multitude, her purity was preferred above all others. She was seen by the eyes of those present as a kind of heavenly deity <note>original: *numen*</note> of modesty, recognized by the solemn oath of all. For all future ages, her name seems to have been granted an almost unfading glory through this veneration. 

But, someone might ask: "If a hundred chaste women were chosen, what additional degree of chastity could be added to this one woman so that she was deservedly preferred above the others?" This is clear to those men and women who understand that chastity is more than simply abstaining from intercourse with a man who is not one's husband.

<summary>This page recounts the selection of Sulpicia as the most chaste woman in Rome to dedicate a statue of Venus Verticordia, illustrating the Roman cultural value of female virtue and public modesty.</summary>
<keywords>Sulpicia, Venus Verticordia, chastity, pudicitia, Roman Senate, Servius Paterculus, Fulvius Flaccus, Sibylline Books, virtue</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the description of Sulpicia, who has just been chosen as the most chaste matron in Rome to dedicate the statue of Venus Verticordia.</meta>

...to restrain the hems of her garments <note>This refers to the Roman practice of a modest woman ensuring her *stola* (long dress) covered her properly, signaling her status and moral character.</note>. She was to utter words that were not only honorable but also few and well-timed. She fled idleness as if it were the most certain and most destructive enemy of modesty <term>pudicitia: a Roman virtue encompassing sexual honor, modesty, and social propriety</term>. She abstained from banquets (since "without wine and bread, love grows cold" <note>original: *absque libero & cerere frigeat venus*. This is a classical proverb meaning that lust (Venus) is fueled by alcohol (Liber/Bacchus) and food (Ceres).</note>). She avoided songs and dances as if they were the arrows of luxury. She devoted herself to thrift and sobriety, managed the affairs of the household, and kept her ears hardened against obscene conversations. She abstained from wandering about the streets. She cast away cosmetics and unnecessary perfumes, and rejected superfluous ornaments. She trampled harmful thoughts and desires with all her strength, persisting in sacred meditations and watchfulness. And—lest I run through every possible evidence of perfect modesty—she cherished her husband alone with the highest affection. She disregarded all other men, unless she loved them with a brotherly affection. Even when approaching her husband's embrace for the sake of conceiving children, she did so not without a modest blush of both her face and her soul. Because all these virtues were perhaps not found in the others...

nor did she even accuse the hiding Harmonia (in whose place she was being killed). Instead, silent and unmoved, she received the death-dealing blows and died. Harmonia was equally fortunate and unfortunate: fortunate in the girl's loyalty, but unfortunate in her own situation. Nevertheless, when the faithful Harmonia <note>Harmonia was the daughter of Gelon of Syracuse. During a revolt, her nurse dressed her own daughter in the princess's clothes to trick the assassins. The nurse's daughter accepted the death to save the princess.</note>, from her hiding place, saw the perseverance of the innocent girl and her brave spirit in the face of death, and the virginal blood flowing from her wounds, she was stunned. Although she could have escaped while the assassins were leaving, she began to admire such loyalty with her whole heart. Captured by a noble sense of duty <term>pietas: a Roman virtue signifying duty and devotion to one's country, gods, and family</term> and with tears flowing, she could not endure to watch that innocent blood go unpunished. Considering it better to descend to the underworld in an early death with such a loyal companion than to prolong a life saved by another's faith only to face old age among treacherous citizens, she came forward into the midst of the scene. O duty! O ancient loyalty! Calling back the bloody swords upon herself, she confessed the deceit of the nurse, the loyalty of the slain girl, and her own identity, voluntarily offering her own blood as a sacrifice for the one already killed. Struck by many wounds, she collapsed in death next to the corpse as best she could. It is most worthy to restore through literature that which death took from her. Truly, it is difficult to discern whose was greater: the loyalty <note>original: "fides"</note> of the one who died first, or the devotion <note>original: "pietas"</note> of the survivor. The first makes the name of the former eternal; the second does the same for the latter.

### Concerning Busa of Canusium, an Apulian woman.

Busa (whom some call Paulina, as if Busa were a family name) was an Apulian woman, originally from Canusium. That magnificent deed, which is the only thing antiquity has left to posterity concerning her, makes me believe she was born of noble blood and was splendid in other qualities of mind. For they say that while Hannibal was waging a hostile war of the Carthaginians <note>original: "penoꝝ" (Poenorum)</note> against the Romans, ravaging all of Italy with fire and sword and defiling it with much blood, he reached the Apulian village of Cannae. There, in a great struggle, he had not only overcome his enemies but had nearly shattered all the Italian forces. It happened that from that conflict and massive slaughter, about ten thousand men arrived at Canusium by night through bypasses, having escaped as scattered and wandering refugees. At that time, the city of Canusium still kept its faith in the Roman alliance.

She provided daily expenses for everyone from her own wealth. Having

<meta>This page concludes the story of Busa of Canusium, focusing on the proper use of wealth, before beginning the biography of Sophonisba, a Carthaginian noblewoman and Queen of Numidia during the Second Punic War.</meta>

<note>A metal paper clip is attached to the left edge of the page.</note>

...she used [her wealth] most excellently. For Mother Nature did not bring gold forth from the deep recesses of the earth into the public light only for it to be carried from the mother's womb into a tomb—as misers do when they bury it in a vault and brood over it with excessive guarding, as if it were to be born a second time. Rather, it was brought forth above all so that it might be offered for the common benefit; then for our own honorable splendor and the fellowship of friends; and, if any remains, so that we may provide aid with a liberal spirit to those cast down by the injury of fortune, those whom the wrath of heaven tires with poverty, those unworthily oppressed, those imprisoned for another’s crime, and any who are worn down by anxious want. This is not so that we may be seen, but so that we may be of use; not that we may profit, but that we may bestow such advantages. Yet, this must be done with the moderation of reason, lest while we help others we bring poverty upon ourselves, by which we might be forced—I will not say to lay violent hands upon the goods of others—but even to look upon them with longing eyes.

## Concerning Sophonisba, Queen of Numidia.

<margin>s</margin>
Sophonisba, though she was a queen of the Numidians, shining in power, became far more illustrious through the intrepid austerity with which she met the death she took upon herself. She was the daughter of Hasdrubal, son of Gisco <note>Hasdrubal Gisco was a major Carthaginian general during the Second Punic War, not to be confused with Hannibal's brother of the same name.</note>, the greatest of the Carthaginian princes, at the time when Hannibal was harrying [the Romans] in battle. When she was in the flower of her age and of quite extraordinary beauty, she was joined as a virgin in marriage by her father to Syphax, the most powerful king of the Numidians. This was not done merely from a desire for a royal alliance; rather, that wise man [Hasdrubal] hoped, as the Roman war pressed on, to withdraw the only "barbarian" <term>barbarus: a term used by Romans and Greeks to describe anyone outside their own cultures, here referring to the North African king</term> king from his alliance with the Romans. He sought to turn him to the side of the Carthaginians against the Romans through the influence of his daughter’s charms. Nor was he deceived by his premeditated scheme. For when Syphax had celebrated the nuptials, he was drawn into such a fire of love by the beauty of the young woman (who had been well-instructed [by her father]) that he considered nothing dear or delightful to himself except her. And while this unhappy man was consumed by passion, it became known that Cornelius Scipio <note>Publius Cornelius Scipio, later known as Scipio Africanus, the Roman general who took the war to Africa to draw Hannibal away from Italy.</note> had crossed from Sicily into Africa with his armies. Hasdrubal, through the warnings of Sophonisba and her flatteries and prayers, so drew the soul of Syphax toward his own desires, that not only...

--- Page 105 ---
<meta>Continuing from the previous page, the narrative describes how Sophonisba persuaded her husband, King Syphax of Numidia, to switch allegiances from Rome to Carthage during the Second Punic War.</meta>

[She persuaded Syphax] to abandon the Romans, to whom he had pledged the faith of his friendship, and to join Carthage and the Carthaginians. Indeed, he went so far as to voluntarily assume leadership of a war that was not his own. For this reason, trampling upon the guarantees of faith which he had promised to his guest-friend <term>guest-friend (hospes): a sacred bond of hospitality and protection in the ancient world</term> Scipio just the day before, he sent a letter to him—while Scipio was still preparing to cross over into Africa—forbidding him from landing.

However, Scipio—a young man of immense spirit—condemned the wickedness of this barbarian king. Having landed his troops not far from Carthage, he prioritized the defeat of Syphax above all else through his ally King Masinissa and his lieutenant Laelius. Through their efforts, Syphax was captured, his army defeated, and he was led back to Cirta, the royal city of Numidia. Nor was he displayed to the citizens, burdened with chains, until the surrender of the city had been made to Masinissa.

While Laelius had not yet arrived, Masinissa entered the city. Amidst the sudden upheaval of events and the general confusion, he entered the palace where he was beloved, and there he met Sophonisba. She, well aware of her change in fortune, saw him entering the vestibule, distinguished from the others by his armor. Recognizing him as the king, she threw herself at his knees; retaining the spirit of her former station, she said:

"Thus, renowned king, it has seemed good to God and to your good fortune that you should have total power over us, who but a moment ago were monarchs. Yet, if a captive is permitted to utter suppliant words before her conqueror and the master of her life and death—and to touch his knees and his victorious right hand—I, cast down, beseech you by your majesty. In that majesty I also shared but a short while ago. I pray by our common lineage, the kingdom, and the shared Numidian name—and if you have been received under a better omen than that under which Syphax has departed—I pray that you do with me, whom adverse fortune has newly placed under your power, what seems pious and good in your eyes; provided only that I am not handed over alive to the insolent and arrogant judgment of the Romans, the greatest enemies of the Carthaginians. For you can easily perceive what I, a Carthaginian enemy of Rome and daughter of Hasdrubal—to say nothing of being the wife of Syphax—might have to fear. And if every other means to this end is removed, grant that I might die by your hand rather than fall alive into the power of the enemy. I pray and beseech you."

Masinissa—who was himself a Numidian and (as they all are) prone to passion—looked upon the beauty of her face as she pleaded (for indeed, her misfortune had added a certain pious and unusual <note>The sentence continues onto the next page, describing the effect of Sophonisba's appearance on Masinissa.</note>

<keywords>Sophonisba, Syphax, Masinissa, Scipio Africanus, Second Punic War, Numidia, Carthage, Cirta, supplication</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the narrative of Sophonisba and King Masinissa following the fall of Cirta. On the previous page, Sophonisba had just begun her desperate plea to Masinissa to save her from being handed over to the Romans.</meta>

Moved by the grace of the suppliant <note>Sophonisba, who had thrown herself at his feet</note>, and drawn both by human compassion and by his own passion—before Laelius <note>Gaius Laelius, the Roman general and second-in-command to Scipio</note> had even arrived—Masinissa, still clad in his armor, gave her his right hand. Amidst the plaintive wailing of the women and the chaos of soldiers running about everywhere, he raised her from her prayer. He immediately joined her to himself as his wife, celebrating the nuptials in the very midst of the din of arms. I believe he judged that by this path he had found a way to satisfy both his own desire and Sophonisba’s desperate pleas.

At last, on the following day, he received the arriving Laelius. Then, with great ceremony, adorned in all his royal finery and bringing the rest of the plunder and his new wife, they returned to the camp. There, they were first received courteously by Scipio <note>Scipio Africanus, the supreme Roman commander in Africa</note> for their success in the campaign. However, shortly thereafter, Masinissa was amicably but firmly rebuked by Scipio for having celebrated a marriage with a woman who was technically a captive of the Roman people.

Distraught, Masinissa withdrew into his tent. Having sent away all witnesses, he was so overcome by sighs and tears and groaned for so long that he was heard by those standing outside. Finally, he ordered that the fate pressing upon Sophonisba be summoned. He called for the most faithful of his servants, to whom he had long ago entrusted a supply of poison to be kept for the uncertain accidents of fortune. He commanded the servant to carry the poison, diluted in a cup, to Sophonisba and to tell her:

"That Masinissa would have gladly kept the faith he had freely pledged to her, if he were able. But since his power to choose was being stripped away by those who had the authority to do so, he was fulfilling the promise she had sought—though not without great grief to himself—if she were willing to use it: specifically, that she should not fall alive into the power of the Romans. Let her, mindful of her father <note>Hasdrubal Gisco, a Carthaginian general</note>, her country, and the two kings to whom she had recently been married, take for herself whatever counsel seemed best."

When she heard this message, she spoke with a steady expression: "I accept this wedding gift; and if no other gift could be given by a husband to his wife, I hold it as a welcome one. But report back that I would have died more honorably if I had not married on the very eve of my funeral."

No sooner had she uttered these sharp words than she took the cup. Showing no sign of fear, she immediately drank it all. Resting but a short time, she collapsed into the wretched death she had sought. 

By Pollux! <note>An ancient Roman oath (Edepol) used here to emphasize the gravity of the event.</note> Even for an aged man for whom life has become a burden and no hope remains except death, it would be remarkable—how much more so for a young royal girl! At an age when she was just entering into the awareness of things and beginning to perceive what sweetness life has to offer, to have met death with such absolute fearlessness is a great and admirable thing, truly worthy of being recorded.

<summary>Masinissa hastily marries Sophonisba to protect her from Rome, but after a rebuke from Scipio Africanus, he sends her poison to allow her to die with dignity rather than be taken as a captive. Sophonisba bravely drinks the poison, preferring death to Roman servitude.</summary>
<keywords>Sophonisba, Masinissa, Scipio Africanus, Laelius, Second Punic War, suicide, poison, Roman captives, Numidia, Carthage, marriage, bravery</keywords>
