<meta>This page transitions from the story of Sophonisba to the account of Theoxena of Thessaly. Both stories highlight the tragic intersection of noble women's lives with the ruthless politics of the Hellenistic world—specifically the conflicts involving Rome and the Kingdom of Macedon.</meta>

# Concerning Theoxena, Daughter of Prince Herodicus

<margin><note>A small lowercase 't' appears in the left margin next to the first line of the main text.</note></margin> Theoxena of Thessaly, a woman of noble origin, left an illustrious testimony of herself to future generations, marked on one hand by sweet piety and on the other by a severe ferocity. She was the daughter of Herodicus, a prince of the Thessalians, during the reign of Philip <note>Philip V (238–179 BCE), the King of Macedon whose expansionist policies led to the Macedonian Wars with Rome.</note>, son of Demetrius, over the Macedonians. She had a sister named Archo, born of the same parents. First, their father was killed by the wickedness of this same Philip; then, in the course of time, their husbands were also driven to a shameful death by his treacherous cruelty. Each sister was left as a widow with a single surviving son.

While they were widows, Archo first married a certain Poris <note>original: *Porridus*</note>, a prince among their people, and bore him many children. Theoxena, however, possessed a more steadfast spirit; though she was sought in marriage by many nobles, she remained a widow for a longer time, her suitors' efforts being in vain. But when Archo was taken by death, Theoxena—moved by compassion for her nephews and fearing they might fall into the hands of a cruel stepmother or be raised with less care by their father—joined herself in marriage to the same Poris so that she might educate them as if they were her own. In those times, no law prohibited such a union. She began to raise them with such pious diligence, no differently than if she had birthed them herself, that it became quite clear she had married Poris more for the sake of the children's welfare than for her own convenience.

While things were in this state, it happened that King Philip of Macedon (for he was of a restless nature) once again planned in his mind a war against the Romans, who were then flourishing in the world with bright prosperity. Because of this, with a massive upheaval throughout his kingdom, he emptied nearly all the maritime cities of Thessaly of their old inhabitants and ordered them to migrate in throngs into the inland region of Paeonia (which was later called Emathia). To the coastal areas, he granted the vacant towns to his Thracian followers <note>Philip replaced the local population with loyalists to ensure the security of his borders against the Roman fleet.</note>, as they were more suited and loyal for the coming war. 

Having heard the curses of those who were being forced to leave, Philip realized that he would never be safe from the sons of the men he had cruelly killed long ago; he decided he must destroy them all. He ordered them to be captured and held in custody, intending not to kill them all at once, but to abolish them one by one over time. It happened that Theoxena heard the edict of the wicked king, and fearing for her own son and her sister’s... <margin><note>A metal paperclip is visible on the right edge of the page.</note></margin>

<summary>Theoxena, a Thessalian noblewoman, marries her widowed brother-in-law Poris to protect her nephews after King Philip V of Macedon murders her father and first husband. The narrative shifts to Philip’s paranoid preparations for war with Rome, including his order to arrest and execute the children of his former victims.</summary>
<keywords>Theoxena, Philip V of Macedon, Herodicus, Thessaly, Poris, Archo, Roman-Macedonian Wars, Paeonia, Emathia, child-rearing, political persecution</keywords>

--- Page 108 ---
<meta>This page continues the account of Theoxena of Thessaly. Having married Poris to protect her nephews after her sister Archo's death, the narrative now shifts to the crisis prompted by King Philip V's decree against the families of his political enemies.</meta>

...it became quite evident that her devotion to her sister’s children was as deep as if she had borne them herself. But this domestic peace was soon shattered by the relentless cruelty of King Philip. Not content with the blood of the fathers he had already spilled, the King issued a terrifying decree <note>original: *edicto*</note> that the children of those nobles he had put to death should be seized and kept in custody.

Upon hearing of this threat, Theoxena was struck with a mother’s terror. She declared that she would rather see the children dead by her own hand than allow them to fall into the King's power to be mistreated or corrupted. She convinced her husband, Poris, that they must flee to save the young ones from this looming shadow. They planned to escape by sea to the city of Athens, seeking sanctuary from the Macedonian reach.

They departed by night from the coast, hoping to reach safety under the cover of darkness. However, fortune was not on their side. A contrary wind arose, stalling their progress, and as the light of dawn began to touch the waves, they were still within sight of land.

<margin>The King’s pursuit</margin>

The King’s guards, who were patrolling the shore to ensure the decree was carried out, soon spotted the fleeing vessel. Following the King's command, **they sent a small boat** <note>original: *lembum* — a fast, light galley often used in the ancient Mediterranean for scouting or pursuit</note>, **those in command** <note>original: *gradu imperantes*</note> **enforcing the edict** with great haste and urgency.

Theoxena, seeing the swift oars of the royal boat closing the distance and realizing that escape was no longer possible, turned to her children. She was a woman of "severe ferocity" when it came to preserving the honor of her house. She produced a cup mixed with poison and drew several daggers, laying them before the children. 

"Death," she told them, "is the only path to freedom remaining to us. Choose your own end, so that you may escape the King's cruelty and the shame of a prison."

<summary>This page describes Theoxena's attempt to flee with her children to escape King Philip V’s decree, only to be pursued by the king's men in a small boat.</summary>

--- Page 109 ---
<meta>This page concludes the story of Theoxena of Thessaly's tragic escape and begins the biography of Queen Beronice (Laodice) of Cappadocia. It follows the moment Theoxena has convinced her family that suicide is the only path to remain free from King Philip V's grasp.</meta>

...it is a delight <note>original: *delectat*</note> to have secured liberty; flee freely into it, since the surges of the stormy sea finally bar the way to life. The enemies were now present, and that fierce woman, the very architect of their death, pressed and urged the hesitating youths. Consequently, some being consumed by one form of death and others by another, they were cast from the ship while still half-alive and gasping for breath, with Theoxena assisting them. 

Because she had driven those she had persuaded toward death for the sake of liberty, she did not want to appear to have kept for herself the very slavery she had convinced others to avoid. With a noble spirit, she embraced her husband—who was still praying—to be her companion in death, and dragged him headlong with her into the stormy sea. She judged it better to die in freedom than to waste away in loathsome servitude while living. Thus, by leaving the ship empty for her enemies, she robbed Philip of the satisfaction of his cruelty, and this stern woman created for herself a monument worthy of remembrance.

[marginal note: a.]
<margin>Concerning Beronice, Queen of Cappadocia.</margin>

[marginal note: b.]
<margin>Beronice of Pontus (who was also called Laodice <note>original: *Leodice*</note>), though she might seem to have sought a place among illustrious women because of the brilliance of her royal blood, is judged to have earned her place far more through the merit of her remarkable audacity in seeking vengeance, rather than for that fervent love for a son which most mothers possess. So that this does not seem to have slipped idly from my pen, it must be explained in a few words.</margin>

She was the daughter of Mithridates, King of Pontus <note>Mithridates V Euergetes</note>, who had waged war against Aristonicus alongside the Romans shortly before, and was eventually snatched away by a sudden death. She married the son of the elder Mithridates <note>Ariarathes VI of Cappadocia</note>, who was both the son and enemy of the long-reigning King. After her husband was killed by a certain Gordius through the treachery of Beronice’s own brother, Mithridates <note>Mithridates VI Eupator, known as "the Great"</note>, two sons survived him. 

However, when Nicomedes, the King of Bithynia at that time, occupied Cappadocia as if it were vacant after the king’s murder, the power-hungry Mithridates feigned family loyalty. Claiming he would recover the kingdom for his nephews, he took up arms against Nicomedes. But when he discovered that the widowed Laodice had actually married Nicomedes, his feigned piety turned into true fury. Having driven Nicomedes out of Cappadocia by force of arms, he restored the ancestral kingdom to Ariarathes, the elder of the brothers.

<meta>Continuing the account of Beronice (Laodice) of Cappadocia. After her husband's death, her brother Mithridates VI of Pontus intervened in the succession, leading to the murder of her sons.</meta>

When he <note>Mithridates VI, the Queen’s brother</note> later, pretending to repent of the deed, had killed him through fraud, a second, younger son named Ariarathes <note>OCR: *Arriaractes*</note> was recalled from Asia by friends. But even as he seemed to begin his reign (as some authorities believe), he too was slaughtered through treachery with the help of that same Mithridates. 

The unhappy mother took the loss of her twin offspring so bitterly that, driven by a grief that made her forget the constraints of her sex, she seized arms in a fury and, having yoked her horses, mounted her chariot. She did not cease her headlong pursuit of the fleeing Ceneus—a royal attendant and the executor of that wicked crime—until she struck him down with a stone, having been unable to reach him with her spear. 

Full of indignation, she drove her chariot over the corpse of the fallen man. Then, amidst the weapons of the enemy and showing no fear of her brother—who was now her foe—she pushed through until she reached the house where she believed the body of her murdered boy was being kept. There, she, a figure of pity, might grant him a mother’s tears and perform the funeral rites <note>original: *officium persolueret funerale*</note>. 

O good God! O the impregnable forces of nature and the strength of unconquered love! What greater or more wonderful thing could you have achieved? You, whom all

<meta>The previous page concluded the tragic account of Queen Beronice of Cappadocia’s vengeance. Lest he should seem to have moved his troops in vain or kept his soldiers idle, once the remnants of the enemy had been cleared from the coastal shores, he <note>Gnaeus Manlius Vulso, the Roman consul who campaigned in Asia Minor in 189 BCE</note> advanced by his own authority into the hidden, mountainous regions. He waged a bitter war against the Galatians <note>original: "gallo grecos"</note>, a people made wild by their own barbarity, because they had aided Antiochus <note>Antiochus III the Great, the Seleucid King</note> with reinforcements against the Romans and had frequently disturbed all of Asia with their raids.

But when the Galatians lost confidence, they abandoned their towns and fled to the mountain peaks, which were naturally fortified by the landscape, taking with them their wives, children, and all their remaining wealth. There, they defended themselves with weapons against the surrounding enemies with all the strength they could muster.

Nevertheless, they were overcome by the tough physical might of the Roman soldiers. They were cast down the mountain slopes and slaughtered; those who survived were brought under Roman rule and acknowledged the victory of Manlius. Among the captives was a vast multitude of both sexes and every age. A Centurion <term>Centurion</term><note>A professional officer of the Roman army, typically commanding a century of around 80 men.</note> put in charge of their custody saw the wife of Ortiagon <note>original: "orgiagōtis"</note>, a minor king. She was in the prime of her life and striking in the beauty of her form. Falling into lust for her and forgetting Roman honor, he used his strength to rape her <note>original: "stuprum intulit"</note>, despite her refusal. The wretched woman endured this indignity from him, a man of noble rank... <note>The text ends mid-sentence; historically, this woman, Chiomara, later took a bloody revenge on the centurion.</note>

--- Page 112 ---
<meta>This page continues the story of Chiomara, the Galatian noblewoman who was raped by a Roman centurion, and begins a new chapter on Tertia Aemilia, the wife of Scipio Africanus.</meta>

...[her] noble heart was driven to such great strength that she did not fear to undergo chains once more if necessary, to be dragged into captivity, to enter a foul prison, or to offer her neck to the executioner's axes. This illustrious woman, the avenger of a wicked crime, commanded her servants' swords to strike off the head of the unlucky rapist. Where indeed could you find a sharper man, a more spirited leader, or a harsher commander against the ill-deserving? Where could you go to find a more shrewd or bold woman, or a more vigilant guardian of matronly honor? This woman saw with remarkable mental foresight that it was better to face certain death than to return to her husband’s house with the uncertainty of disgrace; she realized that only through the greatest daring and risk could she testify that, although her body had been violated, her mind remained unstained. Thus, feminine honor is preserved; thus, when taken, it is recovered; thus, the chaste testimony of the heart is rendered. Therefore, let those who care for illustrious modesty keep this in view: it is not enough to testify to the sincerity of the heart with tears and complaints of having suffered violence, unless one can proceed to vengeance through some remarkable deed.

### Concerning Tertia Aemilia, the wife of Scipio Africanus the Elder
<note>original: "Priami" – This is likely a scribal variant or error for "Primi," meaning "the First" or "the Elder."</note>

**T**ertia Aemilia, although she was already famous both for the nobility of the Aemilian family (from which she drew her splendid origin) and for her marriage to that most serene man, Scipio Africanus the Elder <note>Scipio Africanus (236–183 BCE) was the Roman general who defeated Hannibal.</note>, nevertheless shone with a far greater light because of her own actions. For while Scipio, as a younger man, had once restored an unviolated maiden—a girl in the first flower of youth and of remarkable beauty—to her fiancé, Prince Luceius <note>original: "luceio"</note>, along with the treasures offered by her parents for her ransom, he found that as an older man he was unable to steal himself away from the allurements of forbidden lust. Instead, he fell into an attraction and a sexual relationship with his own servant girl. Since it is extremely difficult to deceive the sharp eyes of a devoted wife, this could not remain hidden from Tertia; indeed, as time passed, she learned everything. And who could doubt that she bore this most bitterly? For some assert that, setting aside all modesty of speech, nothing is more injurious or more intolerable to a bride...

<meta>This page continues the narrative concerning Tertia Aemilia, the wife of Scipio Africanus the Elder. The previous page established her noble lineage and marriage, and this page details her reaction to her husband's infidelity.</meta>

...[nothing more difficult] can happen to a woman than that which she calls her own by the right of the marriage bed <note>original: "iure thori"</note> should be granted by her husband to another woman. And I, by Pollux! <note>original: "edepol" — a common Latin oath invoking the god Pollux, used here to emphasize the author's agreement.</note>, will easily believe it. For whether it arises from the fragility of her sex, or from having too high an opinion of herself, woman is a most suspicious creature <note>original: "suspiciosissimum animal" — while "animal" translates literally, in this context it refers to a living being or creature; the author reflects the period's gendered views on temperament.</note>. She judges immediately that whatever is done by her husband with another woman results in the loss of the love owed to herself. 

But however difficult this seemed, the illustrious woman bore it with a steadfast heart. She suppressed her knowledge of her husband’s crime with such great silence that not only did no one else perceive it, but not even her husband himself realized that she had noticed what he was doing. This discreet wife considered it exceedingly unbecoming for it to be publicly known that he, who by his illustrious virtue had subdued kings and powerful nations, was himself subject to a love for a common slave-girl <note>original: "ancillule" — a diminutive form of "ancilla" (slave-woman), implying a person of very low social status compared to Scipio's greatness.</note>. 

Nor did it seem enough to this most holy woman to have kept this secret while Scipio was alive; indeed, even after he was dead, she wished to remove the seductive stain from her husband’s memory, if in any way she could have atoned for the crime from another source... <note>The text ends mid-sentence; the author is beginning to describe how Tertia Aemilia treated the slave-girl after Scipio’s death to further protect his reputation.</note>

<summary>Tertia Aemilia discovers her husband Scipio Africanus’s affair with a slave but chooses to hide her knowledge of it to protect his heroic reputation. Her silence is presented as a supreme act of wifely devotion and Roman virtue.</summary>

<meta>This page transitions from the life of Tertia Aemilia, a woman of the Scipio family, to two other notable women of antiquity: Oripetrua (historically known as Hypsicrateia) and Sempronia, the sister of the famous Gracchi brothers.</meta>

# Concerning Oripetrua, Queen of Laodicea

We read that Oripetrua was the Queen of Laodicea and the daughter of Mithridates the Great <note>original: "mitridatis." Mithridates VI of Pontus was a legendary enemy of Rome.</note>. While the loyalty she showed to her household gods <note>original: "penatib⁹" — the Penates were Roman spirits of the pantry and household, here used metaphorically for her family and home.</note> made her commendable, in my judgment, a certain unheard-of physical wonder made her even more memorable than her parentage. For (if we are to believe the ancient books) she was born with a double row of teeth <note>original: "gemio dētiū ordīe"</note>, which provided a monstrous spectacle to all the people of Asia during her lifetime. And although she suffered no impediment in chewing from such an unusual quantity of teeth, she was not without a notable deformity. Nevertheless, she lived with praiseworthy loyalty, as has already been touched upon. For when her father, Mithridates, was defeated by Pompey the Great, she followed him constantly, indulging in no rest and shrinking from no dangers or labors. By her exceptionally faithful service, she testified that the crimes of parents should not be imputed to their children.

# Concerning Sempronia, relative of the Gracchi

Sempronia was the daughter of Tiberius Sempronius Gracchus, a man who was most illustrious in his own time. She was born to Cornelia, the daughter of Scipio Africanus the Elder, and was furthermore the wife of the splendid Scipio Aemilianus <note>Scipio Aemilianus was the Roman general who finally destroyed Carthage in 146 BCE.</note>, who later attained his grandfather's surname because of that victory. She was also the sister of Tiberius and Gaius Gracchus, and she did not deviate from her ancestors in the greatness and constancy of her spirit. 

For they say it happened to her, after her brothers were slain because of their political seditions, that she was dragged to trial by a tribune of the plebs before the assembled people—certainly an event of great mental distress. There, however, with the crowd and the power of the tribunes all pressing her to kiss a certain Equitius (a man from Firmum in Picenum), she was being forced to accept him into the Sempronian family as if he were her nephew and the son of her brother Tiberius Gracchus. Although she stood in that place where even great leaders were accustomed to tremble, and though she was being driven and threatened by the discordant shouts of the impetuous multitude and the grim face of the high authority of the tribunes standing against her, her womanly constancy was in no way broken. On the contrary, she remembered that her brother Tiberius had only three sons <note>The Gracchi family was one of the most powerful and controversial in Rome; Sempronia's refusal to recognize a false claimant was a significant act of family integrity.</note>. One of these had died as a youth while serving in the army in Sardinia, and another...

# Concerning Claudia Quinta, a Roman Matron

...she was observed with such finery and excessive attention to her appearance <note>original: "ornatu ac oris cultu nimio"</note> that she appeared resplendent; however, by the more serious matrons, she was judged to be not only less than honorable, but even less than chaste.

Indeed, during the consulship of Marcus Cornelius and Publius Sempronius—specifically in the fifteenth year of the Second Punic War <note>This was 204 BCE. The Second Punic War was the life-or-death struggle between Rome and Hannibal of Carthage.</note>—it happened that the Mother of the Gods <note>original: "deum mater." This refers to the goddess Cybele, whose sacred cult stone was being brought to Rome from Phrygia to fulfill a prophecy that she would help defeat Carthage.</note> arrived at the mouth of the Tiber. To receive her from the ship—when, according to the response of the oracle, Nasica <note>Publius Cornelius Scipio Nasica was chosen by the Senate as the "best man in Rome" to welcome the goddess.</note> had been chosen by the entire Senate of the whole city...

<meta>This page continues the story of Claudia Quinta during the arrival of the goddess Cybele in Rome. It picks up mid-sentence from the previous page, where Scipio Nasica and the Roman matrons go to meet the ship.</meta>

...as the man judged the best of the city <note>original: "urbis vir optimus iudicatus"</note>, along with all the matrons, they went as far as the place where the approaching ship arrived. While the sailors were striving to reach the shore, the ship that carried the sacred image <note>The "simulacrum" or cult statue of the Mother of the Gods, Cybele.</note> became stuck in a shallow sandbank <note>original: "vado"</note> of the river. And when it was seen that the ship could not be moved, even with a great multitude of young men pulling it, Claudia—who was mingled among the other matrons and conscious of her own virtue—publicly knelt down and, as a suppliant, prayed to the goddess.

She prayed that if the goddess judged her to be chaste <note>original: "castam"</note>, she would follow her belt <note>original: "cingulum"</note>. Then, immediately rising up with confidence and hoping that what she had sought in prayer would come to pass, she ordered her belt to be tied to the ship and all the young men to be moved away from it.

No sooner was this done than Claudia, by pulling, very easily dislodged the ship from the shallows; and while everyone watched in amazement, she drew it in the direction she desired. From this most miraculous success, it followed immediately that the public opinion of everyone regarding her preserved modesty <note>original: "pudicitia"</note> was turned into praise for Claudia, quite the opposite of the previous suspicion. And so, she who [had come] to the shore under shameful [repute] for wantonness...

<meta>This page continues the biography of Hypsicratea, Queen of Pontus, and begins the account of Sempronia, a Roman noblewoman associated with the Catilinarian conspiracy.</meta>

[she] brought such comfort that, in whatever anxieties he was driven, he seemed to be refreshed as if in his own marriage bed. O heart, the sanctuary of marital sweetness! O inexhaustible power of friendship! With what holy strength you bolstered this womanly spirit. Certainly, no wife has ever endured similar, let alone more grievous, things for her husband. Because of this merit, antiquity was accustomed to marvel at her perpetual praise, and posterity will be able to do the same through me. 

Yet, despite so many labors and such renowned, well-deserved loyalty, the woman did not receive a sufficiently worthy reward from her husband. For when he was already an old man—having in anger killed the son he had fathered by her—and while the strength of his honors was still flourishing, he had withdrawn not only into his kingdom but into his palace. While he was intending great things in his mind and attempting to rouse various and distant nations to war against the Romans through embassies, he was besieged by his son Pharnaces, who rebelled against him because of his cruelty toward his sons and friends. When he saw this and found his son to be inexorable, judging it to be the final ruin of his affairs, he took Hypsicratea—who had provided so many aids to his life through her labor—along with his remaining wives, concubines, and daughters, and killed her with poison <note>original: "veneno absumpsit"</note> so that she would not survive him. 

Truly, the ungrateful deed of Mithridates could not diminish the deserved glory of Hypsicratea. Her mortal body was taken away by an untimely death by poison, but her name will live on through the monuments of venerable literature even to us and in perpetual, celebrated fame; indeed, she cannot be cheated of her legacy by the long passage of posterity.

Her wit was so ready and so versatile that she immediately understood and carried out, even by imitation, whatever she had seen someone else say or do at any time. For the sake of their chastity, by casting away a few years—during which they might have survived their hanging—they sought a most enduring life in memory. Thus, they left behind a cause for wonder to posterity: namely, that so great a multitude of women, without any formal agreement or public council, within the space of a single night, had all arrived at the same sentence of death, as if one and the same spirit had lived within them all.

## Concerning Porcia, daughter of Cato of Utica.

Porcia was the daughter of that Marcus Cato <note>Marcus Porcius Cato Uticensis, known as Cato the Younger (95–46 BCE), a Roman statesman famous for his Stoic resolve and his stubborn opposition to Julius Caesar.</note>. It was he who, after leading the remnants of Pompey's army out of Egypt and through the burning deserts of Libya into Africa, killed himself at Utica <note>original: "apd vticam se occidit"; Cato committed suicide to avoid living under Caesar's dictatorship, earning him the name "Uticensis."</note>, as he was unable to endure Caesar's victory. Indeed, this distinguished woman did not seem to have fallen away in any respect from her father's courage and perseverance.

Now this woman (setting aside her other outstanding virtues) had married Marcus Brutus <note>The transcription reads "tertio Bruto," which may be a scribe's error for "M. Bruto" (Marcus Brutus) or a reference to him as her subsequent husband; historically, Brutus was her second husband.</note> while her father was still alive. She loved him with such integrity and purity that, among the many concerns of women, her devotion to him was by far her first and foremost priority. Nor could she hide the honest flames of love within her chaste breast when the time was right. Since these events turned out to her everlasting praise, they offer themselves freely to enhance her fame.

The pestilential chaos of the civil wars had already quieted down, with Pompey's supporters crushed everywhere by Caesar. However, when a more sensible part of the Senate conspired against him—since it was clearly observed that as perpetual dictator he was aiming for kingship—Brutus himself was among them. Brutus, who was aware of Porcia's integrity, shared the secret of the wicked plot with her. It happened that on the night before the day dawned when Caesar was to be removed from human affairs by the aid of the conspirators, as Brutus was leaving the bedroom, Porcia took a barber's razor. She pretended she was going to trim her nails, but then, feigning that it had fallen by accident, she intentionally wounded herself deeply.

Indeed, when the handmaidens standing by saw the blood flow, they suspected something more serious and raised a cry. Brutus, who was just leaving, was called back into the room. Porcia was scolded by him because she had usurped the barber's duty <note>In Roman households, trimming nails was typically a task performed by a servant or barber; Brutus initially thinks her injury is a result of clumsy meddling in a servant's job.</note>. But Porcia, once the handmaidens were sent away, said: "This was not done as rashly as you think. I did this to test the spirit with which I might pierce myself with a sword and endure death, should your undertakings not succeed according to your hopes."

O love of tireless vigor! Happy is the husband with such a life-partner! But the conspirators proceeded further into the crime; though the killers escaped after Caesar was slain, they did not do so with impunity. For when everything turned out contrary to what they expected from the Senate, the rest of the condemned parricides <term>patricide: literally 'father-killers'; here used to denote those who killed Caesar, who was officially titled 'Father of the Fatherland'</term> went their separate ways. Brutus and Cassius, heading toward the East, gathered significant forces against Octavian Caesar and Antony, Caesar's heirs. Against them, when Octavian and Antony had led out an army, the battle was fought at Philippi...

<meta>This page continues the biography of Porcia, focusing on her death following the defeat of the conspirators at the Battle of Philippi. It then introduces the story of Curia (historically known as Turia), who protected her husband during the proscriptions of the Second Triumvirate.</meta>

...it was. And when the factions of Cassius and Brutus had been defeated and put to flight, and Brutus himself was also slain, Porcia heard the news. Believing that no joy remained for her once her husband was taken away, and judging that she would endure death with no less spirit than when she had previously tested her resolve by wounding herself with a barber's razor <note>original: "cultri tonsorij vuln⁹"; Porcia had famously stabbed her own thigh to prove to Brutus that she was strong enough to keep the secret of the conspiracy against Caesar.</note>, she sought a way to end her life. When she saw that no instrument for a voluntary death was provided to her as quickly as her grief demanded, she unhesitatingly snatched up glowing coals <note>historical note: Ancient sources like Plutarch and Valerius Maximus record that Porcia committed suicide by swallowing hot coals when her friends removed all weapons from her reach.</note> that happened to be nearby and swallowed them into her throat. As these burned her vitals, her life force was forced to depart into death. There is no doubt that the more unusual her manner of death was, the more it bestowed a brilliance of marital devotion upon those who encounter her story. Not even the wound inflicted by her father's hands <note>original: "patris referatū manibus vulnus"; a reference to her father Cato the Younger, who committed suicide by disemboweling himself after his defeat at Utica.</note> could take away any of the deserved praise for her own courage.

## Concerning Curia, wife of Quintus Lucrecius.

<note>A metal clip is attached to the left margin of the page, likely used by a later researcher or librarian to mark the section.</note>

<margin>c</margin>
Curia <note>This figure is almost certainly "Turia," the subject of the famous "Laudatio Turiae," a tombstone inscription where a husband praises his wife's bravery during the civil wars.</note> was a Roman woman and, if we give credit to her name, she was from the lineage of the Curiones. She was a splendid example of wondrous constancy and the most profound loyalty of antiquity. For when, during the upheaval of affairs, the proscription lists <note>term: "proscribed" (original: "p̄scipt⁹"); during the Roman civil wars, the names of political enemies were posted on public lists (proscriptions). Anyone on the list could be killed for a reward, and their property was seized.</note> were posted at the command of the Triumvirs <note>The Second Triumvirate consisted of Octavian (the future Augustus), Mark Antony, and Lepidus.</note>, her husband Quintus Lucrecius <note>Quintus Lucretius Vespillo, who survived the proscriptions and later became consul in 19 BCE.</note> found himself among those named. While many others abandoned their native soil in swift flight, and men sought safe hiding places among the dens of wild beasts and the solitudes of mountains, or even among the enemies of the Roman name, he alone—by the advice and help of his most loving wife—remained hidden within the walls of Rome. Inside the walls of his own home, in the secret of the marital bedchamber and in the very embrace of his wife, he lay hidden without fear. He was preserved by such skill from his wife, such wise industry, and such integrity of faith, that except for one young maidservant who was in on the secret, no one—not even among his close relatives—was able to know his whereabouts. We can believe how many times, in order to conceal her deed with artifice, this woman went out in public in worn-out clothing and filthy dress, with a mournful face and weeping eyes. Her hair was neglected and she wore none of the customary adornments; her breast was heavy with sighs, and she moved with a feigned stupor of madness, as if unaware of her own self. She ran about the forum, entered temples, and wandered the squares. With a trembling and broken voice, while she appeared to be honoring the gods with prayers and vows, she would ask friends she encountered if they had ever seen her Lucrecius, or if they knew...

<meta>This page concludes the account of Curia (Turia) and her devotion to her husband Quintus Lucretius Vespillo during the Roman proscriptions. It then transitions to the biography of Hortensia, a Roman woman famous for her oratory.</meta>

...[whether he] were alive, and if he lived, where he had taken flight, with what companions, and with what hope. Furthermore, she claimed that she longed to become a companion for her husband in his flight, his exile, and his manner of living. She performed many such actions, as those who are unhappy are accustomed to do, which served as powerful covers for the man’s hiding places. 

Beyond this, with what charms, what soothing words, and what suggestions she must have strengthened the minds of her little servant girls who were privy to the secret, making them as loyal as if they were his own wives! With what consolations did she raise the hope of her trembling husband’s anxious breast and animate his mourning heart so that he might experience some measure of joy? And so, while others were suffering under the same plague <note>The "plague" refers to the proscriptions of the Second Triumvirate, a period of state-sanctioned murder and property seizure.</note> and were wretchedly perishing among the rugged mountains, the boiling tides of the sea, the storms of heaven, the betrayals and hatred of enemies, and sometimes at the hands of those pursuing them, Lucretius alone was kept safe and secure in the lap of his most devoted wife. By this most skillful deed, Curia sought for herself a not undeserved and eternal fame.

## Concerning Hortensia, daughter of Quintus Hortensius.

Hortensia, the daughter of the eminent orator Quintus Hortensius <note>Quintus Hortensius Hortalus (114–50 BCE) was a celebrated Roman orator and the primary rival of Cicero.</note>, is to be extolled with worthy praises. She not only embraced the eloquence of her father with a lively heart, but she even preserved the vigor of his public speaking when the opportunity demanded—a quality that very often is found lacking even in the most learned men. 

In the time of the Triumvirs <note>The Second Triumvirate, consisting of Octavian, Mark Antony, and Lepidus, who ruled Rome together from 43 to 33 BCE.</note>, when a multitude of matrons seemed burdened by an almost intolerable weight of money to be paid out due to the necessity of the Republic <note>The Triumvirs attempted to tax the 1,400 wealthiest women in Rome to fund the war against the assassins of Julius Caesar.</note>, it appeared that no man would move to dare offer patronage <note>In Rome, women were typically represented in legal matters by male relatives or patrons.</note> in such an unsuitable matter. She alone dared, with a constant mind, to take up the cause of the women before the Triumvirs. She pleaded the case so effectively with her inexhaustible eloquence that, to the great admiration of the audience, it was believed that Hortensius had returned to life, with his sex changed. 

Nor was this most excellent work, undertaken and executed by a woman, unsuccessful. For just as she had failed in no part of her speech, nor in the praiseworthy demonstration of her legal rights, so too it was not merely some small part of what she hoped for that was granted by the Triumvirs. On the contrary, it was freely conceded that a far larger portion of the imposed tax was removed at her discretion. Just as much as silence is seen as praiseworthy in public under a matron's robe <note>original: "sub matronali stola"; the stola was the traditional long garment of a respectable Roman married woman, symbolizing her status and modesty.</note>, so too should her eloquence be extolled, which was so fittingly adorned when the occasion demanded it. When this was finally done, not without the greatest glory for Hortensia, the remainder of what was required was easily collected from the matrons. What more shall I say...

## Concerning Sulpitia, the wife of Truscellio.

Sulpitia, the wife of Lentulus Truscellio <note>Historically identified as Lucius Cornelius Lentulus Cruscellio, a Roman official who fled to Sicily to join Sextus Pompey.</note>, gained an indelible fame for herself through nearly equal devotion. For this same Lentulus had been proscribed by the Triumvirs <note>The Second Triumvirate consisted of Octavian, Mark Antony, and Lepidus, who published lists of "proscribed" enemies to be executed and stripped of property.</note> during the same storm mentioned above. When he had secured his safety by a swift flight to Sicily and was lingering there as an impoverished exile, Sulpitia, having been informed of the situation, resolved that she wished to suffer his hardships along with her husband. She judged it unseemly for wives to share in joyful honors and bright fortune with their husbands, yet refuse to share their calamities when flight became necessary. However, it was not easy for Sulpitia to go to her husband. Indeed, she was watched with the greatest diligence by her mother, Julia, so that she might not follow him into exile. But what true love does not mock its guards? Therefore, having seized the right moment and put on the clothes of a servant <note>term: "servile vestis" (servant's clothing). High-born Roman women used such disguises to travel unnoticed during times of civil unrest.</note>, she deceived her mother's guards and some...

## Concerning Cornificia the poet.

Whether Cornificia was a Roman or of foreign birth, I do not recall. However, by the testimony of the ancients, she was most worthy of being remembered. During the reign of Caesar Octavian <note>Augustus, the first Roman Emperor.</note>, she shone with such poetic skill that she seemed not to have been nourished by Italian milk, but by the waters of the Castalian spring <note>A spring on Mount Parnassus sacred to the Muses; Boccaccio suggests her talent was divine/artistic rather than merely biological.</note>. She was the sister of Cornificius, her own brother, who was himself an outstanding poet and to whom she was an equal in illustrious glory. Not content with having mastered such a splendid faculty for words, and with the Muses urging her toward the poetry of Mount Helicon <note>The mythical home of the Muses.</note>, she frequently set her learned hands to the pen, having cast aside the distaff <note>term: "distaff" (colo). A tool used in spinning wool, representing the traditional domestic duties expected of women.</note>. She wrote many epigrams with a quill and with great depth of thought. These works, in the time of the illustrious and most holy Jerome <note>Saint Jerome (c. 342–420 AD), the scholar who translated the Bible into Latin.</note>, were still widely acclaimed, as he himself testifies. Whether they have survived to these later ages, I am not entirely certain.

O, feminine glory! To have neglected womanly trifles and applied her talent to the studies of the great poets. Let the lazy be ashamed, and those who miserably despair of themselves, who—as if born only for leisure and the bedroom—persuade themselves that they are capable of nothing but the embraces of men and the bearing and nursing of children. Yet, all things that make men glorious are held in common with women, if only they were willing to toil in study. This woman was able, by not casting aside her strength but through her intellect and long nights of study, to surpass the female sex. Through honest labor, she sought an eternal name for herself—not an inglorious one, but one that remains rare and excellent even for the few men who achieve it.

## Concerning Mariamne, Queen of the Jews.

Mariamne was a Hebrew woman, more fortunate in her lineage than in her husband. She was born to Alexandra, the daughter of King Hyrcanus, and her father was Aristobulus, King of the Jews <note>Mariamne was a member of the Hasmonean dynasty; her husband was Herod the Great.</note>. She was famous for such great and unheard-of beauty that she was believed not only to surpass all other women of that time in loveliness, but was considered a celestial image rather than a mortal one. Nor was the testimony of Mark Antony, the Triumvir, lacking to support this belief. Now, Mariamne had a brother from the same parents named Aristobulus, who was her equal in beauty and age. When their mother Alexandra [desired] that the high priesthood be given to him...

<meta>Continuity: This page transitions from the domestic disputes of Herod’s court to the broader geopolitical history of Egypt and the rise of Cleopatra VII, whose life and ambitions significantly impacted Herod's reign and the Roman world.</meta>

Let us begin with Dionysus <note>original: "dyonisius." This refers to Ptolemy XII Auletes, the father of the famous Cleopatra VII, who identified himself with the god Dionysus.</note>, also called Mineus, a very close friend of the Roman people. Upon meeting his death during the first consulship of Julius Caesar, he left behind sealed tablets <note>term: "sealed tablets" (signatis tabulis). A formal Roman-style will or legal testament.</note> decreeing that the eldest of his sons (whom some believe was named Ptolemy) should reign after his death, having taken as his wife the eldest of his daughters, Cleopatra. This was carried out because such a custom—marrying brothers and sisters—was very common among the Egyptians, although they excluded mothers and daughters from such unions.

Furthermore, Cleopatra, burning with a lust for power—as it appeared to some—consumed the innocent youth, who was both her brother and her husband, with poison when he was in his fifteenth year of age, and she alone took possession of the kingdom. From here they assert that when Pompey the Great had already occupied nearly all of Asia with his arms and was heading into Egypt, he substituted a surviving boy for the dead brother and made him king of Egypt; because of this, the indignant Cleopatra took up arms against him. 

And so, with matters standing thus, after Pompey was defeated by Caesar at Thessaly <note>original: "apud thesaliā." Refers to the Battle of Pharsalus in 48 BCE, where Julius Caesar defeated Pompey the Great.</note> and the boy-king he had appointed was killed on the Egyptian shore, Caesar arrived after him and found the factions there waging war against each other. When Caesar ordered them to be summoned before him to plead their case—so that we might know the truth regarding the young Ptolemy—the woman Cleopatra, unrestrained in her cunning, first trusting in her own merits, approached him with the marks of royal insignia. 

And having envisioned the kingdom for herself if she could draw the conqueror of the world into her own wantonness—since she was most beautiful and, through the art of her sparkling eyes and the eloquence of her speech, could capture almost whomever she wished—with little effort she drew the lustful prince into her intimate company. She shared many nights with him in the midst of the Alexandrian tumult, and she conceived (as almost everyone agrees) a son whom she later named Caesarion <note>The only biological son of Julius Caesar, later executed by Augustus.</note> after his father’s name. 

Finally, when the boy Ptolemy, having been released by Caesar, turned to war against his own liberator at the instigation of his followers, he ran to meet Mithridates of Pergamon who was coming with an army to help Caesar. And there, having been overcome by Caesar (who had anticipated his arrival by another route), the boy attempted to flee by boat <note>original: "stapha," likely a corruption of "scapha," a small skiff or boat.</note>. Pressed down by the weight of the many men rushing onto the vessel, he was submerged and drowned. 

And so, with affairs pacified and the surrender of the Alexandrians completed, when Caesar was about to set out against Pharnaces, the King of Pontus <note>original: "pharnacē ponti regem." Pharnaces II, whom Caesar defeated so quickly he famously wrote "Veni, vidi, vici."</note> (who had been an ally of Pompey), the kingdom of Egypt fell to Cleopatra. This was granted first because of the intimacy of those nights, and also because she had remained faithful to him, desiring nothing else. Her sister Arsinoe was led away as a captive, lest perhaps with her as a leader a new rebellion might be plotted. Thus, Cleopatra, having obtained the kingdom through a double crime...

### Concerning Antonia, the Daughter of Antony.

**Antonia the Younger** <note>Antonia Minor (36 BCE – 37 CE), a key figure in the Julio-Claudian dynasty.</note> left behind an indelible example of most illustrious widowhood for posterity. She was the daughter of the triumvir **Mark Antony** and **Octavia**, as is believed; she was thus called "the Younger" because she had an elder sister of the same name. She married **Drusus**, the brother of **Tiberius Nero** and the stepson of **Octavian Augustus**. By him, she gave birth to **Germanicus**, **Claudius** (who was later Emperor), and **Livilla**.

While Drusus was occupied with the Germanic expedition, he perished by poison (as some believe) through the help of his brother Tiberius. After his death, although she flourished in the prime of her life and with conspicuous beauty, she believed it was honorable enough for a woman to have married once <note>original: "nupsisse semel." The Roman ideal of the *univira*—a woman who married only once—was a mark of high virtue.</note>, and she could be provoked by no one to a second marriage. Instead, she spent the remaining space of her life under her mother-in-law **Livia**, within the limits of her husband's bedchamber, so chastely and so holily that she surpassed the praises of all past matrons through her celebrated widowhood.

Indeed, among the likes of the **Cincinnatuses**, **Fabriciuses**, **Curiuses**, **Lucretiuses**, and **Sulpiciuses** <note>These are names of legendary Roman heroes known for their frugality, integrity, and ancient Republican virtue.</note>, it is a most holy and splendid thing—even for women advanced in age and the daughters of **Cato**—to have endured life without a mark of wantonness; they should be extolled with the highest praise. If this is so, with what proclamations shall we honor a young woman, distinguished by beauty and the daughter of Mark Antony (a most filthy man), who preserved her chastity? 

She did so not in woods or solitudes, but amidst imperial leisure and delights; among the glowing fires of lust and wantonness of **Julia** <note>Likely Julia the Elder, the daughter of Augustus, whose scandalous behavior led to her exile.</note>, the daughter of **Octavia** <note>The text says "Julia, daughter of Octavia," though Julia was the daughter of Augustus. This may be an error in the manuscript or a reference to her being raised in Octavia’s household.</note>, and **Julia** the daughter of **Marcus Agrippa**; amidst the obscenities and disgraces of her father Mark Antony and later the Emperor **Tiberius**; in a fatherland once frugal but now surrendered to all foulness. Among a thousand examples of concupiscence, with a constant mind and a brave heart, she preserved her purity—not for a brief moment or in hope of a future marriage, but in obedience to virtue, right through to old age and death. And by Pollux, nothing remains in words worthy enough; perhaps something remains for contemplation. Since this indeed exceeds the strength of writers, let it be enough to have left it to sacred geniuses to consider and to extol her merits through such consideration.

### Concerning Agrippina, the Wife of Germanicus.

<margin>Agrippina was born to Marcus Agrippa from Julia, the daughter of Octavian Caesar.</margin> **Agrippina** was the daughter of **Marcus Agrippa** and **Julia**, the daughter of **Octavian Caesar**. It is said that **Gaius Caligula**, the son of this same **Agrippina**, once he had become the ruler of the world, detested the low-born rusticity of his maternal grandfather **Agrippa**. He used to claim that she had not been born from **Agrippa**, but from an act of incest <note>original: "stupro"</note> committed by **Octavian** upon his own daughter **Julia**. He foolishly desired it to be believed that he was of more noble birth, being born of a mother conceived through incest, rather than one fathered by an ignoble man in a lawful marriage.

But regardless of whose daughter she may have been, she was married to **Germanicus**, the most illustrious young man of his age and a great asset to the state, who was the adopted son of **Tiberius Caesar Augustus**. For this reason, she was quite distinguished, and she would have been even more so had she not encountered the treachery of a most insolent prince <note>A reference to the Emperor Tiberius, who was often depicted by later historians as a paranoid and cruel tyrant.</note> due to his stubborn resolve. By her husband **Germanicus**, she had already given birth to three sons—among whom was **Gaius Caligula**, who later ruled Rome—and an equal number of daughters, including the **Agrippina** who was the mother of **Nero Caesar**.

When her husband, that most celebrated man, was removed by poison (as was widely believed) with the help of his father **Tiberius**, she took it heavily. Mourning the murder of her husband with great wailing in the manner of women, she fell into such hatred with **Tiberius** that he seized her by the arm and tormented her with reproaches until she wept. This was because she bore it too impatiently that she was not permitted to rule the state <note>Agrippina was known for her "virile" ambition and her belief that her children, as direct descendants of Augustus, had a better claim to the throne than Tiberius.</note>. After being harassed by many accusations of plots as time went on, he ordered her, though innocent, to be kept under guard.

Furthermore, this extraordinary woman, judging the treatment she received from **Caesar** to be unworthy, resolved to escape or end the disdain of the resentful prince through death. Since no other way was conveniently available, she determined with a noble spirit to summon death by hunger, and she immediately began to abstain from all food. When this was reported to **Tiberius**, and that heartless man realized where the woman’s fasting was leading—lest she withdraw herself from his injuries by such a certain path in so short a time—he found that threats and beatings were of no use in making her eat. It reached such a point, lest he lose the subject of his cruelty, that he ordered food to be violently forced down her throat <note>Tiberius reportedly ordered his guards to pry her mouth open to force-feed her, a detail meant to highlight his sadistic nature.</note>, so that in whatever way it was thrust into her stomach, it might provide nourishment even against her will.

But the more **Agrippina** was provoked by these injuries, the more resolute she became in her purpose. Persevering in her undertaking, she overcame the insolence of the wicked prince by dying. She showed that while he could easily kill many whenever he wished, he could not, with all the power of his dominion, keep a single person alive who was determined to die.

### Concerning Paulina, a Roman Woman.

**Paulina** was a certain Roman woman who achieved an indelible name through her own ridiculous simplicity <note>The author uses "simplicity" here to mean a naive or foolish gullibility, setting the stage for a story where she is easily deceived by her own religious fervor.</note>. While **Tiberius Caesar Augustus** was ruling the Romans, she was held in high regard; being thus married, she was considered by everyone to be a model of celebrated chastity. She cared for nothing other than devotion to her husband, except especially that she might be obedient to **Anubis**, the god of the Egyptians <note>Anubis was the jackal-headed Egyptian god of the dead. His cult, alongside that of the goddess Isis, had become very fashionable among wealthy Roman women during this period, despite being viewed as "foreign" and suspicious by the Roman establishment.</note>, whom she worshipped with total veneration so that she might deserve his favor. Indeed, since everywhere beautiful women are accustomed to be desired and loved by young men—and especially those women who possess a diligent care for both chastity and beauty—one young man named **Mundus** <note>Decius Mundus, a Roman knight of the equestrian order.</note> pursued her intensely, now with gestures of his eyes and witty charms, now with prayers...

<summary>This page concludes the life of Agrippina the Elder, noting how her death shamed Tiberius, and introduces Paulina, a Roman woman famous for her beauty, chastity, and gullible devotion to the god Anubis.</summary>
<keywords>Agrippina the Elder, Tiberius, Paulina, Decius Mundus, Anubis, Roman religion, chastity, Egyptian cults</keywords>

--- Page 137 ---
<meta>This page continues the story of Paulina, who has been tricked into believing the god Anubis desires her. It details the resolution of that scandal and begins the biography of Agrippina the Younger, the mother of the emperor Nero.</meta>

...[that the god had] descended and come for her embrace, so that from him and her a god like himself might be born. Before anything else, she asked her lover-god whether the heavenly beings were able or accustomed to mingle with mortals. To this, Mundus <note>original: "mundus"; Decius Mundus is here impersonating the god Anubis.</note> immediately replied that it was possible, and that even the youth <note>Mundus refers to himself in the third person to maintain the disguise.</note> had entered through the rules <note>Likely referring to the temple's ritual protocols or physical entrance.</note> and was afterwards taken up into heaven. 

Having heard these things, **Paulina** joyfully granted the request. Mundus entered the bed, stripped bare in the guise of **Anubis**, and enjoyed the desired embrace and union. But as night was turning into day, he said as he was leaving in the light that she had conceived a son. In the morning, after the bed had been removed from the temple by the priests, **Paulina** told her husband what had happened. 

The foolish man believed it and applauded his wife for being about to give birth to a god. Nor was there any doubt that the time of birth would have been expected by both, had not the young **Mundus**, burning with too much passion, incautiously revealed the trick. He, perhaps conscious that she had come greedily to his embrace and union, thought that if he showed her that her modesty had been laid low by his cleverness, she would be more flexible and equally eager for another night; and thus, in an easier way, he might return again and more often to those desired embraces. 

Meeting **Paulina** as she went to the temple, he said in a low voice: "You are blessed, **Paulina**, since you have received from me, the god **Anubis**." But truly, it turned out far differently than he expected from those words. For when **Paulina** stood stunned and recalled many things in her mind from what had been done and heard, she immediately sensed the fraud. Distraught, she returned to her husband and revealed to him the trickery of **Mundus** and the priests as she understood it. It followed from this that the husband complained to **Tiberius**, by whom, once the fraud was discovered, it was arranged that the priests should be punished with death <note>The Roman historian Josephus records that Tiberius had the priests crucified and the temple of Isis destroyed.</note>, **Mundus** should be punished with exile, and the mocked **Paulina** became a story among the Roman common people. She became more famous for her simplicity <note>In this context, "simplicity" implies a naive or foolish gullibility.</note> and the fraud of **Mundus** than from her devotion to **Anubis** or her chastity, which she had guarded so skillfully.

### Concerning Agrippina, the Mother of Nero Caesar.

**Agrippina**, the mother of **Nero Caesar**, was distinguished by her wicked lineage, the monstrosity of her son, and her own no less famous crimes. For she was the daughter of **Germanicus Caesar**—a young man of the best and most praiseworthy character—and the elder **Agrippina**. She was called **Julia Agrippina**, and was the sister of the emperor **Gaius Caligula**. She married **Gnaeus Domitius**, a man from the **Ahenobarbus** <note>original: "enobardorum"; the Ahenobarbi were a powerful Roman noble family. The name literally means "bronze-beard."</note> family who was most arrogant and severe.

--- Page 138 ---
<meta>This page continues the biography of Agrippina the Younger, shifting from the scandalous story of Paulina on the previous page to the lineage and rise of the woman who would become the mother of Nero.</meta>

...from whom she brought forth **Nero**, that beast notable to the whole world, who emerged from his mother's womb feet first <note>original: "praemissis... pedibus"; in Roman belief, a breach birth was considered an ill omen, signaling a difficult and unnatural life for the child.</note>. But after her husband **Domitius** died in the interim, she took up the care of **Nero** while he was still a small child. Since she was a woman of great beauty, her brother **Gaius** <note>Gaius Caesar, better known by his nickname Caligula.</note>, a most filthy man, abused her with shameful debauchery. At that time, either because the Emperor's mind was sharpened against her, or because he disapproved of her morals for joining with **Lepidus** in the hope of seizing power, or perhaps driven by some rival, he stripped her of almost all her goods and exiled her to an island. 

When **Gaius** was finally slaughtered by his own soldiers and **Claudius** was substituted in his place, she was recalled by him. In the course of time, when she heard that **Valeria Messalina** <note>The third wife of Emperor Claudius, executed for her scandalous behavior and conspiracy.</note> [was dead], having been driven to her end by her various deeds, **Agrippina** immediately recognized her sole hope: to provide for herself and her son within the rule of the empire. 

She approached the now-celibate Emperor, even though he was the brother of her father **Germanicus**. Using the grace of her beauty against her rivals, **Lollia Paulina** (who was aided by the freedman **Callistus**) and **Aelia Paetina** (who was favored by **Narcissus**), **Agrippina** utilized the help of **Pallas** to draw **Claudius** into a great desire for marriage with her. 

However, decency seemed to stand entirely in the way, because she was his niece and the daughter of his brother. Yet, through a speech by a suborned <note>To be "suborned" means to be bribed or secretly persuaded to perform an unethical or illegal act.</note> **Vitellius**, it was arranged that the Senate should be pressured into supporting his desire. With **Vitellius** pleading the case, a decree was made by the Senate stating that it should be permitted for paternal uncles to marry their nieces. And thus **Agrippina**, with **Claudius** willing and the Senate praying for it, entered into marriage with him. 

She was finally declared **Augusta** <note>The highest title for a woman in the Roman Empire, equivalent to "Empress."</note> and was carried in a carriage <note>original: "carpento"; a two-wheeled covered carriage, the use of which in the city was a high honor previously granted only to priests and sacred objects.</note> to the Capitol, an honor previously granted only to priests. She then began to rage against those opposed to her with various punishments. Finally, since she was a most cunning woman at the time...

<summary>This page details the early life of Agrippina the Younger, including the birth of her son Nero, her exile by her brother Caligula, and her strategic marriage to her uncle, Emperor Claudius.</summary>
<keywords>Agrippina the Younger, Nero, Claudius, Caligula, Vitellius, Roman Empire, Augusta, imperial marriage, exile</keywords>

--- Page 139 ---
<meta>This page continues the biography of Agrippina the Younger, following her successful effort to marry her uncle, Emperor Claudius, and her subsequent plot to secure the throne for her son, Nero.</meta>

...entirely in the way of such a union. For according to the long-standing customs of the Romans, the marriage of an uncle and his brother’s daughter was considered incestuous and forbidden. However, this obstacle was cleared away through the political maneuvers of **Vitellius** <note>Lucius Vitellius the Elder, a prominent Roman senator and supporter of Agrippina.</note>, who persuaded the Senate to pass a decree that made such marriages legal.

# The Rule of Agrippina

Thus, she was joined to the Emperor, but the union was **weakened by a shameful stain** <note>original: "turpi macula labefactus est"; though legal, the marriage was still viewed by many as a moral violation.</note>. For while other women were known for many lovers and the pursuit of pleasure, **Agrippina** directed her focus toward the accumulation of power. She ruled the Emperor and the state with a cold, masculine authority, immediately turning her efforts toward the advancement of her son, **Nero**.

She succeeded in persuading the aging and easily led **Claudius** to adopt **Nero**, thereby positioning him as the primary heir to the Empire, ahead of the Emperor’s biological son, **Britannicus**. As time passed and **Claudius** began to show signs of regret regarding his marriage and his choice of successor, **Agrippina** realized that her window of opportunity was closing. 

<note>A detailed woodcut illustration depicts a royal banquet. Agrippina stands over a seated Emperor Claudius, gesturing toward a bowl of mushrooms, while a physician stands ready in the shadows with a feather.</note>

Driven by fear and ambition, she sought the help of a notorious poisoner named **Locusta**. A lethal substance was prepared and mixed into a dish of mushrooms <note>mushrooms were famously the favorite food of the Emperor.</note>.

<margin>The murder of the Emperor</margin>

When the poison seemed to act too slowly, the Empress's physician, **Xenophon**, who was complicit in the crime, allegedly finished the deed. Under the guise of helping the Emperor vomit to relieve his distress, he used a feather dipped in a swift-acting poison to tickle his throat. In this way, **Claudius** was murdered, and **Agrippina** finally saw her son **Nero** proclaimed Emperor, though her triumph would ultimately lead to her own destruction.

<summary>This page details how Agrippina bypassed Roman incest laws to marry Emperor Claudius and describes the conspiracy to poison him with mushrooms to ensure Nero's rise to power.</summary>
<keywords>Agrippina the Younger, Emperor Claudius, Nero, Locusta, poisoning, Roman Empire, Vitellius, succession</keywords>

--- Page 140 ---
<meta>This page continues the account of Agrippina the Younger’s downfall. After the death of Claudius, her relationship with her son Nero soured as he sought to escape her overbearing influence, leading him to plot her assassination.</meta>

...in which **Agrippina**, having been welcomed and being ignorant of the snare, might perish. When this plan pleased **Nero**, he received her as she arrived from **Antium** <note>A coastal resort city where Nero was born and where he lured his mother for the festival of Minerva.</note>, feigning a repentant filial affection as if he regretted their past hatreds. He took her in his arms and escorted her all the way to her residence. From there, a ship had been prepared for her destruction; unaware, she boarded it to go to dinner, accompanied by her freed servants **Crepereius Gallus** and **Acerronia**. As they sailed through the night, a signal was given by the conspirators, and the roof of the ship, weighted down with a great amount of lead, collapsed and crushed **Crepereius**. Then, as the ship's crew attempted to capsize the vessel in the calm sea, the ship turned on its side. **Acerronia**, calling out for help <note>Historical accounts suggest Acerronia cried out that she was Agrippina, hoping to be saved, but was instead struck down by the assassins who thought she was the Empress.</note>, was killed by poles and oars. **Agrippina**, however, was wounded in the shoulder and eventually cast into the sea. She was rescued by helping swimmers and brought to the **Lucrine Lake** <note>A saltwater lake in the Campania region, famous in Roman times for its oyster beds and luxury villas.</note> and then to her own villa. 

From there, she sent her freedman **Agermus** to report to **Nero** that she had escaped. Nero, however, ordered the messenger to be detained, pretending that the man had come to assassinate him. He then sent **Anicetus** <note>OCR: "auicenus"; Anicetus was the commander of the fleet at Misenum and a former tutor to Nero.</note>, **Herculeius** the trierarch <note>OCR: "herculius tetrarchus"; a trierarch was a commander of a galley.</note>, and **Obaritus**, a centurion of the fleet, to finish her off. When the house was surrounded by **Anicetus**, and the lone maidservant who had remained with **Agrippina** fled, the executioners entered. **Herculeius** was the first to strike her head with a club. Then, seeing the centurion drawing his sword to deal the death blow, she bared her womb and cried out that they should **strike her belly** <note>original: "ventrem ferirent"; a dramatic and symbolic gesture, commanding them to destroy the womb that had birthed such a monster as Nero.</note>. Thus she was killed and cremated that same night. She was covered with cheap earth, and at last, a light mound was placed for her on the road near **Misenum** <note>A major Roman naval base near Naples.</note> and the villa of **Julius Caesar**. Others claim that **Nero** viewed her corpse after the murder, and that he criticized some of her limbs while praising others, before she was finally buried.

# On Epicharis, the Freedwoman

**Epicharis** is believed to have been a foreign woman rather than a Roman. She was not distinguished by any brilliance of lineage, being the daughter of a freedman and a freedwoman herself. What is far more shameful is that she was not practiced in any of the noble arts. However, toward the end of her life, she revealed that she possessed a noble spirit and a masculine strength. Indeed, as the insolence and debauchery of **Nero**, the leader of the Romans, grew among all the Romans and Italians, it reached such a point that several senators and other citizens conspired against him, with **Lucinius Piso** as their leader. While they attempted to bring the matter to a conclusion through various discussions, I know not by what means the entire plot and the names of the conspirators came to the knowledge of **Epicharis**. But when she grew weary of the work being dragged out too long by their judgment, she withdrew into **Campania**...

<summary>This page details the dramatic assassination of Agrippina the Younger on Nero's orders and introduces the conspiracy of Piso, highlighting the unexpected involvement of the freedwoman Epicharis.</summary>
<keywords>Nero, Agrippina, Anicetus, Misenum, assassination, matricide, Epicharis, Pisonian conspiracy, Roman history</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the narrative following the murder of Agrippina, focusing on the growing Pisonian conspiracy against Nero and the role of the freedwoman Epicharis.</meta>

While she happened to be staying near **Puteoli** <note>Modern-day Pozzuoli, a major Roman port near Naples, where many naval officers were stationed.</note> (not wishing to let the time pass idly), she met **Volusius Proculus**, a captain <note>original: "chiliarchum"; a term borrowed from the Greek meaning a commander of a thousand men, here referring to a high-ranking naval officer.</note> of the fleet and formerly the assassin of **Agrippina**. Believing that it would add much weight to the conspiracy if she could draw him to their side, she recounted a long list of **Nero’s** shameful acts, his foolish and disgusting habits, and his various insolences. She then spoke of Nero’s ingratitude toward Proculus himself, noting that despite his involvement in such a great crime—namely, the murder of Agrippina—the Emperor had not promoted him in any way that matched his service. Having said this, she revealed the conspiracy.

With all her strength, she attempted to gain him as an ally for the conspirators. However, the outcome for **Epicharis** was far different than she had imagined. For Volusius, seeking to see if he could turn the Emperor's favor toward himself through a show of loyalty, was quickly granted an audience with **Caesar** <note>A title used here to refer to the Emperor Nero.</note>, where he reported everything Epicharis had said. Yet, this did not achieve what he expected. For this shrewd woman had not revealed the name of a single conspirator to the man, who was still an unknown quantity to her. Consequently, when she was summoned, nothing could be proven from the questioning because...

...it is a sin to believe that they were merely slumbering. Let us therefore assume that they were all perfect; yet we must judge that we ourselves were never so, as the work itself indicates. Truly, men ought to blush with guilt when they are surpassed not only by wanton women <note>The author refers here to Epicharis, who was a freedwoman of low social standing and questionable reputation, yet showed more courage than the high-born male conspirators.</note> but even by the most constant endurance of any labors. For if we prevail in our sex <note>original: "sexu"; referring to the biological and social superiority men were assumed to hold in the 14th century.</note>, why do we not also prevail in strength, as is fitting? It is as if, being effeminate ourselves, we are rightly seen to have transgressed against proper morals.

## Concerning **Pompeia Paulina**, the wife of **Lucius Annaeus Seneca**

<margin>**Pompeia Paulina**</margin> **Pompeia Paulina** was the illustrious wife of **Lucius Annaeus Seneca**, who was the tutor of **Nero**. Whether she was Roman or a foreigner, I do not recall reading. However, while I observe the nobility of her spirit, I would rather believe she was Roman than a foreigner <note>original: "forensem"; meaning someone from outside the city of Rome, often implying lower status in classical texts.</note>. Even if we lack a certain record of her origin, we certainly do not lack the most devoted example of her love for her husband <note>The Roman concept of "pietas" or "pious love" implies a sense of duty and religious-like devotion.</note>, as shown by the testimony of illustrious men. They believed that...

<keywords>Pompeia Paulina, Seneca, Nero, feminine virtue, Roman history, Stoicism, gender roles, devotion</keywords>

--- Page 143 ---
<meta>This page continues the praise of Pompeia Paulina’s devotion to her husband Seneca. The author uses her desire to die alongside him as a contrast to the behavior of contemporary women who remarry frequently.</meta>

...honestly (if she could have) to die with her aged husband <note>referring to the philosopher Seneca, who was ordered by Nero to commit suicide</note> rather than to preserve her life—as most women do—by entering into second marriages, not without shame. Indeed, it is a supreme disgrace to matronly modesty <note>original: "matronalis pudicicie"; the Roman ideal of the 'univira,' a woman who married only once</note> that in these days for some—I will not say a second or third marriage (which is common to almost all)—but a sixth, seventh, and eighth marriage (if the occasion arises) is so wondrously familiar. They carry the wedding torches into the chambers of new husbands so frequently that they seem to have stolen the habits of prostitutes, whose custom it is to change their bed-partners very often, night after night. Nor do they approach the frequently repeated laws of marriage with any different look than if they were performing a most holy and honorable duty. 

Indeed, it is not sufficiently certain whether such women should be said to emerge from a brothel cell <note>original: "lupanaris cellula"</note> or from the bedroom of a deceased husband. Nor would I hesitate to suspect that either the woman entering is burning with dishonor, or the husband introducing her is quite foolish. Alas! Our morals have deservedly collapsed from the ancient customs; among the ancients, it was previously thought ignominious to the holiness of their minds to join not only a seventh but even a second marriage. Nor could such women henceforth be rightfully mixed with honorable matrons. 

Today, things are far different. For, keeping silent about their lustful itching <note>original: "libidinosam pruriginem"; a common medieval medical and moral description for uncontrollable desire</note>, they think themselves more beautiful and dearer because, through frequent betrothals, they have overcome the misfortune of widowhood and have pleased so many different husbands.

Line 16: "Woman." Tacitus <note>the Roman historian whose *Annals* serve as the source for this account</note> has "female." The first letter is "s" or "f." It looks like an "s."
Line 16: "Skillful." Tacitus has "artful."
Line 19: "Lavish." Tacitus has "profuse."

Let's do a final pass.

Since she never spared her reputation <note>original: "fame pcēt" (famae parceret); Poppaea Sabina was notorious for prioritizing her ambitions over her social standing</note>, she directed her lust toward whatever appeared to be a more ready advantage <note>original: "vilitas"; literally "cheapness" or "baseness," implying she was attracted to whatever served her immediate desires without regard for value or honor</note>. She made no distinction between husbands and adulterers. A woman marked by these traits found her fortune to be sufficiently compliant. ...but rather that marriage should follow immediately; nor would she be unworthy <note>original Latin: "degenerẽ"; literally "degenerate," though here Poppaea argues her noble background makes her a fit match for the Emperor</note>, deserving the Emperor’s marriage by her ancestral eloquence <note>original: "facunditate veteri"; Poppaea claimed her lineage and charm made her a rightful peer to the throne</note> and the beauty of her body. And when she had drawn the burning Prince <note>original: "p̃ncipẽ"; referring to the Emperor Nero</note> into a desire for marriage, Octavia—his wife and formerly the daughter of Claudius Caesar—was first banished, though innocent, to the island of Pandateria. And finally, in the twentieth year of her life, she was killed at the instigation of Poppaea and by Nero’s command; and thus Poppaea was joined in marriage to Caesar. 

But she did not long enjoy the high status she had sought and attained through such extensive arts <note>original: "longis artibz"; referring to her calculated schemes and manipulation of Nero</note>. For while she was pregnant once more, by a turn of fortune she died, having been struck by a kick from Nero in a fit of anger. He forbade her body to be consumed by fire according to the Roman custom, but instead ordered it to be carried publicly in a magnificent funeral procession following the rite of foreign kings <note>original: "exterarū regū ritu"; this refers to the practice of embalming common in the East, rather than the standard Roman practice of cremation</note>. And he ordered her body, filled with spices, to be laid in the tomb of the Julii <note>the ancestral mausoleum of the Julio-Claudian dynasty</note>. He himself, standing before the Rostra <note>the public speaking platform in the Roman Forum</note>, praised her—and most especially her beauty—in a long and carefully prepared speech, attributing to her various gifts of fortune or nature as if they were the most illustrious virtues. For me, among these fortunes of Poppaea...
