--- Page 146 ---
<meta>This page continues from the account of the Roman women of the first century, concluding the story of Triaria, the wife of Lucius Vitellius, known for her fierce and perhaps "unfeminine" military conduct, before transitioning to the life of the Christian poet Proba.</meta>

Omitting no extreme of military severity, she rushed upon the wretched. Indeed, it is reported that she acted with excessive cruelty and pride toward her enemies. The forces of marital love in an insane heart are vast; for them, there is no fear so long as the husband’s glory is exalted; no memory of piety; no shame for the female sex; no regard for the circumstances of the times. Triaria was able to endure all things for her husband’s honor with easy labor—she who could strike horror not only into women (who, as is their custom, are often terrified in their husband’s embrace by even the daily murmur of a wall <note>original Latin: "diurno muris murmure"; a poetic way of saying women were thought to be frightened by the slightest sounds or rumors of danger</note>) but even into robust and warlike young men. And if this woman threw herself into these nocturnal arms with such force, who would believe she was conspicuous for this deed alone? For the hearts of mortals are not accustomed to—or rather, it is rare for them to—be filled with many virtues at once, whether they be hidden or famous. I, for my part, believe that even if they have been removed from memory, Triaria was remarkable for many other merits.

# Concerning Proba, the wife of Adelphius

**Proba** was a woman most worthy of remembrance for her reputation in literature <note>original Latin: "lrarū [literarum] noticia"; referring to her scholarly or literary fame</note> and for her name. Although her nobility and origin are unknown to some, I believe by conjecture that she was Roman. Other most illustrious men assert she was born in that city <note>original: "ex oppido ortā"; likely referring to Rome as "the city" or a specific notable town</note>, was the wife of a certain Adelphius, and was of the Christian religion. It can be clearly perceived that she excelled in the liberal arts, no matter under which teacher she was trained. 

Truly, among her other studies, she became so learned and familiar with the poems of Virgil through such watchful care that she seemed to have held the entire work in her sight and memory as if she had composed it herself. Perhaps while reading with a more acute attention, the thought occurred to her that from those verses, the entire history of the Old Testament and the sequence of the New Testament could be described with a calm, ready, and robust <note>original Latin: "succi pleno"; literally "full of juice," a rhetorical term for a style that is vigorous and rich</note> usage. It does not lack cause for wonder that such a sublime idea entered a woman’s brain; but it was far more wonderful to have carried it out to completion. 

Therefore, applying her labor to this pious conception, running now here and now there through the *Bucolics*, the *Georgics*, and the *Aeneid*—plucking now whole verses, now fragments of meters <note>This refers to the "Cento" technique, a literary work made up of quotations from other authors</note>—with marvelous skill she adapted them to her purpose. She placed whole lines so aptly and connected fragments so well, preserving the laws of meter and the dignity of the poem, that no one could ever find fault <note>original Latin: "ūerpulsim⁹ apages"; likely a corruption of "repulsimus" or a similar term for rejection or criticism</note>. And starting from the beginning of the world...

<summary>This page concludes the biography of the fierce Roman woman Triaria and introduces Faltonia Betitia Proba, a Christian poet who famously rearranged the works of Virgil to tell biblical stories.</summary>
<keywords>Triaria, Lucius Vitellius, Proba, Adelphius, Virgil, Cento, Old Testament, New Testament, Christian poetry, liberal arts</keywords>

--- Page 147 ---
<meta>This page continues the biography of the Christian poet Proba (Falconia Proba), focusing on her literary achievement in creating a Virgilian Cento—a poem that retells the story of the Bible using only the words and verses of the Roman poet Virgil.</meta>

Making a beginning from what she read of history in the Old and New Testaments, she composed it so skillfully up to the descent of the Holy Spirit that a person ignorant of this composition might easily believe Virgil had been both a prophet and an evangelist. From this, we may take it as no less commendable that this woman possessed a complete or very full knowledge of the sacred volumes—a thing which we know, with regret, happens to very few men in our own age. Moreover, this excellent woman wished for the work composed by her labor to be called a <term>Cento</term> <note>A "Cento" (from the Latin for "patchwork") is a poetic work composed entirely of verses or passages taken from other authors, rearranged to tell a new story.</note>, which we ourselves have often seen.

And the more we consider that work worthy of weighty memory, the less we believe that the genius of so famous a woman rested on this labor alone. On the contrary, I suspect that if she lived to an advanced age, she composed other praiseworthy things besides, which through the neglect of librarians have been unable to reach us in our time. Among these (though it is no longer extant) was a Homeric Cento, produced with the same skill and from the same material <note>original Latin: "ex eadez materia"; meaning the same biblical subject matter</note> as she had taken from Virgil, but using verses taken from Homer. 

From this—if it is indeed so—it is concluded with even greater praise that she knew Greek literature as most learnedly as Latin. I ask now: what could be more desirable than to hear of a woman scanning the verses of Maro <note>original: "Maronis"; referring to Publius Vergilius Maro, commonly known as Virgil</note> and Homer, setting aside things suitable for her work, and weaving together her selections in an ingenious context? Let the most learned men look on, for whom the distinguished profession of sacred letters is yet arduous and difficult: to draw out parts now from here, now from there, from the vast bosom of the sacred volume, and to force them into a single sequence of the life of Christ with scattered words and patches, just as she did from a pagan poem <note>original: "ex gentili carmine"; referring to Virgil's poetry, which was considered "gentile" or pagan by Christian standards</note>.

It would have been enough for her (if we consider womanly customs) to have the distaff, the needle, and the loom, if she had wished to be idle like most others. But because she was diligent in sacred studies and wiped away every rust of laziness from her mind, she escaped into eternal light. Would that those who surrender themselves to pleasures and leisure would take this to heart with a good spirit! For them, it is a great thing to sit in their bedrooms, wasting irrecoverable time on frivolous tales, and from early morning until the dead of night, often drawing out conversations that are either harmful or empty with their babbling, or devoting themselves only to wantonness. By heavens, let them notice how much difference there is between seeking fame through praiseworthy works and burying one's name together with one's corpse, departing from life as if they had never lived!

<summary>Boccaccio praises the 4th-century poet Proba for her "Cento," a poem weaving Virgil’s verses into the story of the Bible, and contrasts her intellectual diligence with the idleness of other women.</summary>
<keywords>Falconia Proba, Cento, Virgil, biblical history, female education, domesticity vs. scholarship, literary fame</keywords>

"De faustina Augusta" (About Empress Faustina).
A biography of Faustina the Younger (wife of Marcus Aurelius), her lineage, her beauty, her depictions on coins, and the scandals/infidelities attributed to her (including the gladiator story).

    *   *De faustina Augusta* -> About the Empress Faustina.
    *   *F Faustina augusta ( q̄ ꝛ int' diuos postea r̄lata ē )* -> Faustina the Empress (who was also afterwards counted among the gods/deified).
    *   *glorie plimū viues morieſqꝫ viri sui magis benignitatem q̄ꝫ ope suo ꝭsecuta ē.* -> In her life and at her death, she achieved much glory, following more the kindness of her husband than her own merit.
    *   *fuit Antonini pij cesaris Augusti ex faustina ꝭiugi filia.* -> She was the daughter of the Emperor Antoninus Pius and his wife Faustina [the Elder].
    *   *ꝛ marco Antonino pio adoptato in filiū ꝭnubio iūcta.* -> And she was joined in marriage to Marcus Antoninus Pius [Marcus Aurelius], who had been adopted as a son.
    *   *eogꝫ pr̄e mortuo impauit vnacū viro.* -> And after her father died, she ruled together with her husband.
    *   *ꝛ ꝭsultu senatꝰ Augusta appellata ē.* -> And by a decree of the Senate, she was called "Augusta."
    *   *nō pua eo tꝑe glia m'lieri* -> No small glory for a woman at that time.
    *   *Nā etsi pr̄eden̄bꝫ ab augustis viris augustay ꝭognomē eēt* -> For although wives of the Emperors had received the title "Augusta" before her.
    *   *nulli an̄ hāc senatus ꝭsulto fuiſse ꝭceſsū adiuēnio.* -> I find that before her, none had it granted by a formal decree of the Senate.
    *   *Fuit pr̄erea tam exq̄siti decoris vt aliqd diuinū mortalitati eiꝰ crederet admixtū.* -> Furthermore, she was of such exquisite beauty that something divine was believed to be mixed with her mortality.
    *   *qd ne ꝭsumeret' senio aut morte. actū ē vt iuuen cula ꝛ etate ꝑfectioꝛ aureis argenteiſqꝫ ac ereis nūmis eiꝰ effigies sculpereť.* -> Lest this beauty be consumed by old age or death, it was arranged that her image be sculpted on gold, silver, and bronze coins while she was a young girl and in her more mature years.
    *   *ꝛ in hodiernū vſqꝫ ꝑdurat.* -> And they endure even to this day.

# About the Empress Faustina

**F**austina the Empress <note>original: "Faustina augusta"; referring to Faustina the Younger (c. 130–175 AD)</note> (who was also afterwards counted among the gods <note>original: "inter divos postea relata est"; referring to her deification, or apotheosis, by the Senate after her death</note>) achieved great glory in both her life and her death, though this was due more to the kindness of her husband than to her own merit. She was the daughter of the Emperor Antoninus Pius and his wife Faustina <note>Faustina the Elder</note>, and she was joined in marriage to Marcus Antoninus Pius <note>Marcus Aurelius</note>, who had been adopted as a son. Upon her father’s death, she ruled alongside her husband, and by a decree of the Senate <note>original: "consultu senatus"</note>, she was called **Augusta**. This was no small glory for a woman of that time; for although the wives of previous Emperors had held the title of "Augusta," I find that before her, none had it granted by a formal decree of the Senate.

Furthermore, she was of such exquisite beauty that it was believed something divine was mixed into her mortal form. Lest this beauty be consumed by old age or death, it was arranged that her image be sculpted on gold, silver, and bronze coins while she was still a young girl and again in her more mature years. These endure even to this day. In these portraits, although the expression of the mouth, the movement of the eyes, the vivid color, and the cheerfulness of the face are missing, the features nonetheless bear witness to her great beauty.

Indeed, as much as her fame was celebrated throughout the whole world, just as much was it polluted by the mark of shameful unchastity. It was believed, in truth, that she was not content with just one lover besides her husband; rather, she came into the embraces of many. The names of some of these men are worn down by infamy: for a certain old man was counted among her adulterers, as was Orphicus, and after him, Moderatius. But the one who surpassed the others was named Tertullus, whom they even say was invited to dinner by Antoninus <note>Marcus Aurelius</note> himself. Added to these was Marcus [Lucius] Verus, despite the fact that he was her son-in-law through her daughter Lucilla.

And (what is more shameful than all these), they say she loved a certain gladiator <note>Gladiators, while celebrated performers, were legally "infamous" and occupied the lowest rung of Roman social status, making such an affair a peak scandal.</note> so much that she fell into a nearly fatal illness because of her desire for him. In her longing for health, she revealed her lust to Antoninus. He, following the advice of a physician to soothe the fever of the ailing woman, had the gladiator killed. While the blood was still warm, he had the whole body of the sick woman bathed in it. Thus, he freed his wife from the impetuous heat of love and also from the disease. Wise men believed this remedy was a fiction, for in the course of time, the troublesome Commodus—conceived during that time—provided testimony to the truth through his wicked deeds. By his character, he was believed to be the son of the gladiator rather than the son of Antoninus. While these things echoed to the shame of Faustina...

<summary>This page describes the life of Empress Faustina the Younger, highlighting her unprecedented honor of being named 'Augusta' by the Senate and her legendary beauty preserved on coins, while contrasting this public image with rumors of her many infidelities.</summary>
<keywords>Faustina the Younger, Marcus Aurelius, Roman Empire, Augusta, deification, coinage, adultery, Commodus, gladiator blood legend</keywords>

# About the Empress Faustina

Faustina the Empress (who was also afterwards counted among the gods <note>original: *inter divos relata est*; this refers to the Roman practice of deification, where the Senate officially declared a deceased ruler a deity</note>) achieved great glory during both her life and at her death, though she followed the kindness of her husband more than her own merit. She was the daughter of the Emperor Antoninus Pius and his wife Faustina <note>Known as Faustina the Elder to distinguish her from her daughter</note>. She was joined in marriage to Marcus Antoninus Pius <note>The philosopher-emperor Marcus Aurelius</note>, who had been adopted as a son by her father. 

When her father died, she ruled together with her husband, and by a decree of the Senate, she was called "Augusta." This was a glory of no small account for a woman at that time. For although the wives of previous emperors had held the title "Augusta," I find that before her, it had been granted to none by a formal decree of the Senate. She was, furthermore, of such exquisite beauty that she seemed to add something divine to her mortal nature...

...Macrinus fell, and his soldiers transferred their loyalty to Heliogabalus <note>original: *heliagaboli*; also known as Elagabalus, the teenage emperor who reigned from 218 to 222 AD</note>. A little while later, in a village of Bithynia <note>A Roman province in what is now northwest Turkey</note>, he was killed along with his son Diadumenus. From this, Heliogabalus—as if he had avenged the death of his father Caracalla <note>original: *Caracalle p̄ris*; Elagabalus’s grandmother claimed he was the illegitimate son of the former emperor Caracalla to win the army's support</note>—obtained the undisputed rule through the help of his grandmother Maesa.

Upon arriving in Rome, he was awaited and welcomed by the entire Senate with the greatest anticipation. Because of this sudden advancement, Soaemias <note>original: *Semia*; Julia Soaemias, who exercised significant power during her son's reign</note> was elevated almost to the stars. Having been named "Augusta" <note>The highest title for women in the Roman imperial family</note>, she shone with the authority she gained over the vaulted halls of the Roman imperial court. She was all the more radiant for this one reason: for, even granted that Heliogabalus was a wicked man, he recognized that he had become emperor through the actions of his grandmother, and he remained devoted to his mother...

...so that she was held in such high esteem by the common people that she was even preferred before all the Sibyls <note>Prophetic women of the ancient world whose oracles were highly revered in Rome; the text implies Soaemias was being treated with divine-like status</note>. There, in the manner of the Senate, they were to issue decrees concerning the morals and the actions regarding the status of matrons, and they were to establish laws. For this "distinguished" senate, he appointed Soaemias <note>original: *Semiamirā*</note> as the presiding leader. It was discovered that from this body flowed many senatorial decrees, however ridiculous they might be. Indeed, it was ordained in that assembly what kind of clothing each woman should wear, and with what ornaments she was to be equipped...

--- Page 154 ---
<meta>This page continues the biography of Queen Zenobia, following the description of her military bearing and her preference for appearing before her troops in a helmet. It transitions from her habits on campaign to her eventual defeat and her life in Roman exile.</meta>

She traveled frequently in a simple wagon, rarely in a carriage, and even more rarely in a lady’s litter <term>pilento; a luxurious, two-wheeled covered carriage used by Roman matrons of high status</term>. She would often march on foot for many thousands of paces. Seeking the glory of a triumph <note>A "Triumph" was the most prestigious civil ceremony and religious rite in ancient Rome, held to publicly celebrate and sanctify the success of a military commander.</note> to be brought before Rome, she did so despite the ignominy <note>original: *ignomīiaz*; the author reflects the Roman view that it was shameful for a woman to be the focus of a military triumph, which was traditionally a male honor</note> of a woman being seen in a triumph for the first time—much as the daughter of Pompey <note>original: *erpiadaz filia pōpeii*; likely a scribal variant or reference to a daughter of Pompey the Great being led in an earlier spectacle</note> was once led. 

She was successful for a long time because she was aided by the circumstances of the era; for while the Roman Empire was in a state of neglect and lack of care, she conquered. At that same time, Gallienus <note>Roman Emperor (reigned 253–268 AD) whose reign was troubled by the "Crisis of the Third Century," allowing regional powers like Palmyra to rise</note> held power—or rather, the Empire was being held together poorly. In that time of fate, as we have said before, she maintained her victory for a period, until she was finally conquered by the Divine Aurelian <note>Emperor Aurelian (reigned 270–275 AD), who successfully reunified the Roman Empire</note>. 

Having been defeated, she was led in a triumph through this city of Rome, which was a lasting mark of Zenobia's shame. Long afterward, a place was named after her, which is located not far from the palace of the Divine Hadrian <note>This refers to the Villa of Hadrian in Tivoli, where tradition says Zenobia was granted an estate to live out her remaining years as a Roman matron</note>, in that spot which was called "The Shells" <term>conche; likely "Concae," a local toponym possibly referring to the shell-like architectural niches of the nearby villa</term> by the local inhabitants.

### Concerning the English Pope, Joanna

Although she appeared to be a man by the name of John <note>original: *Iohannes*; the legendary figure known in English as Pope Joan</note>, she was nonetheless female in sex. Her ingrained boldness brought it about that she became known to the whole world and ensured she would be recognized by posterity. Although some say that her homeland was Mainz <note>original: *magūtinā*; a city in modern-day Germany, often cited as her birthplace in the legend</note>, it is known what name she took while living as a man. Indeed, there are those who say her name was Gilberta. This is established by the testimony of certain people that, as a maiden, she was loved by a young scholar <term>scholastico; a student or someone associated with a cathedral school or university</term>. It is said she loved him so much that, setting aside maidenly modesty and feminine fear, she fled secretly from her father’s house and followed the young man she loved in the dress of a man and under a changed name. While studying with him in England, she was thought by all to be a cleric, and she devoted herself both to love and to the study of letters <note>original: *veneri ⁊ lr̄az militauit studijs*; literally "she campaigned in the studies of Venus [love] and letters," suggesting she balanced a secret romantic life with her education</note>. Later, after the young man was taken away by death, because she knew herself to be strong in natural talent and was drawn by the sweetness of knowledge, she retained her male dress; she neither wished to attach herself to another man nor to reveal herself as a woman. On the contrary, persisting in her studies with vigilance, she became so [proficient] in the liberal arts and sac[red...]

<summary>This page introduces the legend of Pope Joan, describing how she disguised herself as a man named John (or Gilberta) to follow a young scholar to England and pursue an education in letters and the liberal arts.</summary>
<keywords>Pope Joan, Joanna the Englishwoman, Mainz, clerical disguise, medieval education, Gilberta, women in scholarship</keywords>

--- Page 156 ---
<meta>This page continues the legend of Pope Joan, describing the scandalous conclusion of her papacy and the beginning of the biography of the Byzantine Empress Irene.</meta>

...the patience of God. What finally could blind the eyes of men for so long, hiding such an unholy birth <note>original: *incestuosum partum*; implying a birth that violated sacred laws or religious vows</note> from plain sight? For when she was near her term, while celebrating the sacred city-procession <term>amburbale; an ancient Roman term for a purification circuit, here applied to a papal procession</term> from the Janiculum hill toward the Lateran <note>the Basilica of Saint John Lateran, the official ecclesiastical seat of the Pope</note>, she was seized by labor between the Colosseum and the church of Pope Clement. There, without a midwife being called, she gave birth in public. She had successfully maintained this deception for a long time through the help of her lover and others. From there, cast out into outer darkness <note>original: *in tenebras exteriores*; a biblical reference to Matthew 22:13, signifying total exclusion</note> by the leading men of the city, the wretched woman perished along with her child. To mark her name in memory and to show detestation for her impurity, even to this day the Supreme Pontiffs, when conducting the sacred Rogation rites <term>rogacōnū; formal processions involving prayers and litanies for the community</term> with the clergy and the people, find that location on the route so abominable that they bypass it. They turn aside through different streets and, once that detestable place is behind them, they return to their path to finish the journey they began.

->## Concerning Irene, Empress of the Romans<-

Irene of Athens was a most noble woman, conspicuous for her remarkable beauty. When the Emperor Constantine <note>Constantine V, who sought a bride for his son, the future Leo IV</note> had summoned her from her homeland to Constantinople...

<summary>This page concludes the legend of Pope Joan, describing her public labor during a papal procession and the origin of the tradition where popes avoid that specific street. It then introduces Irene of Athens, the famous Byzantine Empress.</summary>
<keywords>Pope Joan, papal procession, Lateran, Colosseum, San Clemente, Rogations, Empress Irene, Constantinople, Byzantine Empire</keywords>

--- Page 157 ---
<meta>This page continues the biography of the Byzantine Empress Irene, detailing the brutal power struggle between her and her son, Constantine VI, her eventual downfall, and the beginning of the story of Enguldruda of Ravenna.</meta>

...to Nikephoros and Christopher, his brothers, he tore out their tongues. This same man <note>referring to Constantine VI, Irene's son</note> deprived Alexios, the patrician of Armenia, of his sight. He also forced his wife Maria to take up the monastic habit and brought in Theodote, a lady-in-waiting, whom he immediately crowned. Because of these atrocities, Irene—though she had been hidden away and forced by fortune to set aside her rule—nevertheless preserved her extraordinary spirit. In the hope of reclaiming the sovereign power, she began to distribute gold lavishly to the nobles. Using the abundant treasures she had hidden in the palace where she lived in seclusion while he ruled, she won over the minds of the leading men to her cause.

When she had drawn them to her side with great gifts, she arranged for those who had once deposed her to capture her son Constantine and deprive him of his sight <note>original: *lumibusque privaret*; blinding was a common Byzantine practice to disqualify a rival from ruling</note>. Thus, this courageous woman reclaimed the empire that had been taken from her. As for Constantine, he perished, consumed by illness. At length, when she had ruled again for five years, she was besieged in the Palace of Eleutherios by the rebel Nikephoros. After he had received the imperial diadem from Tarasios, the Patriarch of Constantinople—with the support of the patricians Leo and Triphyllios, as well as the courtier Staurakios, whom Irene herself had recently enriched—it was arranged that he should enter Irene's presence with humility and use flattering words. She, however, noticed nothing of the plot, and asked for nothing from the empire except the palace in which she resided. Once the promise of her request was granted, she opened all her treasures to him.

Having obtained these, the wicked man broke his word and banished her to exile on the island of Lesbos. There, now an old woman, this illustrious lady ended her life. Others, however, seem to hold a different view of her end. They say that because the mother and son were in discord and were stripping each other of the empire in turn, the Romans defected from them and transferred the empire to Charlemagne, then King of the Franks. It is said Charlemagne attempted to reunite the divided empire <note>the Western and Eastern Roman Empires</note> through a permanent marriage alliance, and that Irene had agreed to it. When the patrician Aetios <note>transcribed as *euticius*</note> heard this, he immediately elevated Nikephoros and, by besieging Irene, forced her to surrender the empire and enter a monastery, where she finally grew old.

->## Concerning the virgin Enguldruda<-

Enguldruda drew her origin from a once most noble family of Ravenna, our city. ...had been granted and confirmed, she was immediately banished into exile on the island of Lesbos. There, after she had for some time earned her bread by the use of the distaff and spindle <note>original: *colo et fuso*; having been stripped of the imperial treasury, the former Empress was reduced to the humble labor of spinning wool to survive</note>, she passed from this world in the year of Christ 802. 

Some bring this against her as a reproach—that she tore out her own son's eyes so that she might reign. Others, however, hold her in praise, contending that she did it not out of a thirst for power, but from zeal for the Christian faith, seeing that her son was a heretic and inclined to split the Church. But however that may have been, the Catholic faith was strengthened during this time, and the images <note>original: *ymagines*; this refers to the religious icons that were the center of the Iconoclastic Controversy</note> were restored to the temples to be honored once more, which Constantine Cabalinus <note>original: *Cabalinus*; a derogatory nickname for Constantine V meaning 'Dung-named,' a major figure in the movement to destroy icons</note> and his son Leo had removed. **Charlemagne**, the King of the Franks and then Emperor, had even sent ambassadors wishing to marry this Irene, so that the Eastern and Western Empires might be joined; but because Irene was driven into exile, this plan was never brought to fruition.

# CIII. ENGULDRUDA

->Enguldruda of Ravenna, a noble woman<-

**Enguldruda of Ravenna**, a noble woman distinguished both for her high birth and the beauty of her person, was the daughter of the illustrious Cassianus of Ravenna. Because her beauty was so extraordinary that its fame had reached the ears of the Emperor **Otto I** <note>Otto the Great, reigned 962–973</note>, it happened that when the Emperor came to Ravenna, he was received into the home of her father with great honor. While the Emperor was at the table, Cassianus, perhaps more eager for imperial favor than protective of his daughter’s modesty, commanded Enguldruda to come before the prince. When the girl appeared, the Emperor was struck by her grace and the nobility of her bearing. However, her father—seeking to please the Emperor—began to speak of her in a way that suggested she was for sale to the ruler’s desires.

...and I made you a handsome youth again. I recall this to your mind, which you ought to remember and cannot deny. You, however, in return for such memorable benefits, have repaid me with such "thanks" that you dared to deny a fair marriage—one fortified by honest and holy testimonies and secured by such seals—and to despise your redeemer, treating her as worthless <note>original: *flocifacere*; literally "to make of a lock of wool," a Latin idiom meaning to value something at nothing</note> and attempting to stain her with base suspicion. 

You were ashamed, you man of an insane mind, to have a widow born of a knightly man as your wife. Oh, how much more fitting it would have been to be ashamed of having voided the faith you pledged, of having vilified the holy and terrible name of God, and of having shown yourself so overflowing with these vices through your execrable ingratitude! I confess that I am not a woman of royal blood. But since from my cradle <note>original: *incunabulis*; the source of the English word "incunabula," referring to the earliest stages of something</note> I have been conversant among royal virgins, daughters-in-law, and wives, it is no wonder that my manners and spirit have surpassed those of royalty; this is enough for nobility, and enough to take on a royal status. 

But why say more? I will be easy with you in this matter, in which you were difficult with me with all your might: you denied being mine when you were. But although I have won proof that you are mine, I freely grant that you need not be mine. Let your royal brightness be yours, though it is defiled by the mark of infidelity; let your youthful strength and your fleeting beauty be yours. For my part, I will remain content in my widowhood. And the fortunes which God has provided me, I shall leave to heirs of a more honorable race than yours. 

Go therefore, ill-fated youth; learn at your own expense with what arts and what deceits you may play with other women. It is enough for me that I have been deceived by you once; for this reason, it is never my intention to be joined with you. Indeed, I judge that I should maintain a celibate life, which I consider far preferable to your embraces.

Having said these things, she withdrew herself from his sight, nor could she thereafter be moved from her laudable purpose by any prayers or warnings. Roland, however, confused and late in repenting for his worthlessness <note>original: *ignaviae*; can mean cowardice, laziness, or general lack of spirit</note>, was despised by everyone. With a dejected face, fleeing the sight not only of his brothers but even of common people, he departed into a miserable fortune, not daring to seek her again through fraud. The King and the other nobles wondered at the noble spirit of the woman. They extolled her with wonderful praises, uncertain which was more commendable: that Camiola, contrary to the stereotype of "feminine greed" <note>original: *tenacitate femineam*; literally "feminine tenacity" or "clinging," often used in a derogatory way toward women's supposed attachment to money</note>, had redeemed the youth with so great a sum of money, or that she had so spiritedly despised and rejected the man she redeemed once he was convicted of being unworthy.

# Concerning Joanna, Queen of Jerusalem and Sicily

Joanna, Queen of Jerusalem and Sicily, is a woman distinguished beyond all other women of our age for her lineage, power, and character. It seemed to me that it would be holier to remain silent about her than to write only a little, lest I seem to omit her merits out of spite <note>original: *odium*; Boccaccio suggests that a brief account might seem like an intentional slight against her vast reputation</note>. 

She was the first daughter of the most serene prince Charles, the illustrious Duke of Calabria and the first-born son of Robert, King of Jerusalem and Sicily <note>original: *celebris memorie Roberti*; Robert "the Wise," a celebrated patron of the arts and Boccaccio’s own mentor</note>, who is of famous memory, and of Mary, the sister of Philip, King of the Franks <note>Philip VI of France</note>. 

If we wished to trace her lineage through grandfathers and great-grandfathers to the very end, we would not stop until, after passing through countless ascending kings, we reached Dardanus <note>The legendary founder of Troy; medieval royalty often claimed descent from Trojan refugees to establish ancient legitimacy</note>, the first founder of Troy. Some might even attempt to say his father was Jupiter <note>The king of the gods in Roman mythology</note>. From such an ancient and noble stock <note>original: *prosapia*; a term for a long-established family line</note>, so many remarkably famous princes have flowed that no Christian...

Every band of wicked men, struck with terror, either fled or shut themselves away in high mountain fortresses <note>original: *celsis arcibus*; Boccaccio is praising Joanna for restoring law and order to a kingdom previously plagued by brigands and lawless mercenaries</note>.

Sending out an army of soldiers under an excellent commander, she would not lift the siege of such strongholds until the fortifications were captured and those unspeakable men were brought to justice through execution—a feat that some of the previous kings either lacked the will or the power to accomplish. She brought the lands she possessed into such order that not only the poor, but even the wealthy, might travel safely wherever they wish, singing by day or night <note>This is a classical literary trope used to describe a "Golden Age" of security where travelers have no fear of robbery</note>.

And, what is no less beneficial, she restrained the prominent men and the nobles of the realm with such great moderation, and corrected their loose morals for the better, so that they set aside that ancient pride with which they once held kings in low esteem <note>original: *parvipendebant*; meaning they previously belittled or ignored royal authority</note>...

--- Page 167 ---
<meta>This page serves as the final conclusion to Giovanni Boccaccio’s "De Mulieribus Claris" (Of Famous Women). It follows the lengthy biography of Queen Joanna I of Naples, whom Boccaccio presented as the crowning example of womanly achievement in his own time.</meta>

# Conclusion

We have finally reached the women of our own time (as is clear enough), among whom the number of shining examples <note>original: *rutilantium numerus*; Boccaccio uses the imagery of glowing or reddish-gold light to describe virtuous women</note> is so rare that I believe it more honorable to bring what I have begun to an end, rather than to proceed further through these modern days which lead toward more corrupted things <note>original: *adulteriora*; Boccaccio suggests that contemporary history lacks the purity and clear moral examples found in antiquity</note>. This is especially true since the most illustrious queen <note>Joanna I of Naples, the subject of the final biography</note> has now concluded that which Eve, the first mother of all, began.

I know, however, that there will be those who say that many women have been omitted. There will be others who raise different objections, which might perhaps be rightfully argued. But as for me, I shall respond with humility, as I promised: I freely admit to having omitted many. Indeed, I could not have touched upon every woman before all others, since time—the conqueror of fame—has consumed the memory of so many of them. Nor was it granted to me to see every woman among those still remaining, and a memory, even when sought out, does not always provide everything to one who desires it.

Indeed, so that they do not think me entirely forgetful, I want them to believe that it was not without my noticing that so many women—be they barbarians, Greeks, or Latins, as well as the wives of Emperors and Kings—have been passed over. I have indeed seen countless women and known of their deeds, but it was not my intention when taking up the pen to describe them all. Rather, as I testified from the beginning of this little work, my goal was to select and present certain ones from the great multitude. Since I believe this has been done appropriately enough, any further objection remains superfluous.

As for the rest, let it be said that I easily believe it possible that some things happened or were set down less than correctly. For often not only an ignorance of facts deceives a writer, but even an excessive affection of the laborer for his own work. If that has happened, I grieve for it. I ask that through the venerable honor of noble studies, more prudent readers bear with an even mind whatever has been less well done. And if there is any spirit of pious charity in them, let them correct and amend what is written less than it should be, adding or subtracting where necessary. Let them do this so that the work may flourish for someone's benefit, rather than perishing for no one's gain, torn apart by the teeth of the envious <note>original: *dentibus invidorum*; a common medieval metaphor for harsh, malicious critics</note.

**Here ends the compendium of Giovanni Boccaccio of Certaldo, which he successfully published concerning famous women and their perpetual fame.**

<summary>In this concluding passage, Boccaccio reflects on his selection process, admits to omitting many women due to the passage of time or personal choice, and asks his readers for charitable corrections rather than harsh criticism.</summary>
<keywords>Giovanni Boccaccio, conclusion, famous women, literary humility, Queen Joanna I, Eve, historical record, envy</keywords>

# Conclusion

Therefore, it is not about a vain or trivial matter (as some think) that is being contested between us and our adversaries in this cause, but rather concerning the highest and most important doctrine of our light, grace, and salvation. If any error is made here, the whole of the Christian religion is put in danger. Therefore, while there are many reasons that compel us to make this confession <note>original: *confessionem*; here referring to a formal statement of religious belief</note>, the greatest is this: that we recognize this doctrine to be true and confirmed by divine testimony. If we were to deny it, we would bind ourselves in the greatest crime and subject ourselves to eternal damnation. 

Therefore, since these things are so, we urge all pious people and lovers of truth to read this confession of ours diligently, compare it with the Holy Scriptures, and then judge whether we or our adversaries are wandering from the truth. For we do not doubt that all who consider this cause without prejudice will agree with us. 

(May God, who is the author of peace and concord, so enlighten our minds that we may at last come together in one and the same truth, for the glory of His name and the salvation of His church. Amen.) 

But if there should be anyone who wishes to slander this confession of ours, or interpret it in a "sinister" <note>original: *sinistram*; meaning unfavorable, perverse, or literally "on the left hand"</note> way, let him know that he is declaring war not upon us, but upon Truth itself and upon God, who is the author of truth—whose judgment no mortal will be able to escape.

### Here ends the review of the doctrine of the Lord’s Supper <term>Lord’s Supper: also known as the *Coena Domini*, the Eucharist, or Holy Communion; a central point of theological dispute during the Reformation</term>, collected from the lectures and writings of Doctor Philipp Melanchthon <term>Philipp Melanchthon: (1497–1560) A key German Reformer and collaborator of Martin Luther, famed for his intellectual defense of the Lutheran faith</term>.

--- Page 169 ---
<meta>Although the previous page translation provided discusses 16th-century theology and the Lord's Supper, the text on this page is actually the conclusion of Giovanni Boccaccio’s 14th-century work *De Mulieribus Claris* (Concerning Famous Women). This discrepancy suggests this page may come from a different section of a composite volume or that the paper was reused for a different manuscript.</meta>

**Conclusion**

I have brought this work down even to the women of our own time, among whom honorable virtue shines so rarely <note>original: *rarus rutil*; likely *rarus rutilat*, suggesting that virtue in his contemporary era was seen as less common than in antiquity</note> that I believe it is more honorable to stop here than to proceed further. This is especially true while Nature, the mother of all <note>original: *natura omnium parens*</note>, has only just begun her work in them, meaning that many women would necessarily be omitted from this account. 

And indeed, I might be justly rebuked for having left out so many; to this I would respond that I omitted many because I could not have touched upon them all without being entirely consumed by the effort. Nor was it safe for me to include everything, and things that are merely imagined or invented do not belong in a record of memory. 

Indeed, lest I be thought entirely ignorant, I should say that while many things happened without my noticing, I am well aware of the crimes of many empresses <term>augustorum diuges; the consorts of the Augusti or Emperors</term> and queens. I did not wish to describe these crimes, for I am far from wanting to recount such wickedness. From the great multitude of those I could have chosen, I thought it appropriately done and not superfluous to have said what was possible, even though I lacked the leisure to remain certain of every detail. For a man is often deceived by working too much upon his own work. 

I ask, therefore, by this venerable honor, that where I have performed less than perfectly, those who possess a spirit of charity might correct and amend the text, so that they may improve it rather than tearing it apart to no one's benefit.

<note>A large color and resolution test chart is visible at the bottom of the image, used by archivists to ensure the accuracy of the digital reproduction.</note>
